GenAI · RAG · Agentic AI · AWS · as of November 2026

Agentic AI Engineer Track

One place to learn generative AI, RAG and agentic AI from zero to interview-ready: how it started, how it works, what teams actually ship in late 2026, and how to build it yourself on AWS with LangChain, LangGraph, Strands, CrewAI, LlamaIndex and Bedrock AgentCore.

How this guide is laid out

Lessons run in the order you should learn them. Each one has a plain explanation, a diagram or a live widget, working code, a few interview questions and a short check. Lessons marked Project are complete builds you can put in your portfolio; lessons marked from scratch assume no background. Use ←/→ to move between lessons and / to search. Mark a lesson done and the sidebar keeps count in this browser.

What the labels mean

Python / Terminal
Code you can copy and run. Python examples target 3.11+. AWS account IDs are placeholders (123456789012).
Live lab
An interactive widget in the page. Change the inputs and watch what happens.
Interview
A question the way an interviewer asks it, with an answer you can say out loud in about a minute.
New 2026
Something released or renamed in 2026. Interviewers ask about these to see if you keep up.
Legacy
Still in old code and tutorials. Know it, but don't build new things on it.
About model IDs and versions

Package versions in this guide were last checked on 14 November 2026 (langchain 1.5.0, langgraph 1.2.22, strands-agents 1.65.0, crewai 1.15.41, llama-index 0.14.26, langchain-aws 1.9.3). LangChain 1.5 needs Python 3.11 or newer. Model IDs change every few months, so the code reads the model ID from an environment variable (BEDROCK_MODEL_ID). Pick a current one from the Bedrock console's model catalog, or run aws bedrock list-inference-profiles.

The one-paragraph version of everything

A large language model predicts the next token. Everything else is engineering around that. Prompting controls what it sees. RAG fetches the right facts and puts them in the prompt. Tool calling lets the model ask your code to do something and read the result. An agent is that tool call in a loop, until the job is done. The harness is the code that runs the loop safely: tools, context, memory, limits, approvals and logs. Evals tell you whether any of it works. On AWS, you call models through Bedrock (Converse API, or the OpenAI-compatible endpoints), retrieve with Knowledge Bases or your own vector store, and run agents on AgentCore.

Start here

Your route to a job

You already have the hard part most candidates lack: production data pipelines on AWS. This route builds the GenAI layer on top of it in about 10 to 12 weeks of evenings, aimed at Agentic AI Engineer and Agentic AI Data Engineer roles.

Three roles, one skill set

Role titleWhat they hire forWhat they test
GenAI Engineer / LLM EngineerRAG apps, prompt and model choice, API integration, evalsRAG design, chunking, retrieval metrics, LLM API coding, cost and latency
Agentic AI EngineerAgents that use tools, multi-step workflows, multi-agent systems, production deploymentReAct and tool loops, LangGraph/Strands, MCP, memory, guardrails, agent evals, system design
Agentic AI Data EngineerData pipelines that feed and are run by agents: indexing, text-to-SQL, data-quality agents, AI-ready lakehousesEverything above plus ingestion and re-indexing pipelines, metadata, access control, Glue/Athena/Step Functions as tools

How to study each lesson

Read it once. Run the code. Change one thing and predict what will happen before you run it again. Then answer the interview questions out loud without looking. If you can't explain it in a minute, read it again tomorrow.

What you can skip

For these roles you do not need to train models from scratch, do the maths of backpropagation by hand, or learn image diffusion, voice bots and GPU cluster tuning. Know what they are in one sentence each. Spend the time on RAG, agents, evals and production instead, because that is what interview loops for these roles spend their time on.

Interview: Why do you want to move from data engineering to agentic AI?

Say it as a continuation, not a switch: "Agents are only as good as the data and tools they can reach. I have spent six years building the pipelines, catalogs and quality checks that make data trustworthy on AWS. Over the last year I started putting agents on top of that: an assistant that finds tables and runs Athena SQL, a migration agent, a PR reviewer that placed second in a global hackathon. I want a role where building that layer is the job."

What changed: October–November 2026

GenAI tooling moves every month. This page lists what shipped in the last six weeks that an interviewer could reasonably ask about, and what it means for the rest of this guide. Read it last on your first pass, and first on every revision pass.

AreaWhat changedWhy you should care
LangChain New 2026langchain 1.5.0 and langgraph 1.2.22 are the current PyPI releases. LangChain 1.5 drops Python 3.10. 1.4.x added opt-in per-call interrupts to the human-in-the-loop middleware and standard "refusal" content blocks.Use Python 3.11+ for anything new. The create_agent + middleware design in the LangChain lesson is unchanged.
AgentCore Runtime V2 New 2026A newer platform version for Runtime. Agents start from a snapshot, so cold starts are consistent. Container images up to 10 GB, optional committed vCPU/memory baseline with burst, FUSE mounts, a build-warmup lifecycle hook, up to 250 concurrent connections per session, and an OverheadLatency CloudWatch metric that isolates platform time from your agent's time."How do you handle agent cold starts and latency on AWS?" now has a concrete answer.
AgentCore GatewayModel fallback rules (ordered chain of targets with a circuit breaker), InvokeModel wire format on inference targets, a Step Functions connector that exposes state machines as MCP tools, a Web Answer connector (cited web answers in one tool call), and MCP Tasks for long-running tool calls.Step Functions as agent tools is exactly the "agentic data engineer" pattern: the agent decides when to run a workflow, the state machine decides how.
AgentCore harnessNow built on Strands Harness with managed defaults (recommended model, summarisation when context overflows, automatic prompt caching) and built-in tools for planning, web retrieval, filesystem, context offloading and subagents. IAM auth to remote MCP servers.Managed defaults can change over time. For fixed behaviour, set model, prompt and allowed tools explicitly.
AgentCore CLI 1.0Package @aws/agentcore 1.0: direct resource commands (harness, runtime, memory, identity, gateway) plus project commands (create, dev, deploy, invoke, logs, traces, eval). agentcore migrate moves v0 projects.The commands in this guide and in your AgentCore handbook use this shape.
AgentCore Evaluations and MemoryMulti-dimension LLM-judge evaluators (several scored dimensions in one judge call), a Builtin.FailureAnalysis evaluator that groups failures by root cause, TTL on long-term memory records, consumption-based pricing for short-term memory.Failure analysis is the managed version of the "read 50 failed traces and bucket them" habit taught in the Evals chapter.
BedrockStructured outputs for all Claude models, contextual grounding checks in a Standard tier for Guardrails, Bedrock cost breakdowns by model and feature in Cost Explorer and Budgets, new small/fast models aimed at subagents.Schema-guaranteed JSON and per-model cost reporting remove two things teams used to build by hand.
StrandsBidi (speech-to-speech) agents GA, red-teaming and field-by-field structured-output scoring in Strands Evals, Strands Box local sandbox (preview).Red-team evals are a good answer to "how do you test for prompt injection?"
MCPSpec revision 2026-07-28 is current: stateless core (no initialize handshake, no protocol sessions), Tasks extension for long-running work, MCP Apps. AgentCore Runtime and Gateway support it.Old tutorials describe the stateful handshake. Say "stateless since the July 2026 revision" and you sound current.
How to keep this page true

Once a month, skim three pages: the AgentCore release notes, the AWS "ICYMI for AI builders" post, and the LangChain changelog. Links are in Sources. Ten minutes a month keeps you ahead of most candidates.

Interview: What's new in the agent space that you've looked at recently?

"Three things I've followed. First, MCP went stateless in the July 2026 revision and added a Tasks extension, which makes MCP servers much easier to scale behind a load balancer and lets a tool return a task ID for long work. Second, on AWS, AgentCore Runtime V2 starts agents from a snapshot for consistent cold starts and Gateway can now expose Step Functions workflows as tools and fall back between models when one is throttled. Third, evaluation is getting productised: AgentCore has a failure-analysis evaluator that clusters failed sessions by root cause. For my own work, the Step Functions connector matters most, because it lets an agent trigger a pipeline I already trust instead of re-implementing it as tool code."

How it all started

Timeline: 1950 to 2026

Generative AI didn't arrive with ChatGPT. It is seventy years of ideas, and three of them did most of the work: words as vectors (2013), attention (2017) and training at scale and then on human feedback (2020–22). Filter by era to see how each step built on the last.

1950Turing asks "Can machines think?"

Proposes the imitation game, a behavioural test for intelligence.

1956Dartmouth workshop names "artificial intelligence"

The field gets a name and a research agenda.

1966ELIZA

A rule-based chatbot that matched patterns. People still felt understood, which is the first lesson about chat interfaces.

1986Backpropagation popularisedpaper

Rumelhart, Hinton and Williams show how to train multi-layer networks efficiently. Every LLM is trained with it.

1997LSTMpaper

Recurrent networks that remember longer. They ran machine translation and speech for two decades.

2003Neural probabilistic language modelpaper

Bengio et al. predict the next word with a neural network and learn word vectors along the way: the shape of every LLM since.

2012AlexNet

Deep networks on GPUs win ImageNet by a wide margin. The deep learning boom starts.

2013word2vecpaper

Mikolov et al. at Google: words become vectors, and meaning becomes geometry (king − man + woman ≈ queen). Embeddings and vector search come from here.

2014Seq2seq and attentionpaper

Sutskever et al. map one sequence to another. Bahdanau et al. add attention so the decoder can look back at any input word. Goodfellow publishes GANs the same year.

Jun 2017"Attention Is All You Need"paper

Vaswani et al. at Google drop recurrence and keep only attention: the Transformer. It trains in parallel on GPUs, which is what made scale possible.

Jun 2018GPT-1paper

OpenAI pre-trains a decoder-only Transformer on books, then fine-tunes it per task. "Generative pre-training" is the G and P in GPT.

Oct 2018BERTpaper

Google's encoder-only model, trained by filling in masked words. Still the base of many embedding and reranking models.

Feb 2019GPT-2 (1.5B parameters)

Writes coherent paragraphs. OpenAI stages its release over misuse worries.

Jan 2020Scaling lawspaper

Kaplan et al.: loss falls predictably as you add parameters, data and compute. This justified building ever-bigger models.

May 2020GPT-3 (175B) and few-shot promptingpaper

"Language Models are Few-Shot Learners": show a few examples in the prompt and the model does a new task without retraining. Prompt engineering is born.

May 2020RAG paperpaper

Lewis et al. at Facebook AI combine a retriever over Wikipedia with a generator. The name Retrieval-Augmented Generation comes from this paper.

Jun 2021LoRApaper

Hu et al. fine-tune by training small low-rank matrices instead of the whole model. Cheap fine-tuning for everyone.

Jan 2022Chain-of-thought promptingpaper

Wei et al.: asking for step-by-step reasoning improves maths and logic. The seed of today's reasoning models.

Mar 2022InstructGPT and RLHFpaper

Ouyang et al. fine-tune on human-written answers, then on human preference rankings. A smaller aligned model beats a bigger raw one on what people prefer. Chinchilla (DeepMind) shows most models were under-trained on data.

Oct 2022ReActpaper

Yao et al. interleave reasoning ("Thought") with actions ("Action") and results ("Observation"). This loop is still the core of every agent.

Oct–Nov 2022LangChain and GPT Index (LlamaIndex) open-sourced

Harrison Chase releases LangChain; Jerry Liu releases GPT Index, later renamed LlamaIndex. Frameworks for chaining prompts and connecting data appear.

30 Nov 2022ChatGPT

A chat interface on an RLHF-tuned GPT-3.5. A million users in five days. Every company starts a GenAI project.

Dec 2022Constitutional AIpaper

Anthropic trains a model to critique and revise itself against written principles, reducing the human labelling needed.

Feb 2023Toolformer and LLaMApaper

Meta shows a model can learn when to call APIs, and releases LLaMA weights to researchers. Open models take off.

Mar 2023GPT-4, Claude, AutoGPT, HuggingGPT ("JARVIS")

GPT-4 and Anthropic's first Claude ship. AutoGPT and BabyAGI loop an LLM on its own goals and go viral. Microsoft's HuggingGPT, code-named JARVIS, uses an LLM as a controller that picks other models as tools. Reflexion adds self-critique memory.

Apr 2023Amazon Bedrock announced; Generative Agents

AWS announces Bedrock (GA September 2023). Stanford's Generative Agents simulate a town of agents with memory and reflection.

May 2023Tree of Thoughts, QLoRA, DPOpaper

Search over reasoning paths; fine-tuning a quantised model on one GPU; preference tuning without a reward model.

13 Jun 2023Native function calling

OpenAI's API returns structured tool calls instead of free text. Tool use stops being prompt parsing and becomes an API feature. Every provider follows.

Jul 2023Llama 2 and "Lost in the Middle"paper

Commercially usable open weights. Liu et al. show models use the start and end of a long context far better than the middle, which is why retrieval order and context size matter.

Oct 2023Self-RAG and MemGPTpaper

The model decides when to retrieve and grades its own evidence; an OS-style memory hierarchy for agents (later Letta). CrewAI appears around this time.

Jan 2024LangGraph and Corrective RAG

LangChain releases LangGraph for stateful, cyclic agent graphs. The CRAG paper grades retrieved documents and falls back to web search.

Apr 2024GraphRAGpaper

Microsoft Research builds a knowledge graph and community summaries for questions that span a whole corpus.

30 May 2024Bedrock Converse API

One request shape for every chat model on Bedrock, with tool use. Before this, every provider had its own JSON body.

Sep 2024Reasoning models (OpenAI o1) and Contextual Retrieval

Models trained to think before answering. Anthropic publishes Contextual Retrieval: prepend a short context to each chunk before embedding it.

Oct 2024Computer use

Anthropic lets Claude operate a desktop through screenshots and clicks. The same month, press reports describe a Google browser agent code-named "Project Jarvis"; Google later shows its browser agent as Project Mariner.

25 Nov 2024Model Context Protocol (MCP)

Anthropic open-sources a standard way for AI apps to connect to tools and data. It becomes the USB-C of agent tools within a year.

Dec 2024"Building effective agents"

Anthropic's guide separates workflows from agents and names five patterns (chaining, routing, parallelisation, orchestrator-workers, evaluator-optimiser). Interviewers still quote it.

Jan 2025DeepSeek-R1paper

An open-weights reasoning model trained largely with reinforcement learning on verifiable rewards, at a fraction of expected cost.

Feb–Mar 2025Coding agents and the Agents SDK

Claude Code (research preview) and Deep Research products show long-running agents doing real work. OpenAI ships the Responses API and Agents SDK.

Apr 2025Agent2Agent (A2A) protocol

Google proposes a protocol for agents built by different teams to discover and delegate to each other. It later moves to the Linux Foundation.

16 May 2025Strands Agents

AWS open-sources its model-driven agent SDK, already used inside Amazon Q Developer and AWS Glue.

Jun 2025"Context engineering"

The term spreads (Shopify's Tobi Lütke, Andrej Karpathy): the job is filling the context window with exactly the right information, not writing clever prompts.

Jul 2025Bedrock AgentCore preview and S3 Vectors preview

AWS announces managed building blocks to run agents in production, and vector storage built into S3.

Oct 2025AgentCore GA, LangChain/LangGraph 1.0, Agent Skills

AgentCore becomes generally available (13 Oct). LangChain 1.0 is rebuilt around create_agent and middleware on LangGraph. Anthropic introduces Agent Skills: folders of instructions an agent loads when needed.

Dec 2025Agentic AI Foundation; AgentCore Policy and Evaluations preview

MCP, OpenAI's AGENTS.md and Block's goose move to a neutral Linux Foundation home. AWS previews Cedar-based tool policies and built-in agent evals.

Jan–Feb 2026OpenClaw goes viral

A self-hosted "Jarvis"-style personal agent (first called Clawdbot) that you message on WhatsApp or Telegram. It passes 300k GitHub stars and its creator joins OpenAI. It also becomes the textbook example of agent security risk.

Early 2026"Harness engineering"

Agent = model + harness. Teams improve agents mostly by improving the loop, tools, checks and guardrails around a fixed model.

3 Mar 2026Bedrock Mantle OpenAI-compatible APIs GA

Call Bedrock models with the OpenAI SDK by changing the base URL and key.

31 Mar 2026AgentCore Evaluations GA

Built-in evaluators plus custom LLM and code-based judges, online and on demand.

17 Jun 2026AgentCore harness GA; Bedrock Managed Knowledge Base GA

Declare model, tools, skills and instructions; AWS runs a Strands-based agent loop for you. Managed RAG with agentic multi-hop retrieval and no vector DB to run.

Mid 2026"Loop engineering"

The next label after harness engineering: design loops that prompt the agent themselves, with a goal, a verifier, stop conditions and budgets.

28 Jul 2026MCP spec 2026-07-28

The biggest protocol revision: stateless base protocol (no session handshake), a Tasks extension for long-running calls, MCP Apps and an extensions framework.

Sep 2026LangChain 1.4, Strands Harness SDK

LangChain 1.4 (3 Sep) with more built-in middleware; AWS positions Strands as a harness SDK with a prebuilt default harness (9 Sep).

Oct 2026Another round of frontier models

New flagship releases from OpenAI, Google, Anthropic and open-weight labs. The skills in this guide carry over; only the model ID changes.

The three ideas to remember

Embeddings (2013) gave us meaning as numbers, which powers retrieval. Attention (2017) let models use all their context at once and train in parallel. Scale plus feedback (2020–22) turned next-word predictors into assistants that follow instructions. Agents (2022 onward) are what you get when an instruction-following model is allowed to call tools in a loop.

How it all started

The papers that matter

You don't need to read every paper. You need to know what each important one changed and be able to say it in a sentence. Read the abstract, the main figure and the results table; that's usually enough for interviews.

Paper (year)What it showedWhat to grasp for the job
Efficient Estimation of Word Representations (word2vec, 2013)Training a shallow network to predict neighbouring words produces vectors where similar meanings sit close together.Embeddings and cosine similarity: the base of every vector search.
Attention Is All You Need (2017)Self-attention alone, with positional encodings, beats recurrent models on translation and trains in parallel.Q/K/V attention, multi-head, why context length costs quadratic compute, why decoder-only models generate token by token.
BERT (2018)Pre-train an encoder by masking words, then fine-tune for classification or QA.Encoders power embedding models and cross-encoder rerankers. Decoders power generation.
Scaling Laws for Neural Language Models (2020)Loss improves as a power law of parameters, data and compute.Why bigger models got better; Chinchilla (2022) later showed you also need proportionally more data.
Language Models are Few-Shot Learners (GPT-3, 2020)A big enough model learns tasks from examples placed in the prompt.Zero-shot vs few-shot prompting; in-context learning needs no weight updates.
Retrieval-Augmented Generation for Knowledge-Intensive NLP (2020)Retrieve passages with a dense retriever, then condition generation on them; better factuality and you can update knowledge by changing the index.The retrieve-then-generate pattern and why RAG beats retraining for fresh or private knowledge.
LoRA (2021)Freeze the model and train small low-rank adapter matrices.Parameter-efficient fine-tuning; QLoRA adds 4-bit quantisation.
Chain-of-Thought Prompting (2022)Showing worked reasoning in examples makes large models reason better.Why reasoning models and "think step by step" help on multi-step tasks, and why they cost more tokens.
Training language models to follow instructions (InstructGPT, 2022)Supervised fine-tuning plus RLHF makes models helpful and more truthful.The pretrain → SFT → preference tuning pipeline behind every chat model.
ReAct (2022)Interleaving reasoning traces with tool actions beats reasoning-only or acting-only.The Thought → Action → Observation loop. Explain it with an example.
Toolformer (2023)A model can learn when and how to call APIs.Tool use is learnable; today it's trained into models and exposed as function calling.
Reflexion (2023)Agents improve by writing reflections on failures into memory and retrying.Self-critique loops and evaluator-optimizer patterns.
Generative Agents (2023)Memory stream + reflection + planning produce believable long-running agents.Memory types: recent events, importance scoring, reflection summaries.
Lost in the Middle (2023)Models use information at the start and end of long contexts better than the middle.Why you rerank, keep context short and put the best evidence first or last.
Self-RAG (2023) and Corrective RAG (2024)Let the model decide when to retrieve and grade retrieved passages; fall back when they're poor.Agentic RAG: retrieval as a decision, not a fixed first step.
From Local to Global: GraphRAG (2024)Entity graph + community summaries answer corpus-wide questions plain vector RAG misses.When to use graphs: "What are the main themes across all incidents?"
DeepSeek-R1 (2025)Reinforcement learning on verifiable rewards produces strong reasoning in an open model.Reasoning models, "thinking" tokens, and RL with verifiable rewards (RLVR).

Non-paper reading that interviewers quote

  • Anthropic, "Building effective agents" (Dec 2024): workflows vs agents, five composable patterns, start simple.
  • Anthropic, "Introducing Contextual Retrieval" (Sep 2024): contextual chunk embeddings + BM25 + reranking cut failed retrievals substantially in their tests.
  • Anthropic, "Effective context engineering for AI agents" (2025) and the many 2026 posts on harness engineering: context is a finite budget; curate it.
  • MCP specification (modelcontextprotocol.io): host, client, server; tools, resources, prompts.
  • AWS docs: Bedrock APIs and AgentCore developer guide: the services you'll actually deploy on.
Interview: Explain the Transformer in one minute.

"Each token becomes a vector. In every layer, each token builds a query, a key and a value. It compares its query against every other token's key to get attention weights, then takes a weighted mix of their values. That lets every token pull in information from any other token in one step, and the whole sequence is processed in parallel, which is why it scales on GPUs. Stack dozens of these layers with feed-forward blocks and you have an LLM. In a decoder-only model like GPT or Claude, a mask stops tokens looking ahead, so the model learns to predict the next token."

Interview: What did InstructGPT change?

"It showed that a raw pre-trained model is a document completer, not an assistant. Fine-tuning on human-written demonstrations and then optimising against a reward model trained on human preference rankings made a much smaller model preferred over GPT-3. That recipe, pre-train then supervised fine-tune then preference-tune, is how every chat model since has been made. DPO and RL on verifiable rewards are newer variants of the last step."

How it all started

Prompt apps → RAG → agents → agentic systems

How people actually built GenAI apps, generation by generation, and the problem that pushed each upgrade. If you can tell this story, you can answer most "why" questions in an interview.

Generation 1 (late 2022–2023): the prompt app

The first apps were a prompt template plus one API call. A "summarise this email" button, a marketing copy generator, a chatbot with a system prompt. LangChain's early "chains" piped one prompt's output into the next.

Generation 1: a template and one callPython
template = "Summarise this support ticket in 3 bullet points:\n\n{ticket}"
answer = llm(template.format(ticket=ticket_text))

What broke: the model only knows its training data. Ask about your company's refund policy or yesterday's pipeline failure and it either refuses or makes something up (hallucination). Pasting whole documents into the prompt hit context limits and cost too much.

Generation 2 (2023–2024): RAG

Split your documents into chunks, turn each into an embedding, store them in a vector database. At question time, embed the question, fetch the closest chunks, and put them in the prompt with "answer only from this context".

Generation 2: retrieve, then generatePython
chunks = vector_store.search(embed(question), k=5)
prompt = f"Answer using only this context:\n{chunks}\n\nQuestion: {question}"
answer = llm(prompt)

What broke: the retrieval step always ran exactly once, with the user's raw words. Multi-part questions ("compare last quarter's failures with this quarter's"), questions that need a database rather than documents, and questions that need an action (rerun a job, open a ticket) didn't fit a fixed pipeline. Teams bolted on query rewriting, routing and rerankers, and pipelines got complicated.

Generation 3 (2024–2025): agents

With native tool calling, the model itself decides what to do next. Give it tools (search docs, run SQL, call an API) and run a loop: the model picks a tool, your code runs it, the result goes back to the model, repeat until it answers.

Generation 3: the model drives a loopPython
messages = [user(question)]
while True:
    reply = llm(messages, tools=[search_docs, run_athena_sql, open_jira])
    if not reply.tool_calls:
        break                      # the model answered
    for call in reply.tool_calls:
        messages.append(tool_result(call.id, run(call)))

What broke: loops that never ended, wrong tool choices, runaway costs, prompt injection through retrieved text, agents that "succeeded" at the wrong thing, and no way to measure quality. Demos worked; production didn't.

Generation 4 (2025–2026): agentic systems

The model loop is the same, but now it lives inside a harness that teams engineer deliberately: curated tools exposed through MCP, RAG offered as one of several retrieval tools, memory, context compaction, budgets, human approval for risky actions, sandboxed code execution, tracing, and an eval suite that runs on every change. Managed platforms (Bedrock AgentCore, LangSmith Deployment) supply the infrastructure.

Four kinds of "engineering" you'll hear

TermWhen it peakedThe question it answers
Prompt engineering2022–2023How do I word the instruction so the model does the task?
Context engineering2024–2025What exactly should be in the context window at each step: instructions, retrieved facts, tool results, memory, and what should be left out?
Harness engineering2026What system around the model makes it act reliably: tools, verification, guardrails, limits, observability?
Loop engineeringmid-2026How do I design a loop that keeps prompting the agent itself toward a verifiable goal and knows when to stop?

Each one contains the previous. A good harness still needs good context, and good context still needs a clear prompt. You'll study each in its own lesson.

Interview: Why did the industry move from RAG to agents?

"Fixed RAG pipelines retrieve once, with the user's words, from one index. Real questions often need several lookups, different sources (documents, SQL, APIs) or an action. Once models got reliable native tool calling, it was simpler to give the model retrieval as a tool and let it decide when and how to search, than to keep adding routing and rewriting stages. RAG didn't go away; it became a tool inside the agent. That's what people mean by agentic RAG."

Interview: Is RAG dead now that context windows are huge?

"No. Long context helps, but cost and latency scale with tokens, models still use the middle of long contexts less reliably, and you can't fit a whole enterprise corpus anyway. You also need access control per document, which retrieval gives you. In practice teams use retrieval to pick a small, relevant, permitted set and use long context for the documents that matter."

LLM foundations

What an LLM really does

An LLM reads a sequence of tokens and outputs a probability for every possible next token. It picks one, appends it, and repeats. Chat, reasoning, tool calls and JSON output are all this one operation repeated.

Tokens

Models don't see characters or words. A tokenizer splits text into sub-word pieces from a fixed vocabulary (typically 100k–200k entries), using byte-pair encoding or similar. Common words are one token; rare words split into several. English averages about 4 characters or ¾ of a word per token. Code, numbers and non-English text use more tokens. You pay per token, and context limits are in tokens.

Tokenizer playgroundLive lab

This is a teaching approximation of BPE (it splits on spaces, punctuation, case changes and long words). Real tokenizers differ per model; use the provider's token-counting API for real numbers.

Embeddings: tokens become vectors

Each token ID is looked up in an embedding table and becomes a vector of a few thousand numbers. Through training, tokens used in similar ways end up with similar vectors. The model's layers keep transforming these vectors; by the last layer each position holds a rich, context-aware representation that is projected to a score for every vocabulary token.

Generation, step by step

Context window

The context window is the maximum number of tokens (input plus output) the model can attend to at once: from about 128k up to 1M or more in 2026. Everything the model "knows" in a conversation must be inside it. Chat apps feel like they remember because the app resends the whole history each turn. The model itself is stateless between calls.

Common misconception

A bigger context window doesn't mean the model uses all of it equally well. Quality on facts buried in the middle of very long inputs drops ("lost in the middle", and in 2025–26 people call the gradual decay "context rot"). Keep context focused.

Three numbers to know about any model

Parameters
The learned weights. More usually means more capable and more expensive to run.
Context window
How much input and output fits in one call.
Price per 1M tokens
Separate input and output prices; output often costs 3–5× input. Cached input is much cheaper.
Count tokens before you send (Bedrock)Python
import boto3, os, json
brt = boto3.client("bedrock-runtime", region_name="us-east-1")

# CountTokens works for supported models and is free; it returns what the call would bill.
resp = brt.count_tokens(
    modelId=os.environ["BEDROCK_MODEL_ID"],
    input={"converse": {"messages": [{"role": "user", "content": [{"text": "How many tokens is this?"}]}]}},
)
print(resp["inputTokens"])
Interview: Why do LLMs hallucinate?

"Because they're trained to produce plausible next tokens, not to check facts. When the answer isn't strongly represented in training data or the context, the most probable continuation can still be fluent and wrong. You reduce it by grounding (RAG with citations), letting the model say 'I don't know', lowering temperature for factual tasks, verifying outputs with tools or a second check, and evaluating faithfulness."

Interview: Does the model remember previous chats?

"No. The API is stateless. The application resends prior messages, or stores memory elsewhere and injects relevant parts into the prompt. Some APIs, like the OpenAI-style Responses API on Bedrock, store conversation state server-side and let you pass a previous response ID, but that's the service resending context for you."

LLM foundations · from scratch

Tokens, context windows and cost

Models don't read words, they read tokens. Tokens decide what fits in the context window, how long a call takes and what it costs. Nearly every production question (cost, latency, "why did it forget?") comes back to counting tokens.

What a token is

A tokenizer splits text into pieces from a fixed vocabulary (typically 50k–200k entries) and maps each piece to an integer ID. Common words are one token; rare words are split into several sub-word pieces. This scheme is called byte-pair encoding (BPE): start with single bytes, repeatedly merge the most frequent adjacent pair, stop when the vocabulary is full.

TextRoughly how it splitsTokens
The job failedThe · job · failed3
OutOfMemoryErrorOut · Of · Memory · Error4
s3://qm-raw/2026/11/14/s · 3 · :// · q · m · - · raw · / · 20 · 26 · / · 11 · …15+
నమస్కారం (Telugu)several byte-level pieces6–12

Rules of thumb for English: 1 token ≈ 4 characters ≈ ¾ of a word, so 1,000 tokens ≈ 750 words ≈ 1.5 pages. Code, JSON, IDs, paths and non-English text use more tokens per character. Every model family has its own tokenizer, so the same text has different counts on different models.

The context window

The context window is the maximum number of tokens the model can see in one call: system prompt + tool definitions + conversation history + retrieved documents + the answer it is writing. The model has no memory outside it. "Memory" in a chat app is your code re-sending earlier messages every time.

Input tokens
Everything you send. Billed per million. Read in parallel, so fairly fast.
Output tokens
What the model writes. Usually 3–5× the price of input and generated one at a time, so output length drives latency.
Cached input tokens
A stable prefix (system prompt, tools, a long document) can be cached; reads of the cache are billed at a fraction of the input price and are faster.
Reasoning tokens
Thinking tokens that reasoning models produce before the answer. Billed as output even if you never see them.

Cost calculator

What does this feature cost per month?Live lab

Prices are examples per 1 million tokens. Type the real ones from the Bedrock pricing page for the model you use.

Play with it: halve the input tokens (retrieve 4 chunks instead of 8) and watch the bill. Then set caching to 70%. Those two levers, send less and cache the stable part, are the first two answers to every cost question.

Read real token usage from every Converse call and log itPython
import os, boto3
brt = boto3.client("bedrock-runtime", region_name="us-east-1")

resp = brt.converse(
    modelId=os.environ["BEDROCK_MODEL_ID"],
    messages=[{"role": "user", "content": [{"text": "Explain a Glue bookmark in two sentences."}]}],
    inferenceConfig={"maxTokens": 200},
)
u = resp["usage"]                       # the source of truth: never estimate when you can read it
print(u["inputTokens"], u["outputTokens"], u["totalTokens"])
print(u.get("cacheReadInputTokens", 0), u.get("cacheWriteInputTokens", 0))
print(resp["metrics"]["latencyMs"])     # log these four numbers per request, tagged with feature and tenant
Three limits people mix up

Context window: total tokens in + out for one call. Max output tokens: a smaller, separate cap on the answer. Quota: tokens per minute and requests per minute your account may send; exceeding it gives a throttling error, not a wrong answer.

Interview: A user says the chatbot "forgot" what they said 30 messages ago. What happened?

"The model has no memory between calls; the app re-sends history each turn. Either the history was truncated to fit the context window or a budget, or it was summarised and the detail was lost, or it's still in the window but buried in the middle of a very long prompt where recall is weakest. I'd check the actual request payload for that turn. Fixes: keep a running summary plus the last N turns verbatim, store key facts in a long-term memory that's retrieved by relevance, and put the most important instructions at the start and end of the prompt."

Interview: Estimate the monthly cost of a RAG assistant: 5,000 questions a day, 6 chunks of 800 tokens, 400-token answers.

"Input per request is about 6 × 800 = 4,800 context tokens plus roughly 700 for system prompt and question, so 5,500. Output is 400. Per day that's 27.5M input and 2M output tokens. At example prices of $3 and $15 per million, that's $82.50 + $30 = about $112 a day, roughly $3,400 a month. Then I'd say how I'd cut it: rerank and send 3 chunks instead of 6, cache the system prompt, route easy questions to a smaller model, and cap answer length. I'd also add the embedding and vector-store cost, which is usually small next to generation."

LLM foundations

Transformers and attention

Attention is how each token decides which other tokens matter for it. In "the job failed because it ran out of memory", the token "it" needs to look at "job". Attention lets it do that directly, in one step, for every pair of tokens.

Click a word to see where it "looks"Live lab
Click any word.

Weights are illustrative, hand-set to show typical patterns: pronouns attend to their referents, verbs to their subjects and objects. Real models have dozens of heads per layer, each with its own pattern.

How attention works

  1. Each token's vector is multiplied by three learned matrices to get a query (what am I looking for?), a key (what do I contain?) and a value (what will I pass on?).
  2. For each token, compare its query with every key (dot product), scale by √d, and apply softmax. These are the attention weights; they sum to 1.
  3. The token's new vector is the weighted sum of all the values.
  4. Multi-head: do this in parallel with many smaller Q/K/V sets so different heads learn different relationships (syntax, coreference, position).
  5. In decoder-only models a causal mask hides future tokens, so position 5 can only look at positions 1–5.
Scaled dot-product attention in 12 lines of NumPyPython
import numpy as np

def attention(X, Wq, Wk, Wv, causal=True):
    Q, K, V = X @ Wq, X @ Wk, X @ Wv            # (n, d) each
    scores = Q @ K.T / np.sqrt(K.shape[1])       # (n, n): every token vs every token
    if causal:
        scores += np.triu(np.full_like(scores, -1e9), k=1)  # hide the future
    w = np.exp(scores - scores.max(axis=1, keepdims=True))
    w /= w.sum(axis=1, keepdims=True)            # softmax per row
    return w @ V, w                              # new vectors, attention weights

n, d = 5, 8
X = np.random.randn(n, d)
out, weights = attention(X, *(np.random.randn(d, d) for _ in range(3)))
print(weights.round(2))   # lower-triangular because of the causal mask

A Transformer block

Terms you'll be asked about

Positional encoding
Attention ignores order by itself, so position is added. Modern models use rotary position embeddings (RoPE).
KV cache
During generation, keys and values for earlier tokens are stored so each new token only computes its own. It's why long conversations use a lot of GPU memory, and it's the basis of prompt caching.
Mixture of Experts (MoE)
The feed-forward layer is split into many experts and a router activates a few per token. Huge total parameters, much smaller active parameters per token.
Encoder vs decoder
Encoders (BERT) see the whole input both ways; good for embeddings and classification. Decoders (GPT, Claude, Llama) are causal and generate text.
Quadratic cost
Attention compares every token with every other: n² work. Long context is expensive; many tricks (sparse/sliding attention, FlashAttention) reduce the cost.
Interview: What is the KV cache and why does it matter in production?

"When a model generates token 500, it needs the keys and values of tokens 1–499 for attention. Recomputing them every step would be wasteful, so they're cached. It makes generation fast but uses memory proportional to context length, which limits how many long requests a GPU can serve. Providers reuse the cache across requests that share a prefix, which is what prompt caching is, and why putting stable content like system prompts and tool definitions first saves money."

LLM foundations · from scratch

Attention by hand: a worked example

Self-attention sounds abstract until you compute it once with small numbers. Here is the whole operation on a three-token sentence, in plain NumPy, with every intermediate value printed.

The idea in one sentence

For each token, attention asks "which other tokens matter to me right now?", scores every token, turns the scores into weights that add up to 1, and replaces the token's vector with the weighted average of all the vectors. After that step the vector for it carries information about job.

The formula

Attention(Q, K, V) = softmax(Q·Kᵀ / √d) · V

Q (query)
What this token is looking for.
K (key)
What each token offers, used for matching against queries.
V (value)
The content each token passes on if it is attended to.
√d
Divide by the square root of the vector size so scores don't grow with dimension and saturate the softmax.

In a real model Q, K and V are the token vectors multiplied by three learned matrices. To keep the arithmetic visible, the example below uses the token vectors directly.

attention_by_hand.py (runs with only NumPy)Python
import numpy as np

tokens = ["job", "failed", "it"]
X = np.array([[1.0, 0.0],      # job
              [0.0, 1.0],      # failed
              [0.9, 0.1]])     # it  (starts out similar to "job")
d = X.shape[1]

scores = X @ X.T / np.sqrt(d)                 # how well each query matches each key
weights = np.exp(scores) / np.exp(scores).sum(axis=1, keepdims=True)   # softmax per row
out = weights @ X                             # weighted average of values

np.set_printoptions(precision=3, suppress=True)
print("scores for 'it': ", scores[2])
print("weights for 'it':", weights[2])
print("new vector 'it': ", out[2])

# Causal mask: a decoder may not look at later tokens
mask = np.triu(np.ones((3, 3)), k=1).astype(bool)
masked = np.where(mask, -np.inf, scores)
w2 = np.exp(masked) / np.exp(masked).sum(axis=1, keepdims=True)
print("causal weights:\n", w2)
OutputOutput
scores for 'it':  [0.636 0.071 0.58 ]
weights for 'it': [0.398 0.226 0.376]
new vector 'it':  [0.736 0.264]
causal weights:
 [[1.    0.    0.   ]
 [0.33  0.67  0.   ]
 [0.398 0.226 0.376]]

Read the three printed lines for it: it matched job best (0.636), so job got the largest weight (0.398), and the new vector moved toward job. That is coreference resolution in miniature. The causal mask at the bottom is the only difference between an encoder and a GPT-style decoder: each row can only see itself and earlier tokens, which is why the model can be trained to predict the next token.

From one attention step to a whole model

  1. Embedding: token IDs become vectors (a lookup table).
  2. Position information: attention has no sense of order, so position is added (sinusoidal in the original paper; rotary embeddings, RoPE, in most current models).
  3. Multi-head attention: run several attention operations in parallel, each with its own learned Q/K/V matrices, so one head can track syntax and another coreference.
  4. Feed-forward layer: a small neural network applied to each token separately. Much of the "knowledge" lives here.
  5. Residual connections and layer norm: add the layer's input back to its output so information and gradients flow through dozens of layers.
  6. Stack steps 3–5 many times (tens of layers), then project the final vector onto the vocabulary to get a score per possible next token.
Why attention costs what it costs

Every token attends to every other token, so compute grows with the square of sequence length. That is why long prompts are slower, why the KV cache exists (keys and values for earlier tokens are stored and reused while generating), and why prompt caching is cheap for the provider: the cached prefix has already been processed.

Interview: Walk me through self-attention.

"Each token's vector is projected into a query, a key and a value. For a given token, I take the dot product of its query with every token's key, divide by the square root of the dimension, and softmax to get weights that sum to one. The output is the weighted sum of the value vectors. So each token's new representation is a mix of the tokens most relevant to it. Multi-head attention does this several times in parallel with different projections. In a decoder there's a causal mask so a token can only attend to earlier positions. Cost is quadratic in sequence length, which is why context length drives latency and why KV caching matters during generation."

Interview: Encoder-only, decoder-only, encoder-decoder: which is used where?

"Encoder-only models like BERT see the whole input in both directions and output a representation; today they're used for embeddings, rerankers and classifiers. Decoder-only models like GPT, Claude, Llama and Nova use a causal mask and generate text token by token; almost every chat model is this. Encoder-decoder models like the original Transformer and T5 encode the input and decode an output, common for translation and still used in some speech models. In a RAG system I use both families: an encoder-style model to embed and rerank, a decoder-only model to write the answer."

LLM foundations

How models are trained

You won't train a frontier model, but you must know the stages, because they explain model behaviour: why base models ramble, why chat models refuse, why reasoning models think out loud, and when fine-tuning is worth it.

Pre-training
Predict the next token over web, code, books. Produces a base model: knowledgeable but it continues documents rather than answering.
SFT
Supervised fine-tuning on example conversations teaches the assistant format, instruction following and tool-call format.
RLHF / DPO
Humans (or AI judges) rank answers; the model is optimised toward preferred ones. Makes it helpful and safer; also causes over-refusal or flattery if overdone.
RLVR
Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards: reward correct maths answers, passing tests, completed agent tasks. This produced 2025–26 reasoning models and most of the jump in agent reliability.
Reasoning / thinking
Models spend extra "thinking" tokens before answering. Many APIs expose a budget or effort setting. More thinking: better on hard multi-step tasks, slower, pricier.
Distillation
Train a small model on a big model's outputs. Small, fast models (Haiku, Nova Micro/Lite, gpt-oss-20b) are often distilled.

Open-weight vs closed models

Closed (API only)Open weights
ExamplesClaude, GPT, Gemini, Amazon NovaLlama, Mistral, DeepSeek, Qwen, gpt-oss, Gemma
ProsUsually strongest at hard reasoning and agentic tool use; no infraSelf-host, fine-tune fully, data never leaves your VPC, cheaper at high volume
On AWSBedrock serverlessBedrock (many open models via Mantle), SageMaker, EC2/EKS with vLLM
Interview: When would you fine-tune instead of using RAG?

"Fine-tune to change behaviour: a fixed output format, a tone, a domain's jargon, or to make a small cheap model do one narrow task as well as a big one. Use RAG to add knowledge, especially knowledge that changes or needs access control and citations. Fine-tuning bakes facts into weights where you can't update, cite or permission them. Often you do both: RAG for facts, a fine-tuned small model for format and cost. And try prompting first; it's the cheapest experiment."

LLM foundations

Temperature, top-p and limits

The model gives scores (logits) for every next token. Sampling settings decide how one is picked. Drag the sliders and watch the distribution change.

Softmax with temperature and top-pLive lab

Prompt: The Glue job failed with an ___

temperature
Divides logits before softmax. 0 ≈ always the top token (deterministic-ish); 1 = the model's own distribution; above 1 flattens it (more random). Use 0–0.3 for extraction, SQL, tool use; 0.7–1 for creative writing.
top_p
Keep the smallest set of tokens whose probabilities add up to p, then sample from those. Cuts off the long tail of unlikely tokens. Change temperature or top_p, usually not both.
max_tokens
Hard cap on output length. If hit, the stop reason is max_tokens and the answer is cut off: check for it.
stop sequences
Strings that end generation early.
Temperature 0 is not fully deterministic

Batched GPU inference and floating-point order can still change outputs slightly between runs. Some reasoning models fix or ignore temperature. Never rely on byte-identical outputs; evaluate on meaning.

Setting inference parameters in ConversePython
resp = brt.converse(
    modelId=os.environ["BEDROCK_MODEL_ID"],
    messages=[{"role": "user", "content": [{"text": "Write a SQL query that counts failed jobs by day."}]}],
    inferenceConfig={"temperature": 0.0, "maxTokens": 800, "stopSequences": ["</sql>"]},
)
print(resp["stopReason"])   # end_turn | max_tokens | stop_sequence | tool_use | guardrail_intervened ...

LLM foundations

Prompt engineering that works

Prompting is giving a very capable new colleague a clear brief: who they are working for, what good looks like, what they have, and what to hand back. Tricks matter much less than clarity in 2026 models.

The anatomy of a production prompt

System prompt template (tags make sections easy for the model and for you)Prompt
You are a data-quality assistant for the company's analytics lakehouse on AWS.

<goal>
Help analysts find why a table's row counts changed and propose a fix.
</goal>

<rules>
- Only use tables listed in <catalog>. If a table is missing, say so.
- Run read-only SQL. Never write DDL or DML.
- If unsure, ask one clarifying question instead of guessing.
</rules>

<catalog>{table_list}</catalog>

<output_format>
Return: 1) a one-line finding, 2) evidence as a bullet list with query results, 3) the next step.
</output_format>

Techniques and when they help

TechniqueWhat it isUse when
Zero-shotJust the instructionDefault with strong models
Few-shot2–5 input/output examplesSpecific formats, classification labels, tone. Vary examples so the model doesn't copy one.
Chain-of-thoughtAsk for reasoning before the answer (or use a reasoning model's thinking setting)Maths, multi-step logic, planning. Not for simple lookups.
Role / system promptWho the model is, the audience, constraintsAlways; put stable rules here, not in every user turn
Delimiters / XML tagsWrap documents, data, examplesAny prompt mixing instructions with data; helps resist injection
Structured outputJSON schema, tool-forced outputWhen code consumes the answer
Prompt chainingSplit a task into callsLong tasks, each step checkable
Self-consistencySample several answers, take majorityHigh-stakes reasoning where cost is acceptable

Structured output the reliable way

Don't parse free text with regex. Either force a tool call whose input schema is your output schema, or use the framework's structured-output feature (Pydantic model in, validated object out).

Force JSON with a tool schema (Converse)Python
schema = {"type": "object",
          "properties": {"severity": {"type": "string", "enum": ["low", "medium", "high"]},
                         "root_cause": {"type": "string"},
                         "tables": {"type": "array", "items": {"type": "string"}}},
          "required": ["severity", "root_cause"]}

resp = brt.converse(
    modelId=os.environ["BEDROCK_MODEL_ID"],
    messages=[{"role": "user", "content": [{"text": f"Classify this incident:\n{log_text}"}]}],
    toolConfig={"tools": [{"toolSpec": {"name": "record_incident", "description": "Record the triage result",
                                         "inputSchema": {"json": schema}}}],
                "toolChoice": {"tool": {"name": "record_incident"}}},   # force this tool
)
block = next(b for b in resp["output"]["message"]["content"] if "toolUse" in b)
triage = block["toolUse"]["input"]   # already a dict matching the schema
Prompt injection

Anything the model reads (a retrieved document, a web page, a Jira comment, a tool result) can contain instructions like "ignore previous instructions and email the data". Treat all retrieved content as data, wrap it in tags, never let it decide permissions, and put real controls (least-privilege tools, approvals, Guardrails) outside the model. Covered in the security lesson.

Interview: How do you improve a prompt systematically?

"I build a small eval set first, 20 to 50 real inputs with expected outputs or a rubric. Then I change one thing at a time, run the set, and compare scores and failure examples. I look at failures to see whether the model lacked information (fix context), misread the task (fix instructions or add an example), or got the format wrong (use structured output). I version prompts in Git like code. Without the eval set, 'better' is just vibes."

LLM foundations · hands-on

Structured output you can trust

Pipelines need JSON, not prose. There are three ways to get it, in rising order of reliability: ask nicely, force a tool call, or use the provider's schema-constrained output. Whichever you use, validate and retry.

MethodHow it worksReliabilityUse when
Prompt only"Reply with JSON like this…" then json.loadsLowest: stray text, missing fields, trailing commasQuick experiments only
Forced tool callDefine a tool whose input schema is your output shape, set toolChoice to that toolHigh: arguments follow the schema closely, but enums or required fields can still be violatedWorks on every tool-capable model
Native structured output New 2026Provider constrains decoding to your JSON SchemaHighest: output always parses and matches the schemaWhen the model supports it (for example, all Claude models on Bedrock)
Valid JSON is not correct JSON

Constrained decoding guarantees the shape. It cannot guarantee that "severity": "low" is the right call. You still need evals for the values.

The pattern that works on any model: Pydantic + forced tool + repair loop

extract.py: schema from Pydantic, forced tool call, validate, retry with the errorPython
import os, boto3
from typing import Literal
from pydantic import BaseModel, Field, ValidationError

brt = boto3.client("bedrock-runtime", region_name="us-east-1")

class Incident(BaseModel):
    job_name: str = Field(description="Glue job or DAG name exactly as written in the log")
    severity: Literal["low", "medium", "high"]
    root_cause: str = Field(description="One sentence, plain language")
    tables: list[str] = Field(default_factory=list, description="Fully qualified table names mentioned")
    needs_human: bool

TOOL = {"toolSpec": {"name": "record_incident",
                     "description": "Record the triage result for one pipeline failure.",
                     "inputSchema": {"json": Incident.model_json_schema()}}}

def extract(log_text: str, max_attempts: int = 3) -> Incident:
    messages = [{"role": "user", "content": [{"text": f"Triage this failure log:\n<log>\n{log_text}\n</log>"}]}]
    for _ in range(max_attempts):
        resp = brt.converse(
            modelId=os.environ["BEDROCK_MODEL_ID"],
            system=[{"text": "You triage data-pipeline failures. Use only facts in the log."}],
            messages=messages,
            toolConfig={"tools": [TOOL], "toolChoice": {"tool": {"name": "record_incident"}}},
            inferenceConfig={"temperature": 0, "maxTokens": 600},
        )
        msg = resp["output"]["message"]
        call = next(b["toolUse"] for b in msg["content"] if "toolUse" in b)
        try:
            return Incident.model_validate(call["input"])       # the real gate
        except ValidationError as e:
            messages.append(msg)                                 # show the model its own attempt
            messages.append({"role": "user", "content": [{"toolResult": {
                "toolUseId": call["toolUseId"], "status": "error",
                "content": [{"text": f"Validation failed, fix and call again:\n{e}"}]}}]})
    raise RuntimeError("could not produce a valid Incident")

print(extract("ERROR qm_trusted_load: An error occurred while calling o112.pyWriteDynamicFrame. "
              "java.lang.OutOfMemoryError: Java heap space ... table glue_catalog.trusted.qm_sessions"))
Typical outputOutput
job_name='qm_trusted_load' severity='high' root_cause='The job ran out of Java heap memory while writing the DynamicFrame.' tables=['glue_catalog.trusted.qm_sessions'] needs_human=False

Schema design tips that change accuracy

  • Describe every field. The description is prompt text; the model reads it.
  • Use enums (Literal) instead of free strings wherever a fixed set exists.
  • Put reasoning before the verdict. A reasoning field placed before severity lets the model think first; fields are generated in order.
  • Allow "unknown". If a value might be missing, make it optional or add an explicit "unknown" option, otherwise the model invents one.
  • Keep it flat and small. Deeply nested schemas with dozens of fields fail more; split into two calls.
  • Version the schema and store the version with each output so downstream tables can evolve.
Batch extraction in a pipeline

For millions of records, don't loop over Converse. Write the prompts as JSONL to S3 and use Bedrock batch inference (about half the on-demand price, results back in S3), then validate each line with the same Pydantic model in a Glue job and send failures to a retry queue. That is "LLM as a pipeline stage".

Interview: How do you make sure an LLM returns valid JSON in production?

"Three layers. First, use the strongest mechanism the model supports: native structured output with a JSON Schema if available, otherwise a forced tool call whose input schema is my output shape. Second, validate every response with Pydantic or a JSON Schema validator; on failure, send the validation error back and retry up to a small limit. Third, treat shape and correctness separately: constrained decoding only guarantees shape, so I keep an eval set that checks field values against labelled examples. For big volumes I run it through batch inference and route records that still fail to a dead-letter path instead of failing the job."

LLM foundations

Choosing a model in 2026

Model names change every quarter; the way you choose doesn't. Start with the strongest model to prove the task works, measure it with evals, then move down to cheaper models for the steps that don't need the top tier.

The families you'll meet on Bedrock

FamilyTypical tiersKnown for
Anthropic ClaudeOpus (top), Sonnet (balanced), Haiku (fast)Agentic coding and tool use, long documents, instruction following
OpenAI GPT (incl. gpt-oss open weights)Flagship, mini/nano; gpt-oss-120b and -20b on MantleReasoning, broad ecosystem; OpenAI-compatible APIs on Bedrock
Amazon NovaMicro, Lite, Pro, Premier; Nova Sonic for speechLow cost and latency on AWS, multimodal
Meta Llama, Mistral, DeepSeek, Qwen, Gemma, MiniMax, KimiVarious sizesOpen weights, cost control, specific languages or tasks
Embedding modelsTitan Text Embeddings V2, Cohere EmbedVectors for RAG and semantic search
RerankersCohere Rerank, Amazon RerankReorder retrieved chunks by relevance

Decision checklist

  1. Capability: does it pass your eval set? Test 2–3 candidates on the same 50 examples.
  2. Tool use: for agents, check tool-call accuracy and parallel tool calls specifically.
  3. Latency: time to first token and tokens per second at your prompt size.
  4. Cost: (input tokens × input price + output tokens × output price) × daily volume. Include thinking tokens.
  5. Context window and modalities (images, PDFs, audio).
  6. Region and data residency: is it in your region, or only through a cross-region inference profile?
  7. API support: Converse, Responses/Chat Completions, Messages? (Next chapter.)
Model routing

Production agents rarely use one model. A common split: a small fast model to classify and route, a top model to plan and reason, a mid model for tool-heavy steps, and an embedding model plus reranker for retrieval. LangChain's ModelFallbackMiddleware and Bedrock's Intelligent Prompt Routing help.

Find current model and inference-profile IDsTerminal
aws bedrock list-foundation-models --region us-east-1 \
  --query "modelSummaries[?contains(inferenceTypesSupported,'ON_DEMAND')].modelId" --output text

# Most newer models are called through a cross-Region inference profile (us., eu., apac., global.)
aws bedrock list-inference-profiles --region us-east-1 \
  --query "inferenceProfileSummaries[].inferenceProfileId" --output text

export BEDROCK_MODEL_ID="<paste a profile id from above>"

LLM foundations · decisions

Prompting vs RAG vs fine-tuning

There are four ways to make a model better at your task. Interviewers ask this as "when would you fine-tune?", and the expected answer is "rarely, and last".

Problem you seeRight toolWhy
Model doesn't know your company's facts or they change oftenRAGKnowledge lives in documents you can update in minutes; answers can cite sources
Model needs live data or must take actionsTools / agentsQuery the system of record instead of baking data into weights
Output format, tone or style is wrongPrompt + examples, then fine-tuneBehaviour problems are what fine-tuning is good at
A narrow task at huge volume is too slow or costly on a big modelFine-tune / distil a small modelA tuned small model can match a large one on one task for a fraction of the cost
Specialised vocabulary the model keeps misreadingGlossary in prompt → fine-tune if it persistsTry the cheap fix first

One line to remember: RAG changes what the model knows; fine-tuning changes how the model behaves. They're not rivals, and mature systems sometimes use both.

Fine-tuning words you should recognise

Full fine-tuning
Update all weights. Expensive; needs lots of data; rare for LLM apps.
LoRA / PEFT
Freeze the model and train small low-rank adapter matrices (well under 1% of the parameters). Cheap, fast, and adapters can be swapped per task. The default method today.
QLoRA
LoRA on a 4-bit quantised base model so it fits on one GPU.
Distillation
A large "teacher" model labels data; a small "student" model is trained on those labels. Bedrock offers managed model distillation.
Quantisation
Store weights in fewer bits (8 or 4 instead of 16) to cut memory and cost with a small quality loss.
Catastrophic forgetting
Tuning on a narrow task makes the model worse at everything else. Mitigate with adapters and mixed data.
Instruction data format
JSONL rows of prompt/completion (or message lists). Quality and consistency of these rows matter more than quantity.
On AWS

Bedrock supports customising some models (fine-tuning, distillation) and importing your own weights with Custom Model Import. SageMaker AI is the route when you need full control of training (LoRA on open models, your own containers). Customised Bedrock models have their own hosting and pricing terms, so cost them before you start.

Interview: When would you fine-tune instead of using RAG?

"I'd start with prompting and few-shot examples, then RAG if the gap is knowledge. I'd consider fine-tuning when the gap is behaviour that prompts can't fix reliably: a strict output style, a domain-specific classification, or a narrow high-volume task where a tuned small model can replace a large one and cut cost and latency. Preconditions: a few hundred to a few thousand high-quality labelled examples, an eval set that shows prompting has plateaued, and a plan to retrain when the base model is retired. I wouldn't fine-tune to add facts that change, because retraining for every document update is slow and you lose citations. I'd use LoRA rather than full fine-tuning."

Interview: What is LoRA, in simple terms?

"Instead of updating a huge weight matrix W, LoRA freezes W and learns two small matrices A and B whose product is a low-rank update, so the effective weight is W + BA. With rank 8 or 16 that's a tiny number of trainable parameters, so training is fast, needs little memory, and the result is a small adapter file you can load on top of the base model or swap per task. Quality is usually close to full fine-tuning for task adaptation."

Calling LLMs on AWS

Bedrock APIs and endpoints map

Bedrock has two inference endpoints and five API shapes. Interviewers increasingly ask "Converse or Invoke or the OpenAI-compatible API, and why?" This lesson gives you the map.

Which one should you use?

SituationUseWhy
New AWS-native app, want to switch models freelyConverse on bedrock-runtimeSame request/response for every chat model; tool use, documents, images, Guardrails all built in. Most frameworks (LangChain ChatBedrockConverse, Strands BedrockModel, LlamaIndex BedrockConverse) use it underneath.
Need a provider-specific feature not yet in Converse, or embeddings / imagesInvokeModelRaw provider JSON; you handle each provider's format.
Existing OpenAI SDK code to migrateChat Completions or Responses (bedrock-runtime recommended; bedrock-mantle also supported)Change OPENAI_BASE_URL and use a Bedrock API key.
Want stored conversation state, background jobs or server-side tools (web search, code interpreter)Responses API on bedrock-mantleOnly mantle runs background requests and server-side tools.
Anthropic-native features and SDKMessages APIClaude's own format, on either endpoint.

Authentication

IAM / SigV4
The default for boto3 and AWS SDKs. Use roles in Lambda, Glue, ECS, AgentCore. Permissions like bedrock:InvokeModel, bedrock:InvokeModelWithResponseStream (Converse uses the same actions).
Bedrock API keys
Bearer tokens for quick starts and the OpenAI SDK. Short-term keys are preferred; treat long-term keys like passwords and put them in Secrets Manager.
Mantle permissions
Inference on bedrock-mantle authorises bedrock-mantle:CreateInference.

Inference profiles and Regions

Many models are called through a cross-Region inference profile (IDs start with us., eu., apac., global.) that spreads load across Regions for capacity. Geographic profiles keep data inside that geography; global. profiles may process anywhere. If you have data-residency rules, choose a geographic profile and say so in interviews.

Interview: Converse vs InvokeModel?

"Converse gives one message-based schema across all chat models, with tool use, system prompts, documents and Guardrails as first-class fields, and a consistent usage and stop-reason format. Switching from Claude to Nova or Llama is a model ID change. InvokeModel sends the provider's native body, so you get every provider-specific parameter, but you write a different payload and parser per provider. I default to Converse and drop to Invoke for embeddings, image models or a feature Converse doesn't expose yet."

Interview: What is Bedrock Mantle?

"Mantle is AWS's distributed inference engine behind many Bedrock models, especially open-weight ones, and it has its own endpoint, bedrock-mantle.{region}.api.aws. It exposes OpenAI-compatible Chat Completions and Responses APIs and the Anthropic Messages API, so you can point the OpenAI SDK at Bedrock by changing the base URL and key. Its Responses API supports stored conversation state with previous_response_id, background (async) inference and server-side tools. AWS still recommends bedrock-runtime for new apps, which now also serves the Responses and Chat Completions shapes, synchronously and with Guardrails and cross-Region profiles."

Calling LLMs on AWS

Converse API in depth

Every framework on AWS ends up calling Converse. Learn its request and response shape by heart and every framework becomes easier to debug.

Request shape

Converse request, annotated (// comments are notes, not valid JSON)JSON
{
  "modelId": "us.anthropic.claude-...",          // or an inference profile ARN
  "system":   [{"text": "You are a concise AWS data engineering assistant."}],
  "messages": [
    {"role": "user",      "content": [{"text": "What is a Glue DPU?"}]},
    {"role": "assistant", "content": [{"text": "A unit of compute: 4 vCPU and 16 GB."}]},
    {"role": "user",      "content": [{"text": "How many does G.2X use?"}]}
  ],
  "inferenceConfig": {"maxTokens": 512, "temperature": 0.2, "topP": 0.9},
  "toolConfig": {...},                           // optional: tools the model may call
  "guardrailConfig": {...},                      // optional: apply a Bedrock Guardrail
  "additionalModelRequestFields": {...}          // optional: provider-specific extras
}

Messages alternate user/assistant. Each message has a list of content blocks: text, image, document, toolUse, toolResult, reasoningContent, cachePoint.

A complete, reusable client

converse_basics.pyPython
import os, boto3
from botocore.config import Config

brt = boto3.client("bedrock-runtime", region_name=os.getenv("AWS_REGION", "us-east-1"),
                   config=Config(retries={"max_attempts": 8, "mode": "adaptive"}, read_timeout=300))
MODEL = os.environ["BEDROCK_MODEL_ID"]

def ask(question: str, history: list | None = None, system: str = "You are a helpful assistant.") -> tuple[str, list]:
    messages = (history or []) + [{"role": "user", "content": [{"text": question}]}]
    resp = brt.converse(modelId=MODEL, system=[{"text": system}], messages=messages,
                        inferenceConfig={"maxTokens": 1024, "temperature": 0.2})
    msg = resp["output"]["message"]                      # {"role": "assistant", "content": [...]}
    text = "".join(b.get("text", "") for b in msg["content"])
    u = resp["usage"]
    print(f"stop={resp['stopReason']} in={u['inputTokens']} out={u['outputTokens']} "
          f"latency={resp['metrics']['latencyMs']}ms")
    return text, messages + [msg]                        # keep history for the next turn

answer, hist = ask("Explain Iceberg snapshots in two sentences.")
answer, hist = ask("And how do I expire old ones?", hist)  # multi-turn: resend history
print(answer)

Streaming

Stream for any user-facing app so people see text in under a second.

ConverseStreamPython
stream = brt.converse_stream(
    modelId=MODEL,
    messages=[{"role": "user", "content": [{"text": "Write a haiku about S3 lifecycle rules."}]}],
)
for event in stream["stream"]:
    if "contentBlockDelta" in event:
        print(event["contentBlockDelta"]["delta"].get("text", ""), end="", flush=True)
    elif "messageStop" in event:
        print("\nstop:", event["messageStop"]["stopReason"])
    elif "metadata" in event:
        print("usage:", event["metadata"]["usage"])

Documents and images

Ask about a PDF directlyPython
pdf_bytes = open("runbook.pdf", "rb").read()
resp = brt.converse(modelId=MODEL, messages=[{"role": "user", "content": [
    {"document": {"format": "pdf", "name": "runbook", "source": {"bytes": pdf_bytes}}},
    {"text": "List every step that needs manual approval."},
]}])

Prompt caching

Put a cachePoint block after content that repeats across calls (long system prompt, tool definitions, a big document). Later calls with the same prefix read it from cache at a fraction of the input price and with lower latency. Check usage.cacheReadInputTokens to confirm it worked. Caching applies only to models that support it and above a minimum prefix length.

Cache a long system promptPython
resp = brt.converse(
    modelId=MODEL,
    system=[{"text": LONG_POLICY_DOC}, {"cachePoint": {"type": "default"}}],
    messages=[{"role": "user", "content": [{"text": "Is a 45-day refund allowed?"}]}],
)
print(resp["usage"].get("cacheReadInputTokens"), resp["usage"].get("cacheWriteInputTokens"))

Errors you'll hit

ErrorMeaningFix
ThrottlingExceptionTokens-per-minute or requests quota hitAdaptive retries with backoff, cross-Region profile, request quota increase, batch inference for offline work
AccessDeniedExceptionIAM missing or model access not enabledCheck role policy for the profile and the underlying model ARNs
ValidationExceptionBad message order, unsupported block, too many tokensMessages must alternate and start with user; check model's limits
ModelTimeoutExceptionLong generationStream, raise read_timeout, lower maxTokens

Calling LLMs on AWS · the most important lesson

Tool use: build the agent loop by hand

Every agent framework is a nicer version of the 40 lines below. Write it yourself once and you will understand LangGraph, Strands, CrewAI and AgentCore harness from the inside, and you'll be able to debug them.

How tool calling works

  1. You send the conversation plus a list of tool specs: name, description, JSON schema for inputs.
  2. The model replies either with text (done) or with one or more toolUse blocks and stopReason = "tool_use".
  3. Your code runs the tool. The model never executes anything itself.
  4. You append the assistant message and a user message containing toolResult blocks with matching toolUseIds.
  5. Call the model again. Repeat until it stops asking for tools, or you hit a limit.
Watch the loop run: "How many Glue jobs failed yesterday and why?"Live lab
    agent_loop.py: a complete tool-using agent with plain boto3Python
    import json, os, boto3

brt = boto3.client("bedrock-runtime", region_name="us-east-1")
MODEL = os.environ["BEDROCK_MODEL_ID"]

# 1) Real Python functions are the tools
def list_failed_jobs(date: str) -> list[dict]:
    """Stub: in real life, query the Glue API or your audit Iceberg table."""
    return [{"job": "qm_trusted", "run_id": "jr_1", "error": "OutOfMemory"},
            {"job": "marlo_raw_jp", "run_id": "jr_2", "error": "Timeout calling commercetools"}]

def get_job_log(run_id: str) -> str:
    return {"jr_1": "Container killed: exceeding memory limits", "jr_2": "HTTP 504 after 900s"}[run_id]

TOOLS = {"list_failed_jobs": list_failed_jobs, "get_job_log": get_job_log}

# 2) Describe them to the model (name, description, JSON schema)
TOOL_CONFIG = {"tools": [
    {"toolSpec": {"name": "list_failed_jobs",
                  "description": "List AWS Glue job runs that FAILED on a date (YYYY-MM-DD).",
                  "inputSchema": {"json": {"type": "object",
                                           "properties": {"date": {"type": "string"}},
                                           "required": ["date"]}}}},
    {"toolSpec": {"name": "get_job_log",
                  "description": "Get the error log excerpt for one Glue job run id.",
                  "inputSchema": {"json": {"type": "object",
                                           "properties": {"run_id": {"type": "string"}},
                                           "required": ["run_id"]}}}},
]}

def run_agent(question: str, max_turns: int = 8) -> str:
    messages = [{"role": "user", "content": [{"text": question}]}]
    for turn in range(max_turns):                                   # 3) a hard stop
        resp = brt.converse(modelId=MODEL, messages=messages, toolConfig=TOOL_CONFIG,
                            system=[{"text": "You are an on-call data engineer. Today is 2026-11-03."}],
                            inferenceConfig={"temperature": 0})
        msg = resp["output"]["message"]
        messages.append(msg)                                        # keep the assistant turn
        if resp["stopReason"] != "tool_use":                        # 4) done: final answer
            return "".join(b.get("text", "") for b in msg["content"])
        results = []
        for block in msg["content"]:                                # 5) run every requested tool
            if "toolUse" not in block:
                continue
            call = block["toolUse"]
            try:
                out = TOOLS[call["name"]](**call["input"])
                results.append({"toolResult": {"toolUseId": call["toolUseId"],
                                               "content": [{"json": {"result": out}}]}})
            except Exception as e:                                  # 6) errors go back to the model
                results.append({"toolResult": {"toolUseId": call["toolUseId"], "status": "error",
                                               "content": [{"text": f"{type(e).__name__}: {e}"}]}})
        messages.append({"role": "user", "content": results})       # 7) observations
    return "Stopped: turn limit reached."

print(run_agent("How many Glue jobs failed yesterday and why?"))

    What you just built

    That loop is ReAct with native tool calling: the model reasons (in its head or in thinking blocks), acts (tool call), observes (tool result), repeats. Everything frameworks add sits around it:

    Line in your loopWhat frameworks add there
    TOOLS and TOOL_CONFIG@tool decorators generate the schema from type hints and docstrings; MCP loads tools from servers
    messages listState objects, checkpointers (resume after crash), memory, summarisation when it gets long
    max_turnsRecursion limits, token budgets, cost limits, timeouts
    Tool executionParallel execution, retries, sandboxes, human approval before risky tools
    The while loop itselfGraphs with branches (LangGraph), multi-agent handoffs (Swarm), hooks/middleware at every step
    printTracing to LangSmith, Langfuse or CloudWatch via OpenTelemetry
    Interview: Does the LLM execute the tool?

    "No. The model only outputs a structured request: tool name and JSON arguments. The application validates and executes it, with its own credentials and permissions, then sends the result back. That's also where security lives: the model can ask for anything, so the tool layer must enforce least privilege, validation and approvals."

    Interview: How do you stop an agent looping forever?

    "Layered stop conditions: a maximum number of turns or tool calls, a token or dollar budget, a wall-clock timeout, and detection of repeated identical tool calls. When a limit hits, return a partial answer with what was done, and log it as an eval failure. In LangGraph that's the recursion limit plus middleware like ModelCallLimit; in Strands, loop limits and hooks; on AgentCore, session limits too."

    Calling LLMs on AWS New 2026

    Mantle and the OpenAI-compatible APIs

    Much GenAI code and many tools are written for the OpenAI SDK. Bedrock now speaks that dialect, so you can reuse that code against Bedrock models by changing two environment variables.

    Point the OpenAI SDK at BedrockTerminal
    pip install openai

# Option A: bedrock-runtime (AWS's recommendation for new apps)
export OPENAI_BASE_URL="https://bedrock-runtime.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/openai/v1"
# Option B: bedrock-mantle (needed for background jobs and server-side tools)
export OPENAI_BASE_URL="https://bedrock-mantle.us-east-1.api.aws/v1"

export OPENAI_API_KEY="<your Amazon Bedrock API key, not an OpenAI key>"
    List models, then call Chat CompletionsPython
    from openai import OpenAI
client = OpenAI()                       # reads OPENAI_BASE_URL and OPENAI_API_KEY

for m in client.models.list().data:     # what this endpoint serves
    print(m.id)

chat = client.chat.completions.create(
    model="openai.gpt-oss-120b",        # a model id from the list above
