    messages=[{"role": "system", "content": "Answer in one sentence."},
              {"role": "user", "content": "What is Apache Iceberg?"}],
)
print(chat.choices[0].message.content)

    Responses API: stateful conversations

    Multi-turn without resending historyPython
    r1 = client.responses.create(model="openai.gpt-oss-120b",
                             input=[{"role": "user", "content": "My table is partitioned by dt. Remember that."}])
r2 = client.responses.create(model="openai.gpt-oss-120b",            # model required on bedrock-runtime
                             previous_response_id=r1.id,             # server rebuilds the context
                             input=[{"role": "user", "content": "Write a query for last 7 days."}])
print(r2.output_text)

# Privacy: stored responses are kept 30 days by default. Opt out per call:
client.responses.create(model="openai.gpt-oss-120b", input="hi", store=False)
    Featurebedrock-runtime /openai/v1bedrock-mantle /v1
    Chat CompletionsYesYes
    Responses APIYes, always synchronousYes, plus background=true
    Server-side tools (web search, code interpreter)No (client-side tools only)Yes
    Guardrails on ResponsesNo; use Converse for guardrailed GPT callsSee docs per model
    Projects other than defaultDefault onlyYes
    QuotasShared with Converse/Invoke on that endpointMantle's own quotas
    Why this matters for frameworks

    Anything that accepts an OpenAI-compatible base URL (LangChain's ChatOpenAI, CrewAI via LiteLLM, LlamaIndex OpenAILike, many eval tools) can now use Bedrock models with no AWS-specific provider. LiteLLM and Portkey also list a bedrock_mantle provider.

    Calling LLMs on AWS

    Guardrails, cost and scale

    The features that turn a working call into a production service: content and PII controls, cost control, throughput and offline batch jobs.

    Bedrock Guardrails

    A Guardrail is a reusable policy you attach to a model call (or run standalone with ApplyGuardrail, even on non-Bedrock models). Policies: denied topics, content filters (hate, violence, sexual, misconduct, prompt attack), word filters, sensitive information (PII detect/mask, custom regex), contextual grounding checks (is the answer supported by the source and relevant to the query?) and automated reasoning checks.

    Use a guardrail with Converse, or standalonePython
    resp = brt.converse(modelId=MODEL, messages=msgs,
                    guardrailConfig={"guardrailIdentifier": "gr-abc123", "guardrailVersion": "1", "trace": "enabled"})
if resp["stopReason"] == "guardrail_intervened":
    print("Blocked or masked by guardrail")

# Standalone: check retrieved text or a tool result before the model sees it
chk = brt.apply_guardrail(guardrailIdentifier="gr-abc123", guardrailVersion="1", source="INPUT",
                          content=[{"text": {"text": retrieved_chunk}}])
print(chk["action"])   # NONE | GUARDRAIL_INTERVENED

    Controlling cost

    • Measure first: log input, output, cached and thinking tokens per request and per tenant.
    • Prompt caching for repeated prefixes (system prompts, tools, documents).
    • Model routing: small model for classification and routing, big model only where evals show it's needed.
    • Shorter context: retrieve fewer, better chunks; summarise old turns; drop stale tool outputs.
    • Batch inference (about half the on-demand price) for offline jobs: classify a million support tickets overnight from JSONL in S3.
    • Service tiers / provisioned throughput when you need guaranteed capacity.
    Batch inference job (offline, from S3 JSONL)Python
    bedrock = boto3.client("bedrock")
bedrock.create_model_invocation_job(
    jobName="ticket-classify-2026-11-03",
    roleArn="arn:aws:iam::123456789012:role/BedrockBatchRole",
    modelId=MODEL,
    inputDataConfig={"s3InputDataConfig": {"s3Uri": "s3://my-bucket/batch/in/"}},
    outputDataConfig={"s3OutputDataConfig": {"s3Uri": "s3://my-bucket/batch/out/"}},
)
    Data engineer angle

    Batch inference is a Step Functions-friendly, S3-in/S3-out job, exactly like the Glue jobs you already run. "LLM enrichment as a pipeline stage" (classify, extract entities, summarise, embed) is one of the most common Agentic AI Data Engineer tasks.

    Interview: Your GenAI app's bill doubled. How do you investigate?

    "Break cost down by model, feature and tenant from logged token usage, and look at the shape: did request count rise, or tokens per request? Tokens per request usually grows from longer histories, more retrieved chunks, verbose tool outputs left in context, agents taking more turns, or a prompt change that disabled caching. Then fix the biggest bucket: cache stable prefixes, cap and summarise history, trim tool outputs, route easy requests to a small model, set per-run budgets, and move offline work to batch."

    RAG

    Why RAG, and when not to

    Retrieval-Augmented Generation means: before the model answers, fetch the facts it needs from your data and put them in the prompt. It's how you give a model knowledge it was never trained on, keep that knowledge current, and show sources.

    RAG vs long context vs fine-tuning

    NeedBest choice
    Answer from a large, changing, private corpus with citations and per-user permissionsRAG
    Reason over one to a few documents the user just gave youLong context (put them in the prompt, maybe with caching)
    Change style, format, domain vocabulary, or make a small model good at one narrow taskFine-tuning
    Numbers from tables ("revenue by region last month")Text-to-SQL tool, not vector search
    Live state (job status, ticket status)API tool, not an index
    The most common RAG mistake

    Using vector search for structured questions. "How many orders did Japan have yesterday?" needs SQL against a table, not similar-looking paragraphs. Good agents route: documents → retrieval tool, tables → SQL tool, live systems → API tools.

    Where RAG fails (and which lesson fixes it)

    Bad parsing
    Tables flattened, headers lost, scanned PDFs as empty text → parsing and chunking.
    Wrong chunks
    Chunk too big (diluted) or too small (no context) → chunking.
    Vocabulary mismatch
    User says "OOM", docs say "out of memory" → hybrid search, query rewriting.
    Right chunk ranked 9th
    → reranking.
    Multi-hop questions
    → agentic RAG, query decomposition, GraphRAG.
    Stale or deleted documents still retrieved
    → incremental indexing pipeline.
    User sees a document they shouldn't
    → metadata filters with ACLs at query time.
    Answer not supported by the chunks
    → grounding prompts, citations, faithfulness evals.

    RAG

    Embeddings and vector search

    An embedding model turns text into a list of numbers (say 1,024) so that texts with similar meaning have vectors pointing in similar directions. Search becomes "find the nearest vectors".

    Cosine similarity, visualised in 2DLive lab

    Pick a query. Real embeddings have ~1,000 dimensions; these 2D points are a hand-made projection to show the idea.

    Similarity measures

    Cosine similarity
    cos θ = (a·b)/(|a||b|). 1 = same direction, 0 = unrelated. Default for text.
    Dot product
    Same as cosine when vectors are normalised (most embedding APIs can normalise for you).
    Euclidean (L2)
    Straight-line distance. Used by some indexes; equivalent ranking for normalised vectors.
    Embed with Titan Text Embeddings V2 and comparePython
    import json, boto3, numpy as np
brt = boto3.client("bedrock-runtime", region_name="us-east-1")

def embed(text: str, dims: int = 1024) -> np.ndarray:
    body = {"inputText": text, "dimensions": dims, "normalize": True}
    r = brt.invoke_model(modelId="amazon.titan-embed-text-v2:0", body=json.dumps(body))
    return np.array(json.loads(r["body"].read())["embedding"])

q = embed("Glue job ran out of memory")
docs = ["Executor OOM: increase worker type to G.2X", "How to rotate IAM access keys", "Spark container killed: memory overhead exceeded"]
vecs = np.stack([embed(d) for d in docs])
scores = vecs @ q                        # normalised, so dot product = cosine
for d, s in sorted(zip(docs, scores), key=lambda x: -x[1]):
    print(f"{s:.3f}  {d}")

    Approximate nearest neighbour (ANN)

    Comparing a query against 50 million vectors one by one is too slow. Vector indexes use ANN algorithms that trade a little recall for huge speed:

    HNSW
    A layered graph of neighbours; search hops from coarse to fine layers. Fast, high recall, memory-hungry. Default in OpenSearch, pgvector, most vector DBs. Tune m, ef_construction, ef_search.
    IVF
    Cluster vectors; search only the nearest clusters. Lower memory; tune number of lists and probes.
    Quantisation (PQ, binary, int8)
    Compress vectors to cut memory and cost, with a small accuracy loss; often re-score the top results with full vectors.

    Vector stores on and around AWS

    StoreGood forNotes
    Amazon S3 VectorsLarge, cost-sensitive corpora; infrequent queriesVector buckets and indexes inside S3; sub-second queries; very low storage cost; Bedrock Knowledge Bases integration
    OpenSearch Service / ServerlessHybrid search (BM25 + vectors), filters, high QPSMost flexible on AWS; you tune shards and HNSW
    Aurora PostgreSQL / RDS with pgvectorVectors next to relational data, SQL filters and joinsGreat when your metadata is already in Postgres; HNSW and IVFFlat indexes
    Bedrock Managed Knowledge BaseManaged end-to-end RAGNo vector store to run; built-in agentic multi-hop retrieval (GA June 2026)
    Pinecone, Qdrant, Weaviate, Milvus, MongoDB AtlasSpecialised or multi-cloudSupported as Knowledge Base or framework backends
    FAISS, ChromaLocal prototyping and testsIn-process; not a production service by themselves
    Interview: How do you choose an embedding model and dimension?

    "Run a retrieval eval on my own data: 50 to 200 real questions labelled with the chunks that answer them, and compare recall@k and MRR across two or three models. Check language coverage, max input length and cost. Bigger dimensions can help slightly but cost storage and latency; many models support smaller output dimensions with little loss, so I'd test 1024 vs 512. And I keep the model ID with every vector, because changing models means re-embedding everything; you can't mix vectors from different models."

    RAG · fundamentals

    Vector indexes and choosing a store on AWS

    An embedding search is "find the nearest vectors to this one". Doing that exactly over millions of vectors is too slow, so vector databases use approximate indexes. Knowing how HNSW and IVF work, and which AWS store fits which workload, is a standard interview topic.

    Exact vs approximate search

    Exact (flat, brute force)
    Compare the query with every vector. Perfect recall, cost grows linearly. Fine up to roughly a few hundred thousand vectors, and the right baseline for measuring other indexes.
    Approximate nearest neighbour (ANN)
    Build an index that looks at a small part of the data. You trade a little recall (say 95–99% of the true neighbours) for a 10–1000× speed-up.

    HNSW: the default index

    Hierarchical Navigable Small World builds a layered graph. Each vector is a node linked to its nearest neighbours. Upper layers are sparse "motorways", the bottom layer contains every vector. A search enters at the top, greedily hops toward the query, drops a layer, and repeats.

    ParameterMeaningRaise it to getCost
    MLinks per nodeBetter recallMore memory, slower build
    ef_constructionCandidates considered while buildingBetter graph qualitySlower indexing
    ef_searchCandidates considered per queryBetter recall at query timeHigher latency (tunable per query)

    The other names you'll hear

    IVF (inverted file)
    Cluster vectors with k-means into nlist buckets; at query time search only the nprobe nearest buckets. Less memory than HNSW, faster to build, lower recall at the same speed. Needs training data and re-clustering as data drifts.
    PQ (product quantisation)
    Compress each vector into a few bytes by splitting it into sub-vectors and storing the nearest codebook entry for each. Big memory savings, some recall loss. Often combined: IVF-PQ.
    Scalar / binary quantisation
    Store each dimension as 8 bits or 1 bit instead of a 32-bit float. 4–32× smaller; rescore the top candidates with full vectors to recover accuracy.
    Disk-based ANN
    Indexes designed to keep most data on SSD or object storage instead of RAM. Cheaper at very large scale, slower per query. S3 Vectors sits in this cost-first corner.
    Pre-filter vs post-filter
    Applying a metadata filter before the vector search guarantees k results but needs index support; filtering after is simple but may return fewer than k when the filter is selective.

    Distance metrics

    Cosine similarity compares direction and ignores length; dot product equals cosine when vectors are normalised to length 1 (most embedding APIs can normalise for you); Euclidean (L2) measures straight-line distance. Use the metric the embedding model was trained with, which is nearly always cosine or dot product. Index and query must use the same metric.

    Choosing a vector store on AWS

    StoreGood atWatch out forPick it when
    Aurora / RDS PostgreSQL + pgvectorVectors next to relational data, SQL filters and joins, transactions, full-text search for hybrid in one queryYou tune and scale the database; very large indexes need memoryYou already run Postgres, up to tens of millions of vectors, and want one system
    OpenSearch (Serverless or managed)Native hybrid search (BM25 + k-NN), rich filtering, large scale, several ANN enginesCost floor for always-on capacity; more to operateSearch-heavy products, high QPS, hybrid is a must
    S3 VectorsVery low storage cost, no servers, huge volumesHigher latency and lower QPS than in-memory indexes; fewer query featuresLarge, infrequently queried corpora; archives; cost-first agents
    Bedrock Knowledge BasesManaged ingestion, chunking, embedding, sync and retrieval API on top of the stores aboveLess control over each stepStandard document RAG you don't want to hand-build
    Neptune AnalyticsGraph + vector for GraphRAGOnly worth it when relationships matterMulti-hop questions across linked entities
    MemoryDB / ElastiCache (Valkey)Single-digit-millisecond vector search in memoryRAM costSemantic caches and real-time lookups
    How to answer "which vector DB?"

    Don't name a favourite. Ask four things: how many vectors, what query rate and latency, what filters and hybrid needs, and what the team already runs. Then pick the simplest store that meets them, and say you'd measure recall@k against exact search before trusting any ANN setting.

    Interview: Explain HNSW and how you'd tune it.

    "HNSW is a multi-layer proximity graph. Higher layers have few nodes with long-range links, the bottom layer has every vector. Search starts at the top, greedily moves to the neighbour closest to the query, then descends and refines. M controls links per node, ef_construction controls build quality, and ef_search controls how many candidates each query explores. To tune it I build a ground-truth set with exact search on a sample, then raise ef_search until recall@k is around 95–99% and check p95 latency. If recall is still poor I rebuild with higher M or ef_construction. Memory is the main cost, so at large scale I'd add quantisation and rescoring."

    Interview: Your filtered vector search returns 2 results when you asked for 10. Why?

    "That's post-filtering: the index returned its top k nearest vectors, then the metadata filter removed most of them. Fixes are to use a store or index that filters during the search, to over-fetch and then filter, or to partition the index by the filter key, for example one index or namespace per tenant. In pgvector I'd also check the query plan, because with a selective WHERE clause the planner may be better off using the B-tree index and exact distance."

    RAG

    Parsing and chunking

    Retrieval quality is mostly decided before any vector is created: how well you extract text from source files and how you cut it into chunks. Try the chunker below with your own text.

    Chunking playgroundLive lab

    Chunking strategies

    StrategyHowWhen
    Fixed size + overlapN tokens with 10–20% overlapBaseline; uniform text
    Recursive / structure-awareSplit on headings, then paragraphs, then sentences until under the sizeMost documents (LangChain's RecursiveCharacterTextSplitter, Markdown/HTML splitters)
    SemanticSplit where embedding similarity between sentences dropsLong, topic-shifting text
    Hierarchical (parent-child)Search small child chunks; return their larger parent sectionNeed precise matching and enough context to answer
    Contextual chunksPrepend an LLM-written sentence situating each chunk in its document before embeddingWhen chunks are ambiguous out of context ("it", "this table")
    Late chunkingEmbed the whole document with a long-context embedder, then pool token vectors per chunkKeeps document context in each chunk vector; needs supporting models
    Code / tablesSplit by function or class; keep table rows with headers; convert tables to MarkdownRepos, specs, spreadsheets

    Metadata is half the value

    Store with every chunk: doc_id, source_uri, title, section path, updated_at, version, content hash, acl (groups allowed), doc_type, region/business unit, embedding model ID. You'll filter on it ("only Japan docs this user can see, updated this year"), show it as citations, and use it to update and delete.

    Structure-aware chunks with metadata (LangChain splitters)Python
    from langchain_text_splitters import MarkdownHeaderTextSplitter, RecursiveCharacterTextSplitter
import hashlib

md = open("runbook.md").read()
sections = MarkdownHeaderTextSplitter(headers_to_split_on=[("#", "h1"), ("##", "h2")]).split_text(md)
splitter = RecursiveCharacterTextSplitter(chunk_size=1200, chunk_overlap=150)   # characters

chunks = []
for sec in sections:
    for i, piece in enumerate(splitter.split_text(sec.page_content)):
        heading = " > ".join(v for v in sec.metadata.values())
        chunks.append({
            "text": f"{heading}\n\n{piece}",            # heading text helps both embedding and the LLM
            "metadata": {**sec.metadata, "doc_id": "runbook", "chunk": i,
                         "sha": hashlib.sha256(piece.encode()).hexdigest()[:16],
                         "acl": ["data-eng"], "source_uri": "s3://kb/runbook.md"},
        })
print(len(chunks), chunks[0]["text"][:120])

    Parsing tools

    Amazon Textract (scanned PDFs, forms, tables), Bedrock Data Automation and Knowledge Bases' advanced parsing (foundation-model parsing of complex PDFs), LlamaParse, Unstructured, Docling, PyMuPDF for born-digital PDFs. For images and diagrams in documents, multimodal models can produce text descriptions to index.

    Interview: What chunk size do you use?

    "There's no universal number; I start around 300–800 tokens with 10–15% overlap, split on document structure, and then tune using a retrieval eval. Smaller chunks give precise matches but lose context; larger ones carry context but dilute the embedding and waste prompt tokens. If I need both, I use parent-child: search small chunks, return the parent section. I also add the document title and heading path to every chunk."

    RAG · hands-on

    Build a RAG app from scratch

    A complete RAG system in one file with no framework: Titan embeddings, an in-memory NumPy index, Converse for the answer, and citations. Once this makes sense, every RAG framework is just packaging.

    mini_rag.pyPython
    import json, os, re, boto3, numpy as np

brt = boto3.client("bedrock-runtime", region_name="us-east-1")
LLM = os.environ["BEDROCK_MODEL_ID"]
EMB = "amazon.titan-embed-text-v2:0"

def embed(texts: list[str]) -> np.ndarray:
    out = []
    for t in texts:   # Titan V2 takes one text per call; use batch inference for big corpora
        r = brt.invoke_model(modelId=EMB, body=json.dumps({"inputText": t, "normalize": True}))
        out.append(json.loads(r["body"].read())["embedding"])
    return np.array(out, dtype=np.float32)

# ---------- offline: index ----------
DOCS = {
  "glue-oom.md": "If a Glue job fails with OutOfMemory, switch from G.1X to G.2X workers, "
                 "reduce shuffle partitions skew with AQE, and avoid collect() on large DataFrames.",
  "incremental.md": "Incremental loads read the last ingestion timestamp from DynamoDB. "
                    "If the timestamp is missing the job performs a full load.",
  "iceberg.md": "Run expire_snapshots and rewrite_data_files weekly to keep Iceberg tables fast.",
}
def chunk(text: str, size: int = 300) -> list[str]:
    sents, cur, out = re.split(r"(?<=[.!?])\s+", text), "", []
    for s in sents:
        if len(cur) + len(s) > size and cur:
            out.append(cur.strip()); cur = ""
        cur += s + " "
    return out + ([cur.strip()] if cur.strip() else [])

chunks = [{"id": f"{name}#{i}", "text": c} for name, t in DOCS.items() for i, c in enumerate(chunk(t))]
INDEX = embed([c["text"] for c in chunks])              # (n_chunks, 1024)

# ---------- online: retrieve + generate ----------
def retrieve(question: str, k: int = 3) -> list[dict]:
    scores = INDEX @ embed([question])[0]
    top = np.argsort(-scores)[:k]
    return [{**chunks[i], "score": float(scores[i])} for i in top]

def answer(question: str) -> str:
    hits = retrieve(question)
    context = "\n".join(f'<source id="{h["id"]}">{h["text"]}</source>' for h in hits)
    system = ("Answer only from the sources. Cite source ids in square brackets after each claim. "
              "If the sources don't contain the answer, say you don't know.")
    resp = brt.converse(modelId=LLM, system=[{"text": system}],
                        messages=[{"role": "user", "content": [{"text": f"{context}\n\nQuestion: {question}"}]}],
                        inferenceConfig={"temperature": 0})
    return resp["output"]["message"]["content"][0]["text"]

print(answer("My Glue job keeps dying with OOM, what should I try?"))
print(answer("What is the capital of France?"))   # should say it doesn't know
    Typical outputOutput
    Switch the job's workers from G.1X to G.2X, let AQE handle shuffle skew, and avoid
calling collect() on large DataFrames [glue-oom.md#0].

I don't know; the sources don't cover that.

    Practice: make it better

    1. Add metadata filtering: only search chunks whose doc_type matches.
    2. Add BM25 keyword search (rank_bm25 package) and fuse with RRF (next lesson).
    3. Write 10 questions with the chunk id that should be retrieved; compute recall@3.
    4. Swap the NumPy index for pgvector or S3 Vectors.
    Solution sketch for 3 (recall@k)
    Retrieval eval in 10 linesPython
    gold = [("Glue OOM fix?", "glue-oom.md#0"), ("What if the DynamoDB timestamp is gone?", "incremental.md#0")]
hits = 0
for q, expected in gold:
    ids = [h["id"] for h in retrieve(q, k=3)]
    hits += expected in ids
print(f"recall@3 = {hits/len(gold):.2f}")

    RAG

    Advanced retrieval

    Plain top-k vector search is the baseline. These techniques fix its known failure modes, and they're the core of RAG deep-dive interviews.

    Hybrid search: keywords + vectors

    Vectors match meaning but miss exact terms (error codes, table names like qm_trusted_events, product SKUs). BM25 keyword search matches exact terms but misses paraphrases. Run both and fuse the rankings. The simplest robust fusion is Reciprocal Rank Fusion: each list contributes 1/(k + rank) per document, with k = 60 by convention.

    Reciprocal Rank Fusion calculatorLive lab

    BM25 ranking

    Vector ranking

    Reranking

    Retrieve generously (top 30–50 by hybrid search), then a cross-encoder reranker reads the query and each chunk together and scores relevance much more accurately than vector similarity. Keep the top 3–8. On AWS: Cohere Rerank or Amazon Rerank via Bedrock's Rerank API, also built into Knowledge Bases retrieval.

    Rerank with Bedrock (bedrock-agent-runtime)Python
    bar = boto3.client("bedrock-agent-runtime", region_name="us-west-2")
resp = bar.rerank(
    queries=[{"type": "TEXT", "textQuery": {"text": "Glue job out of memory"}}],
    sources=[{"type": "INLINE", "inlineDocumentSource": {"type": "TEXT", "textDocument": {"text": t}}}
             for t in candidate_texts],
    rerankingConfiguration={"type": "BEDROCK_RERANKING_MODEL",
        "bedrockRerankingConfiguration": {"numberOfResults": 5,
            "modelConfiguration": {"modelArn": "arn:aws:bedrock:us-west-2::foundation-model/amazon.rerank-v1:0"}}},
)
for r in resp["results"]:
    print(r["index"], round(r["relevanceScore"], 3))

    Query-side techniques

    Query rewriting
    Turn "and for Italy?" into a standalone query using chat history.
    Multi-query
    Generate 3–5 phrasings, retrieve for each, fuse with RRF.
    Decomposition
    Split "compare Japan and Italy failures last month" into sub-questions.
    HyDE
    Ask the LLM to write a hypothetical answer, embed that, search with it. Helps when questions are short and documents are long.
    Metadata extraction
    LLM extracts filters ("region=JP, year=2026") from the question and applies them to the search.
    Step-back
    Ask a more general question first to retrieve background.

    Corpus-side and loop techniques

    GraphRAG
    Extract entities and relations into a graph (Neptune, Neo4j), summarise communities. Good for "what themes connect all these incidents?" and multi-hop entity questions. Expensive to build; use when vector RAG measurably fails.
    Self-RAG / Corrective RAG
    Grade retrieved chunks for relevance; if poor, rewrite the query or search elsewhere; check the answer is grounded. Easy to express as a LangGraph graph.
    Context compression
    Extract only the relevant sentences from each chunk before sending to the LLM.
    Ordering
    Put the strongest evidence first (and/or last); avoid burying it in the middle.
    Interview: Your RAG answers are wrong 30% of the time. How do you debug?

    "First separate retrieval failures from generation failures. For a sample of wrong answers, check whether the right chunk was in the top-k. If not, it's retrieval: look at parsing, chunking, missing hybrid search, filters, or the right chunk being ranked low, which reranking fixes. If the chunk was there but the answer was still wrong, it's generation: prompt doesn't force grounding, too much noisy context, or the model is too small. I'd fix by measuring recall@k and faithfulness separately, changing one thing at a time against a labelled eval set."

    RAG · project

    Project: production RAG with pgvector, hybrid search and reranking

    The from-scratch lesson used NumPy. This is the version you would actually deploy and talk about in interviews: Postgres with pgvector, idempotent ingestion, hybrid search fused in one SQL query, a reranker, grounded answers with citations, and a refusal path.

    1. Schema

    schema.sql (Aurora or RDS PostgreSQL 15+ with the pgvector extension)SQL
    CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS vector;

CREATE TABLE chunks (
  id          text PRIMARY KEY,                 -- sha256 of doc_uri + chunk text: makes upserts idempotent
  doc_uri     text NOT NULL,                    -- s3://bucket/key
  doc_type    text NOT NULL,                    -- runbook | design | postmortem ...
  team        text NOT NULL,                    -- for access control filters
  chunk_no    int  NOT NULL,
  text        text NOT NULL,
  embedding   vector(1024) NOT NULL,            -- Titan Text Embeddings V2 default size
  embed_model text NOT NULL,                    -- never mix models in one index
  tsv         tsvector GENERATED ALWAYS AS (to_tsvector('english', text)) STORED,
  updated_at  timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now()
);
CREATE INDEX chunks_hnsw ON chunks USING hnsw (embedding vector_cosine_ops) WITH (m = 16, ef_construction = 64);
CREATE INDEX chunks_tsv  ON chunks USING gin (tsv);
CREATE INDEX chunks_doc  ON chunks (doc_uri);

    2. Ingest

    ingest.pyPython
    import hashlib, json, os, re, boto3, psycopg
import numpy as np
from pgvector.psycopg import register_vector          # pip install "psycopg[binary]" pgvector

brt = boto3.client("bedrock-runtime", region_name="us-east-1")
EMB = "amazon.titan-embed-text-v2:0"

def embed(text: str) -> list[float]:
    r = brt.invoke_model(modelId=EMB, body=json.dumps({"inputText": text, "normalize": True}))
    return json.loads(r["body"].read())["embedding"]

def chunk(text: str, size: int = 1200, overlap: int = 150) -> list[str]:
    """Paragraph-aware chunks of about `size` characters with a small overlap."""
    paras, out, cur = [p.strip() for p in re.split(r"\n\s*\n", text) if p.strip()], [], ""
    for p in paras:
        if cur and len(cur) + len(p) > size:
            out.append(cur)
            cur = cur[-overlap:]                       # carry the tail forward for context
        cur = (cur + "\n\n" + p).strip()
    return out + ([cur] if cur else [])

def ingest(conn, doc_uri: str, text: str, doc_type: str, team: str) -> int:
    rows = []
    for i, c in enumerate(chunk(text)):
        cid = hashlib.sha256(f"{doc_uri}\n{c}".encode()).hexdigest()
        rows.append((cid, doc_uri, doc_type, team, i, c))
    with conn.cursor() as cur:
        cur.execute("SELECT id FROM chunks WHERE doc_uri = %s", (doc_uri,))
        existing = {r[0] for r in cur.fetchall()}
        keep = {r[0] for r in rows}
        new = [r for r in rows if r[0] not in existing]           # embed only what changed
        for cid, uri, dt, tm, i, c in new:
            cur.execute("""INSERT INTO chunks (id, doc_uri, doc_type, team, chunk_no, text, embedding, embed_model)
                           VALUES (%s,%s,%s,%s,%s,%s,%s,%s) ON CONFLICT (id) DO NOTHING""",
                        (cid, uri, dt, tm, i, c, np.array(embed(c), dtype=np.float32), EMB))
        stale = list(existing - keep)                             # chunks that no longer exist in the doc
        if stale:
            cur.execute("DELETE FROM chunks WHERE id = ANY(%s)", (stale,))
    conn.commit()
    return len(new)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    with psycopg.connect(os.environ["PG_DSN"]) as conn:
        register_vector(conn)
        n = ingest(conn, "s3://runbooks/glue-oom.md", open("glue-oom.md").read(), "runbook", "data-platform")
        print("embedded", n, "new chunks")           # run it twice: the second run prints 0

    3. Retrieve, rerank, answer

    ask.pyPython
    import os, boto3, psycopg
import numpy as np
from pgvector.psycopg import register_vector
from ingest import embed

brt = boto3.client("bedrock-runtime", region_name="us-east-1")
bar = boto3.client("bedrock-agent-runtime", region_name="us-west-2")
RERANK_ARN = "arn:aws:bedrock:us-west-2::foundation-model/amazon.rerank-v1:0"

HYBRID = """
WITH sem AS (
  SELECT id, ROW_NUMBER() OVER (ORDER BY embedding <=> %(qv)s) AS r
  FROM chunks WHERE team = ANY(%(teams)s)
  ORDER BY embedding <=> %(qv)s LIMIT 40),
kw AS (
  SELECT c.id, ROW_NUMBER() OVER (ORDER BY ts_rank_cd(c.tsv, q) DESC) AS r
  FROM chunks c, websearch_to_tsquery('english', %(q)s) q
  WHERE c.tsv @@ q AND c.team = ANY(%(teams)s)
  ORDER BY ts_rank_cd(c.tsv, q) DESC LIMIT 40)
SELECT c.id, c.doc_uri, c.chunk_no, c.text,
       COALESCE(1.0 / (60 + sem.r), 0) + COALESCE(1.0 / (60 + kw.r), 0) AS rrf
FROM sem FULL OUTER JOIN kw USING (id) JOIN chunks c USING (id)
ORDER BY rrf DESC LIMIT 20;
"""

def retrieve(conn, question: str, teams: list[str]) -> list[dict]:
    with conn.cursor() as cur:
        cur.execute("SET LOCAL hnsw.ef_search = 100")
        cur.execute(HYBRID, {"qv": np.array(embed(question), dtype=np.float32), "q": question, "teams": teams})
        cols = [d.name for d in cur.description]
        return [dict(zip(cols, row)) for row in cur.fetchall()]

def rerank(question: str, hits: list[dict], k: int = 5) -> list[dict]:
    if not hits:
        return []
    resp = bar.rerank(
        queries=[{"type": "TEXT", "textQuery": {"text": question}}],
        sources=[{"type": "INLINE", "inlineDocumentSource": {"type": "TEXT", "textDocument": {"text": h["text"]}}}
                 for h in hits],
        rerankingConfiguration={"type": "BEDROCK_RERANKING_MODEL", "bedrockRerankingConfiguration": {
            "numberOfResults": min(k, len(hits)), "modelConfiguration": {"modelArn": RERANK_ARN}}})
    return [{**hits[r["index"]], "rel": r["relevanceScore"]} for r in resp["results"]]

SYSTEM = ("You answer questions for data engineers using only the numbered sources. "
          "After every claim cite the source number like [2]. "
          "If the sources do not contain the answer, reply exactly: I don't know based on the indexed documents.")

def ask(conn, question: str, teams: list[str]) -> dict:
    top = rerank(question, retrieve(conn, question, teams))
    if not top or top[0]["rel"] < 0.2:                       # nothing relevant: don't call the LLM at all
        return {"answer": "I don't know based on the indexed documents.", "sources": []}
    context = "\n\n".join(f'<source n="{i+1}" uri="{h["doc_uri"]}">\n{h["text"]}\n</source>' for i, h in enumerate(top))
    resp = brt.converse(
        modelId=os.environ["BEDROCK_MODEL_ID"], system=[{"text": SYSTEM}],
        messages=[{"role": "user", "content": [{"text": f"{context}\n\nQuestion: {question}"}]}],
        inferenceConfig={"temperature": 0, "maxTokens": 700})
    return {"answer": resp["output"]["message"]["content"][0]["text"],
            "sources": [{"n": i + 1, "uri": h["doc_uri"], "chunk": h["chunk_no"]} for i, h in enumerate(top)],
            "usage": resp["usage"]}

if __name__ == "__main__":
    with psycopg.connect(os.environ["PG_DSN"]) as conn:
        register_vector(conn)
        print(ask(conn, "Glue job fails with java.lang.OutOfMemoryError, what do I change?", ["data-platform"]))

    Design decisions to say out loud

    DecisionWhy
    Chunk ID = hash of URI + textRe-running ingestion embeds only changed chunks and removes deleted ones. Cheap and idempotent, the same idea as an incremental load.
    embed_model columnVectors from different models are not comparable. When you change model you re-embed into a new table and switch over.
    Hybrid with RRFVectors miss exact tokens like OutOfMemoryError or a job name; keyword search misses paraphrases. Reciprocal rank fusion merges the two lists by rank, so you never have to put the two scores on one scale.
    Retrieve 40, fuse to 20, rerank to 5Recall first (wide net), precision second (cross-encoder reads question and chunk together), small context last (cheaper, less distraction).
    team = ANY(...) inside the SQLAccess control is enforced in the retrieval query with the caller's entitlements, never by asking the model to behave.
    Relevance threshold before generationIf nothing relevant comes back, refusing is cheaper and safer than letting the model improvise. Calibrate the threshold on your eval set.

    Make it yours

    1. Wrap ingest in a Lambda triggered by S3 events (or a Glue job for backfills) and add a dead-letter queue.
    2. Add contextual chunking: before embedding, prepend one sentence that says where the chunk sits in its document.
    3. Build a 50-question eval set with the expected doc_uri; report recall@5 before and after hybrid and reranking. Put those numbers in your README.
    4. Expose ask as a tool to the LangGraph agent in the Frameworks chapter.
    Interview: Walk me through a RAG system you'd build for internal runbooks.

    "Ingestion is an event-driven pipeline: S3 upload triggers parsing, structure-aware chunking of about 300 tokens with a little overlap, and embedding. Each chunk gets an ID that's a hash of document and content, so re-runs only embed what changed and deletes are propagated. I store vector, full text and metadata like team and doc type in Postgres with pgvector, with HNSW and GIN indexes. At query time I run vector and keyword search with the caller's team filter, fuse with reciprocal rank fusion, rerank the top 20 with a cross-encoder down to 5, and generate with a prompt that requires citations and allows 'I don't know'. I log the trace, token usage and retrieved IDs. Quality is tracked with a labelled set: recall@5 for retrieval, faithfulness and correctness for answers, and it runs in CI when chunking, prompt or model changes."

    RAG · your strongest topic

    Indexing pipelines on AWS

    In production, RAG quality depends on a reliable ingestion pipeline: new documents appear within minutes, edits replace old chunks, deletions vanish, and permissions follow the data. This is plain data engineering, and it's where an Agentic AI Data Engineer earns their title.

    Rules for a production index

    1. Deterministic chunk IDs (doc_id#chunk_no or hash) so re-processing upserts instead of duplicating.
    2. Skip unchanged documents by comparing content hash with the manifest; re-embedding costs money.
    3. Handle deletes: on delete events, remove all chunk IDs for that doc_id. Stale chunks are a compliance problem, not just quality.
    4. Version the embedding model: store model ID; a model change means a full re-index into a new index, then switch an alias.
    5. Carry ACLs as metadata and filter at query time with the caller's groups. Never rely on the prompt to hide documents.
    6. Mask PII before embedding if users without PII access can query (Comprehend, Guardrails, your existing masking rules).
    7. Monitor: documents in vs chunks out, embedding failures, index lag, retrieval eval score per release.

    Bedrock Knowledge Bases: managed RAG

    Knowledge Bases run the pipeline for you: connectors (S3, Confluence, SharePoint, Salesforce, web crawler and more), parsing, chunking strategies (fixed, hierarchical, semantic, or your own Lambda), embedding, storage (S3 Vectors, OpenSearch Serverless, Aurora pgvector, Pinecone, others) and sync jobs. The Managed Knowledge Base option (GA June 2026) also manages vector storage and adds agentic multi-hop retrieval.

    Query a Knowledge Base: retrieve only, or retrieve-and-generatePython
    bar = boto3.client("bedrock-agent-runtime", region_name="us-east-1")

# 1) Retrieve chunks (you control the prompt and model)
r = bar.retrieve(
    knowledgeBaseId="KB12345678",
    retrievalQuery={"text": "How do we handle backdated files?"},
    retrievalConfiguration={"vectorSearchConfiguration": {
        "numberOfResults": 8,
        "overrideSearchType": "HYBRID",                       # where the store supports it
        "filter": {"equals": {"key": "team", "value": "data-eng"}},
    }},
)
for res in r["retrievalResults"]:
    print(round(res["score"], 3), res["location"], res["content"]["text"][:80])

# 2) Or let Bedrock do retrieval + generation + citations in one call
rg = bar.retrieve_and_generate(
    input={"text": "How do we handle backdated files?"},
    retrieveAndGenerateConfiguration={"type": "KNOWLEDGE_BASE", "knowledgeBaseConfiguration": {
        "knowledgeBaseId": "KB12345678",
        "modelArn": "arn:aws:bedrock:us-east-1:123456789012:inference-profile/" + os.environ["BEDROCK_MODEL_ID"]}},
)
print(rg["output"]["text"]); print(rg["citations"][0]["retrievedReferences"][0]["location"])
    Trigger a sync after your pipeline drops new files in S3Python
    ba = boto3.client("bedrock-agent")
job = ba.start_ingestion_job(knowledgeBaseId="KB12345678", dataSourceId="DS12345678")
print(job["ingestionJob"]["status"])   # STARTING → IN_PROGRESS → COMPLETE (incremental: only changed files)
    Managed KB or your own pipeline?

    Choose Knowledge Bases when standard connectors and chunking fit and you want speed. Build your own when you need custom parsing, row-level ACL logic, joins with lakehouse metadata, exotic sources, or full control of the index. Many teams start managed and move hot paths to custom. Saying this trade-off out loud is a strong interview signal.

    Interview: Design document ingestion for 2 million Confluence pages with permissions.

    "Initial backfill as a parallel batch: export pages to S3, Glue or Step Functions Distributed Map to parse and chunk, Bedrock batch or concurrent calls to embed, bulk load into OpenSearch with chunk IDs. Then incremental: Confluence webhooks or a scheduled delta crawl push change events to SQS; Lambdas re-process only changed pages by hash, delete chunks for removed pages. Each chunk stores space, page ID, version and allowed groups; at query time I resolve the user's groups from the identity provider and apply them as a filter. A manifest table tracks status and lets me reconcile counts nightly, and a retrieval eval set runs after each big change."

    RAG Trending

    Agentic RAG: retrieval as a tool

    In 2026 the default design is not "always retrieve, then answer". It is an agent that has retrieval tools and decides when to search, what to search, which index to use, and whether the results are good enough.

    Classic RAG

    • Always retrieves once
    • Uses the raw question
    • One index
    • Can't notice bad results
    • Cheap and predictable

    Agentic RAG

    • Retrieves zero to many times
    • Rewrites and decomposes queries
    • Routes between indexes, SQL and APIs
    • Grades results, retries, asks for clarification
    • More capable; needs limits and evals
    Agentic RAG with Strands: two retrieval tools and a SQL toolPython
    import os, boto3
from strands import Agent, tool
from strands.models import BedrockModel

bar = boto3.client("bedrock-agent-runtime", region_name="us-east-1")

def _kb(kb_id: str, query: str, k: int = 5) -> list[dict]:
    r = bar.retrieve(knowledgeBaseId=kb_id, retrievalQuery={"text": query},
                     retrievalConfiguration={"vectorSearchConfiguration": {"numberOfResults": k}})
    return [{"text": x["content"]["text"], "source": str(x["location"]), "score": x["score"]}
            for x in r["retrievalResults"]]

@tool
def search_runbooks(query: str) -> list[dict]:
    """Search data-platform runbooks and design docs. Use for 'how do we...' and 'why does...' questions."""
    return _kb("KBRUNBOOKS1", query)

@tool
def search_tickets(query: str) -> list[dict]:
    """Search past Jira incident tickets. Use to find similar past failures and their fixes."""
    return _kb("KBTICKETS01", query)

@tool
def run_athena_sql(sql: str) -> str:
    """Run a READ-ONLY Athena SQL query on the analytics catalog. Use for counts, dates, numbers."""
    if not sql.lstrip().lower().startswith(("select", "with")):
        return "Rejected: only SELECT/WITH queries are allowed."
    return "...rows..."   # call Athena here (start_query_execution + poll), cap rows returned

agent = Agent(
    model=BedrockModel(model_id=os.environ["BEDROCK_MODEL_ID"], temperature=0),
    tools=[search_runbooks, search_tickets, run_athena_sql],
    system_prompt=("Answer data-platform questions. Pick the right tool: runbooks for procedures, "
                   "tickets for past incidents, SQL for numbers. If results look irrelevant, rewrite the "
                   "query and search again (max 3 searches). Cite sources. Say when you don't know."),
)
result = agent("Did the Marlo Japan raw job fail more often this month than last, and what fixed it before?")
print(result)
    Tool descriptions are retrieval routing

    In agentic RAG, the tool docstring is your router. Say what each index contains and when to use it. Vague descriptions ("search documents") cause wrong-index searches, which your trajectory evals will show.

    Interview: When would you NOT use agentic RAG?

    "When questions are simple and uniform, latency budgets are tight, or behaviour must be fully predictable, for example a customer FAQ bot. A fixed retrieve-rerank-generate pipeline is cheaper, faster and easier to evaluate. I'd add agentic behaviour only where evals show multi-hop or multi-source questions failing, and I'd cap searches per question."

    RAG · production

    Debugging RAG: the failure playbook

    "The RAG bot gave a wrong answer" is the most common production ticket and a favourite interview scenario. The skill is to locate which stage failed before changing anything. Work from left to right.

    SymptomHow to confirmLikely causeFix
    Right document exists but never retrievedSearch the index for the exact sentence; compute recall@k on a labelled setParsing dropped it (tables, PDFs, scanned pages); chunk split the fact in two; wrong metadata filterBetter parser, structure-aware chunks, overlap, fix filters
    Exact codes, IDs, table names are missedQuery with the literal tokenPure vector searchHybrid search with BM25, keyword boost on identifiers
    Follow-up questions fail ("and for Italy?")Log the query actually sent to retrievalRaw follow-up was embedded without historyRewrite to a standalone query first
    Right chunk at rank 15, not in the top 5Inspect ranks before and after rerankWeak rankingAdd or fix the reranker; retrieve wider then rerank
    Right chunk retrieved, answer ignores itTrace shows the chunk in the promptToo many chunks, relevant one buried mid-prompt, conflicting sourcesFewer chunks, best ones first and last, tell the model how to handle conflicts
    Answer adds facts not in sourcesFaithfulness score; claim-by-claim checkPrompt allows outside knowledge; temperature; no refusal path"Only from sources" + citations + explicit "I don't know"; contextual grounding guardrail
    Answers were right last month, wrong nowCompare index timestamps with source timestampsStale index; failed sync; duplicates of old versionsIncremental re-index, delete propagation, freshness monitoring
    Same question, different answersReplay with fixed inputsANN non-determinism at ties, temperature, changing top-kTemperature 0, stable tie-breaks, cache
    Works in English, fails in Hindi or TeluguPer-language eval sliceEmbedding model or full-text config is English-onlyMultilingual embeddings; language-specific text search config
    User sees content from another teamAudit retrieved IDs against entitlementsFilter applied after retrieval or not at allFilter in the query with caller identity; test it
    Slow answersPer-stage timings in the traceUsually generation length; sometimes rerank or cold indexStream, shorten output, smaller model, cache, tune ef_search

    A 20-minute triage routine

    1. Reproduce with the exact question and user identity. Pull the trace.
    2. Look at the retrieved chunks before looking at the answer. Is the fact there?
    3. If not: search the index for the fact directly. Missing means ingestion or chunking. Present but not retrieved means retrieval.
    4. If it was retrieved: is it in the final prompt, and where? Then it's a context or generation problem.
    5. Add the case to the eval set before fixing, so the fix is proven and the bug can't silently return.
    6. Fix one stage, rerun the whole eval set, compare.
    The two numbers that split the problem

    Recall@k tells you whether retrieval found the evidence. Faithfulness tells you whether the answer stuck to the evidence. Low recall: fix stages 1–3. High recall but low faithfulness: fix stages 4–5. Saying this in an interview shows you debug with measurements, not guesses.

    Interview: Users report your RAG assistant is hallucinating. What do you do?

    "First I check whether it's really generation. I take the reported questions, pull their traces and look at what was retrieved. In my experience most of these are retrieval misses: the right chunk wasn't in the context, so the model filled the gap. I'd measure recall@k on those cases. If recall is low I look at parsing, chunking, filters, and add hybrid search and a reranker. If the evidence was in the context and the answer still drifted, I tighten the prompt to answer only from sources with citations and an explicit refusal, reduce the number of chunks, and add a groundedness check such as Bedrock Guardrails contextual grounding. Every reported case goes into the eval set, and I rerun the full set to make sure the fix didn't break other questions."

    Interview: Retrieval quality dropped after you changed the embedding model. Why, and how should the migration have been done?

    "Vectors from different embedding models live in different spaces, so if queries were embedded with the new model against an index built with the old one, similarity is meaningless. Even with a full re-embed, chunk size and score thresholds tuned for the old model may no longer fit. The safe migration is: build a new index alongside the old one, re-embed everything, run the retrieval eval set on both, re-tune thresholds and top-k, shadow or A/B test, then switch the alias and keep the old index for rollback. I store the model name with each vector so mixing is impossible."

    RAG · structured data · project

    Text-to-SQL over Athena, done safely

    Most enterprise data is in tables, not documents. Text-to-SQL is "RAG for structured data": retrieve the right schema, generate a query, check it, run it, explain the result. For a data engineer moving into agentic AI, this is the most valuable project to have built.

    Why naive text-to-SQL fails

    • Too much schema. Pasting 400 tables into the prompt is slow, costly and confuses the model. Retrieve the 3–8 relevant tables first.
    • Names don't carry meaning. stts_cd = 3 means nothing without a column description. The data catalog comments you write are the model's documentation.
    • Business definitions. "Active customer" or "net revenue" are not in the schema. Keep a glossary or a semantic layer and retrieve from it.
    • Dialect. Athena is Trino SQL; Redshift is PostgreSQL-like. Tell the model which, and show one example.
    • Joins. Without declared keys the model guesses. Give join paths explicitly.
    • Safety and cost. A generated SELECT * on an unpartitioned petabyte table is an expensive mistake; a generated DROP is worse.

    The guard that never trusts the model

    sql_guard.py: parse the SQL, allow one read-only statement, force a LIMIT (pip install sqlglot)Python
    import sqlglot
from sqlglot import exp

FORBIDDEN = (exp.Insert, exp.Update, exp.Delete, exp.Drop, exp.Create, exp.Alter, exp.Merge, exp.Command)

def guard(sql: str, allowed_tables: set[str], max_rows: int = 200) -> str:
    """Return safe SQL or raise ValueError with a message the model can act on."""
    try:
        statements = sqlglot.parse(sql, read="trino")
    except sqlglot.errors.ParseError as e:
        raise ValueError(f"SQL does not parse: {e}")
    if len(statements) != 1 or statements[0] is None:
        raise ValueError("Exactly one statement is allowed.")
    tree = statements[0]
    if not isinstance(tree, (exp.Select, exp.Union)):
        raise ValueError("Only SELECT queries are allowed.")
    if any(tree.find(node) for node in FORBIDDEN):
        raise ValueError("Query contains a write or DDL operation.")
    cte_names = {c.alias_or_name.lower() for c in tree.find_all(exp.CTE)}
    used = {f"{t.db}.{t.name}".lower() if t.db else t.name.lower() for t in tree.find_all(exp.Table)}
    unknown = {t for t in used if t not in allowed_tables and t not in cte_names}
    if unknown:
        raise ValueError(f"Unknown or disallowed tables: {sorted(unknown)}. Allowed: {sorted(allowed_tables)}")
    if isinstance(tree, exp.Select) and not tree.args.get("limit"):
        tree = tree.limit(max_rows)
    return tree.sql(dialect="trino")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    ok = {"ops.job_runs"}
    print(guard("SELECT job_name, sum(dpu_hours) AS dpu FROM ops.job_runs WHERE run_date >= date '2026-11-07' GROUP BY 1 ORDER BY 2 DESC", ok))
    for bad in ["DROP TABLE ops.job_runs", "SELECT * FROM hr.salaries", "SELECT 1; SELECT 2"]:
        try:
            guard(bad, ok)
        except ValueError as e:
            print("blocked:", e)
    OutputOutput
    SELECT job_name, SUM(dpu_hours) AS dpu FROM ops.job_runs WHERE run_date >= CAST('2026-11-07' AS DATE) GROUP BY 1 ORDER BY 2 DESC LIMIT 200
blocked: Only SELECT queries are allowed.
blocked: Unknown or disallowed tables: ['hr.salaries']. Allowed: ['ops.job_runs']
blocked: Exactly one statement is allowed.

    The full loop

    text2sql.py: schema from Glue Data Catalog, generate, guard, run on Athena, self-correctPython
    import os, time, boto3
from sql_guard import guard

glue = boto3.client("glue"); athena = boto3.client("athena")
brt = boto3.client("bedrock-runtime", region_name="us-east-1")
DB, WORKGROUP = "ops", "genai-readonly"       # workgroup has a per-query bytes-scanned limit

def schema_text(tables: list[str]) -> str:
    """Compact DDL-like text with column comments. Comments are the model's documentation."""
    out = []
    for name in tables:
        t = glue.get_table(DatabaseName=DB, Name=name)["Table"]
        cols = t["StorageDescriptor"]["Columns"] + t.get("PartitionKeys", [])
        parts = {c["Name"] for c in t.get("PartitionKeys", [])}
        lines = [f'  {c["Name"]} {c["Type"]}{" -- PARTITION KEY" if c["Name"] in parts else ""}'
                 f'{" -- " + c["Comment"] if c.get("Comment") else ""}' for c in cols]
        out.append(f'TABLE {DB}.{name}  -- {t.get("Description", "")}\n' + "\n".join(lines))
    return "\n\n".join(out)

def run_athena(sql: str, timeout_s: int = 60) -> list[list[str]]:
    qid = athena.start_query_execution(QueryString=sql, QueryExecutionContext={"Database": DB},
                                       WorkGroup=WORKGROUP)["QueryExecutionId"]
    deadline = time.time() + timeout_s
    while True:
        st = athena.get_query_execution(QueryExecutionId=qid)["QueryExecution"]["Status"]
        if st["State"] in ("SUCCEEDED", "FAILED", "CANCELLED"):
            break
        if time.time() > deadline:
            athena.stop_query_execution(QueryExecutionId=qid)
            raise TimeoutError("Athena query timed out")
        time.sleep(1)
    if st["State"] != "SUCCEEDED":
        raise RuntimeError(st.get("StateChangeReason", st["State"]))
    rows = athena.get_query_results(QueryExecutionId=qid, MaxResults=201)["ResultSet"]["Rows"]
    return [[c.get("VarCharValue", "") for c in r["Data"]] for r in rows]

SYSTEM = """You write Amazon Athena (Trino) SQL for a data-platform team.
Rules: one SELECT statement only; use only the tables and columns given; always filter on partition keys
when the question has a time range; never SELECT *; qualify tables as database.table.
Return only the SQL, no explanation, no markdown fences."""

def answer(question: str, tables: list[str], max_fixes: int = 2) -> dict:
    allowed = {f"{DB}.{t}" for t in tables}
    messages = [{"role": "user", "content": [{"text":
        f"<schema>\n{schema_text(tables)}\n</schema>\n\nToday is {time.strftime('%Y-%m-%d')}.\nQuestion: {question}"}]}]
    for attempt in range(max_fixes + 1):
        r = brt.converse(modelId=os.environ["BEDROCK_MODEL_ID"], system=[{"text": SYSTEM}], messages=messages,
                         inferenceConfig={"temperature": 0, "maxTokens": 800})
        sql = r["output"]["message"]["content"][0]["text"].strip().strip("`")
        try:
            safe = guard(sql, allowed)
            rows = run_athena(safe)
            return {"sql": safe, "rows": rows, "attempts": attempt + 1}
        except (ValueError, RuntimeError) as e:                      # feed the error back, let the model fix it
            messages += [r["output"]["message"],
                         {"role": "user", "content": [{"text": f"That query failed: {e}\nReturn a corrected query."}]}]
    raise RuntimeError("no valid query after retries")

print(answer("Which 5 Glue jobs used the most DPU-hours in the last 7 days?", ["job_runs"]))

    Six layers of defence

    LayerWhat it stops
    IAM role with read-only Athena, Glue and S3 permissions; Lake Formation column and row filtersWrites and access to data the caller shouldn't see, even if every other layer fails
    Dedicated Athena workgroup with a bytes-scanned limit per queryRunaway cost
    SQL parser guard (above)DDL/DML, multiple statements, unknown tables, missing LIMIT
    Allow-list of tables retrieved for this questionWandering into unrelated schemas
    Timeouts and retry capHung queries and infinite fix loops
    Human approval for expensive or sensitive queries; audit log of question, SQL, rows returnedThe rest

    How to evaluate text-to-SQL

    Don't compare SQL strings; two different queries can both be right. Use execution accuracy: run the generated query and a hand-written gold query against the same data and compare result sets. Keep 50–100 question/gold-SQL pairs covering joins, aggregations, date logic and the trick questions your users actually ask. Track also: percent that parse first time, percent needing a retry, average bytes scanned.

    As an agent tool

    Wrap answer() as a tool called query_lakehouse and add a second tool find_tables(question) that does vector search over table and column descriptions. Now the agent retrieves schema, writes SQL, runs it and explains the result, and you've built the core of a data-platform assistant. On AgentCore, expose both through Gateway so any framework can call them over MCP.

    Interview: How would you build a natural-language-to-SQL assistant over a lakehouse with hundreds of tables?

    "I'd treat it as RAG over metadata plus a guarded execution loop. Offline, I index table and column descriptions, sample values, join keys and business-term definitions into a vector store; catalog quality matters more than the model here. Online, I retrieve the handful of relevant tables, give the model their schema with comments, the SQL dialect and today's date, and generate one SELECT at temperature zero. Before running I parse the SQL: single statement, read-only, only allowed tables, partition filter present, LIMIT added. I run it in a read-only Athena workgroup with a scan limit under an IAM role with Lake Formation filters, so the database enforces security, not the prompt. If it errors, the error goes back to the model for at most two repairs. I evaluate with execution accuracy against gold queries and log every question, SQL and cost."

    Interview: The model keeps joining the wrong tables. What do you change?

    "That's a context problem, not a model problem. I'd add explicit join paths to the schema context, such as 'orders.customer_id joins customers.id', add column descriptions and a few example question-to-SQL pairs retrieved by similarity, and reduce the number of candidate tables so there are fewer wrong options. If the same join logic is needed often, I'd expose a curated view or a semantic-layer metric and tell the model to use that instead of raw tables. Then I'd add those failing questions to the eval set."

    Agents

    Workflows vs agents

    An agent is an LLM that decides its own next step in a loop, using tools, until a goal is met. A workflow is LLM calls arranged by your code in a fixed path. Most production systems are workflows with small agentic parts, and that's usually the right call.

    The five workflow patterns (Anthropic, "Building effective agents")

    PatternShapeData engineering example
    Prompt chainingStep 1 → check → step 2 → step 3Parse Alteryx XML → write mapping doc → generate PySpark → lint it
    RoutingClassify input, send to a specialised pathTicket triage: SQL question → text-to-SQL; access request → IAM workflow
    ParallelisationRun sections or votes in parallel, then combineReview a PR for security, performance and style in parallel
    Orchestrator–workersAn LLM plans subtasks dynamically and delegatesMigrate 40 jobs: orchestrator splits work, workers convert each job
    Evaluator–optimiserGenerator ↔ critic loop until it passesGenerate SQL, run EXPLAIN and tests, fix until green

    Anatomy of an agent

    Model
    The reasoning engine; must be good at tool use.
    Instructions
    Role, goal, rules, when to stop, when to ask a human.
    Tools
    Functions, APIs, MCP servers, retrieval, code execution, other agents.
    State and memory
    Conversation, scratchpad, long-term facts and preferences.
    Loop and control
    Turn limits, budgets, approvals, error handling: the harness.
    Interview: When do you choose an agent over a workflow?

    "When the path can't be known in advance: the number and order of steps depends on what you find, like debugging a pipeline failure or open-ended research. If I can draw the flowchart, I build a workflow, because it's cheaper, faster, testable and predictable. Often the answer is a workflow with an agentic step inside, e.g. a fixed ingestion pipeline where one node is an agent that resolves ambiguous schema mappings."

    Agents

    The ReAct pattern

    ReAct (Reason + Act, Yao et al., 2022) is the loop every agent runs: think about what to do, take an action with a tool, read the observation, repeat. Step through a real trace below.

    ReAct trace: "Which Glue job uses the most DPU-hours this week, and is it growing?"Live lab

      Text ReAct vs native tool calling

      The original paper had the model write Thought:, Action: tool[input] as text and the app parsed it with regex. Modern models emit structured tool calls (previous chapter) and often keep reasoning in separate thinking blocks. The loop is identical; only the format changed. Know both, because interviewers like to ask you to implement ReAct "from scratch".

      ReAct from scratch with text parsing (interview whiteboard version)Python
      import re

PROMPT = """Answer the question. You can use these tools:
- sql[query]: run a read-only SQL query, returns rows
- calc[expression]: evaluate arithmetic

Use this format exactly:
Thought: what you will do next
Action: tool_name[input]
(wait for Observation)
... repeat ...
Thought: I know the answer
Final Answer: the answer

Question: {question}
"""

def react(question, llm, tools, max_steps=6):
    transcript = PROMPT.format(question=question)
    for _ in range(max_steps):
        out = llm(transcript, stop=["Observation:"])         # stop before the model invents results
        transcript += out
        if "Final Answer:" in out:
            return out.split("Final Answer:")[-1].strip()
        m = re.search(r"Action:\s*(\w+)\[(.*)\]", out, re.S)
        if not m:
            transcript += "\nObservation: invalid action format, use tool_name[input]\n"
            continue
        name, arg = m.group(1), m.group(2)
        obs = tools[name](arg) if name in tools else f"unknown tool {name}"
        transcript += f"\nObservation: {obs}\n"
    return "No answer within step limit"

      Why ReAct works, and where it fails

      Strengths

      • Grounds reasoning in real observations instead of guesses
      • Adapts the plan after each result
      • Trace is readable and debuggable

      Failure modes

      • Loops: repeating the same tool call
      • Premature answer before enough evidence
      • Wrong tool or bad arguments
      • Context bloat from long observations
      • One step at a time: slow for long tasks
      Interview: Explain ReAct with an example.

      "ReAct interleaves reasoning and acting. Say the question is 'which Glue job used the most DPU-hours this week and is it growing?' The model thinks it needs usage data, calls a SQL tool on the job-runs table, observes the top job, thinks it needs a comparison, calls SQL for the previous weeks, observes the trend, then answers. Each observation changes the next thought. That's better than chain-of-thought alone, which would guess numbers, and better than acting alone, which can't plan. Today it's implemented with native tool calls in a loop with limits."

      Agents

      Planning, reflection and multi-agent

      ReAct decides one step at a time. These patterns add planning ahead, self-critique, and teams of agents. Use them when evals show the simple loop failing, not by default.

      Single-agent reasoning patterns

      PatternIdeaUse when
      Plan-and-ExecuteWrite a plan (todo list) first, execute steps, re-plan when something changesLong tasks with many steps; makes progress visible (deep agents' write_todos)
      ReWOOPlan all tool calls up front with placeholders, run them, then solvePredictable tool sequences; fewer LLM calls
      Reflection / ReflexionCritique the output (or a failed attempt) and retry with the critique in memoryCode, SQL, writing; anything you can check
      Self-consistency / votingSeveral independent attempts, pick the majority or best-judgedHigh-stakes classification or reasoning
      Tree of Thoughts / searchExplore and score several partial solutionsPuzzles, planning; rarely worth the cost in business apps
      Code-as-action / programmatic tool callingThe model writes a short program that calls several tools, run in a sandboxMany tool calls with data wrangling in between; cuts tokens and turns

      Multi-agent topologies

      Multi-agent costs

      Multi-agent systems use several times more tokens than a single agent and add coordination failures (agents repeating work, losing context in handoffs). They pay off for parallel, breadth-first work (research across many sources, reviewing many files) or when separate permission scopes are needed. Many "multi-agent" designs work better as one agent with good tools, or as subagents with isolated context that return a short summary.

      Subagents and context isolation

      A popular 2025–26 pattern: the main agent hands a focused task to a subagent that has its own fresh context window, does a lot of searching or reading, and returns only a concise result. The main context stays small. This is how coding agents, deep research agents, LangChain Deep Agents, and the Strands and AgentCore harnesses handle big tasks.

      Interview: Design a multi-agent PR reviewer.

      "A supervisor reads the diff and repo metadata, classifies the technology, and fans out to specialist reviewers in parallel: security (with SAST tool results), performance, standards for that stack, and test coverage. Each returns structured findings with severity and file/line. An aggregator de-duplicates, scores risk, and drafts the review. High-risk results go to a human approval step before comments post to GitHub or tickets open in Jira. I'd evaluate with a set of historical PRs with known issues: precision and recall of findings, false-positive rate, and cost per PR."

      Agents · design + code

      Multi-agent systems in depth

      "Multi-agent" means several LLM loops, each with its own prompt, tools and context, coordinated by code or by another agent. It is the most over-used idea in the field. Learn the four shapes, when each earns its cost, and how to build the most useful one.

      Why split at all

      One agent with 40 tools and a 5,000-word prompt gets confused: it picks wrong tools and its context fills with irrelevant results. Splitting gives each agent a small, clean context and a narrow job. That is the real benefit, more than "teamwork". The price is more LLM calls, more latency, and a new failure mode: information lost in handoffs.

      ShapeHow control flowsGood forMain risk
      Supervisor / orchestrator-workersA lead agent calls specialist agents like tools and gets a summary backMixed questions across domains; research; keeping the lead's context smallSupervisor becomes a bottleneck; summaries drop details
      Handoffs (swarm)The active agent transfers the conversation to a peerCustomer-facing flows where one specialist should own the conversationPing-pong between agents; unclear ownership
      Sequential pipelineFixed order, each output feeds the nextRepeatable processes: extract → transform → reviewReally a workflow; errors compound downstream
      ParallelSplit work, run agents at the same time, mergeIndependent subtasks: per-table checks, per-file reviewCost multiplies; merge step must reconcile conflicts

      The decision rule

      1. Start with one agent. Improve its tools and prompt.
      2. If the steps are known in advance, write a workflow (plain code or a graph), not agents talking.
      3. Split into multiple agents only when you can name the reason: context is overflowing, tool selection is failing because there are too many tools, subtasks are independent and can run in parallel, or different parts need different models or permissions.

      Build it: a supervisor with subagents as tools

      This is the most reliable shape in practice. Each specialist is a normal agent; the supervisor sees it as a tool that takes a task description and returns a short result. Each specialist starts with a fresh context, so the supervisor's history stays small.

      supervisor.py (LangChain 1.x; subagents wrapped as tools)Python
      import os
from langchain.agents import create_agent
from langchain.tools import tool
from langchain_aws import ChatBedrockConverse

big = ChatBedrockConverse(model=os.environ["BEDROCK_MODEL_ID"], region_name="us-east-1", temperature=0)
small = ChatBedrockConverse(model=os.environ.get("BEDROCK_SMALL_MODEL_ID", os.environ["BEDROCK_MODEL_ID"]),
                            region_name="us-east-1", temperature=0)       # cheaper model for workers

# ---- specialist 1: lakehouse SQL ----
@tool
def run_athena_sql(sql: str) -> str:
    """Run a read-only Athena query. Returns CSV rows (max 50)."""
    return "job_name,failures\nqm_trusted_load,4\nmarlo_enriched,1"

sql_agent = create_agent(model=small, tools=[run_athena_sql], name="sql_agent",
    system_prompt="You answer data questions by writing and running Athena SQL. "
                  "Reply with the numbers and the SQL you ran. Be brief.")

# ---- specialist 2: runbook search ----
@tool
def search_runbooks(query: str) -> str:
    """Search internal runbooks. Returns the 3 most relevant passages with source ids."""
    return "[glue-oom.md#0] For OutOfMemory move G.1X -> G.2X and avoid collect() on large DataFrames."

docs_agent = create_agent(model=small, tools=[search_runbooks], name="docs_agent",
    system_prompt="You find procedures in runbooks. Quote the passage and its source id. "
                  "If nothing matches, say so.")

# ---- wrap each specialist as a tool for the supervisor ----
def as_tool(agent, name: str, description: str):
    @tool(name, description=description)
    def call(task: str) -> str:
        result = agent.invoke({"messages": [{"role": "user", "content": task}]})
        return result["messages"][-1].text          # only the final answer returns to the supervisor
    return call

supervisor = create_agent(
    model=big,
    tools=[
        as_tool(sql_agent, "ask_data_analyst",
                "Delegate a question that needs numbers from the lakehouse (job runs, row counts, costs). "
                "Give a complete, self-contained task: the analyst cannot see this conversation."),
        as_tool(docs_agent, "ask_runbook_librarian",
                "Delegate a question about procedures, fixes or how-tos. "
                "Give a complete, self-contained task: the librarian cannot see this conversation."),
    ],
    system_prompt="You are the on-call assistant for a data platform. Break the request into sub-tasks, "
                  "delegate each to the right specialist, then write one answer that cites what each said. "
                  "Do not answer from your own knowledge when a specialist can check.",
)

out = supervisor.invoke({"messages": [{"role": "user",
      "content": "Which job failed most this week, and what is the documented fix for its error?"}]})
print(out["messages"][-1].text)

      What makes or breaks it

      Task descriptions
      The subagent sees only the string the supervisor writes. Vague delegation ("look into the failures") gives vague results. Tell the supervisor, in the tool description, to write complete tasks: goal, scope, expected output format.
      What comes back
      Return a short result, not the subagent's whole transcript. That is the context saving. For large outputs, write to a file or store and return a reference.
      Shared state
      Agents shouldn't share a giant message list. Share a small structured state (ticket ID, tables in scope) and let each agent keep its own scratchpad.
      Limits
      Cap depth (subagents shouldn't spawn subagents without limit), calls per run and total tokens. Multi-agent runs commonly cost several times a single agent.
      Tracing
      One trace for the whole run with a span per agent and per tool, or debugging is impossible.
      Cross-team agents
      Inside one codebase, call subagents directly. Across teams or vendors, expose an agent over the A2A protocol so others can discover and call it without sharing a framework.
      The honest interview answer

      "I'd default to a single agent or a workflow. I'd add a second agent only for context isolation, parallelism or a permissions boundary, and I'd measure whether it beats the single agent on the eval set before keeping it." Interviewers hear a lot of multi-agent enthusiasm; restraint backed by a reason stands out.

      Interview: When would you use a multi-agent design instead of a single agent?

      "When a single agent is failing for a structural reason. Three cases: the context is overloaded, so I isolate sub-tasks in subagents that return summaries; there are too many tools and tool selection is degrading, so I group tools under specialists; or the sub-tasks are independent and can run in parallel, like checking 20 tables. A fourth is a security boundary: an agent that reads untrusted content shouldn't also hold write permissions. I'd use a supervisor with subagents-as-tools as the first shape because it's the easiest to trace and evaluate. I'd avoid it when steps are fixed, where a plain workflow is cheaper and more predictable."

      Interview: What goes wrong in multi-agent systems?

      "Handoff loss is the big one: the supervisor writes a thin task or the worker returns a thin summary, and a key detail disappears. Then cost and latency, since every hop is more LLM calls. Then loops, where agents bounce a task between them; duplicate work when two agents do the same thing; conflicting outputs that the merger can't reconcile; and error cascades where one agent's wrong assumption becomes another's fact. My mitigations are explicit task and result formats, step and budget limits, one shared trace, and evals at both the sub-agent and end-to-end level."

      Agents

      Tools, MCP, A2A and Skills

      Tools are how agents touch the world. MCP standardises connecting tools to agents, A2A standardises agents talking to agents, and Skills package know-how an agent loads on demand.

      Designing good tools

      1. Name and describe for the model: what it does, when to use it, what it returns, limits. The description is a prompt.
      2. Few, high-level tools beat many thin API wrappers: find_failed_runs(date, job_pattern) instead of list_jobs + get_runs + filter.
      3. Typed, validated inputs: enums, required fields, formats. Reject bad input with a clear error the model can act on.
      4. Small outputs: cap rows, summarise, paginate. A 2MB tool result wrecks context and cost.
      5. Idempotent and safe: read-only by default; writes need approval or dry-run mode.
      6. Errors as messages: "Table not found. Available tables: ..." lets the model recover.

      Model Context Protocol (MCP)

      Host
      The AI application. It creates one client per server connection.
      Server
      Exposes tools (functions the model can call), resources (readable data like files or schemas) and prompts (templates).
      Transports
      stdio for local servers; Streamable HTTP for remote ones.
      Why it matters
      Write a tool server once, use it from any MCP host: Claude, Strands, LangChain, CrewAI, LlamaIndex, AgentCore Gateway, IDEs. N×M integrations become N+M.
      2026-07-28 spec New
      Stateless base protocol (no initialize handshake or session header), so servers scale behind plain load balancers; a Tasks extension for long-running calls that return a handle; MCP Apps for server-rendered UI; formal extensions; OAuth/OIDC-aligned authorisation.
      An MCP server exposing your data tools (official Python SDK 2.x)Python
      # pip install "mcp>=2"   (in mcp 1.x the same class was FastMCP: from mcp.server.fastmcp import FastMCP)
from mcp.server.mcpserver import MCPServer
import boto3, time

mcp = MCPServer("lakehouse-tools")
athena = boto3.client("athena", region_name="us-east-1")

@mcp.tool()
def run_readonly_sql(sql: str, max_rows: int = 50) -> list[list[str]]:
    """Run a read-only Athena query on the analytics database and return up to max_rows rows."""
    if not sql.lstrip().lower().startswith(("select", "with")):
        raise ValueError("Only SELECT/WITH queries are allowed")
    qid = athena.start_query_execution(QueryString=sql, WorkGroup="agents-readonly")["QueryExecutionId"]
    while (state := athena.get_query_execution(QueryExecutionId=qid)["QueryExecution"]["Status"]["State"]) in ("QUEUED", "RUNNING"):
        time.sleep(1)
    if state != "SUCCEEDED":
        raise RuntimeError(f"Query {state}")
    rows = athena.get_query_results(QueryExecutionId=qid, MaxResults=max_rows + 1)["ResultSet"]["Rows"]
    return [[c.get("VarCharValue", "") for c in r["Data"]] for r in rows]

@mcp.resource("schema://{table}")
def table_schema(table: str) -> str:
    """Column names and types for a Glue Catalog table."""
    glue = boto3.client("glue")
    cols = glue.get_table(DatabaseName="analytics", Name=table)["Table"]["StorageDescriptor"]["Columns"]
    return "\n".join(f"{c['Name']} {c['Type']}" for c in cols)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    mcp.run()                          # stdio; use mcp.run(transport="streamable-http") to serve remotely
      Version note

      The official MCP Python SDK 2.x renamed FastMCP to MCPServer. Most tutorials online still show the 1.x import. The separate fastmcp package is a different, community-maintained project with its own API.

      On AWS

      AgentCore Gateway turns Lambda functions, OpenAPI specs and existing MCP servers into one secure MCP endpoint with auth, semantic tool search and Cedar policies (AgentCore Policy). AWS also publishes open-source MCP servers for many services (awslabs/mcp).

      A2A: agent to agent

      The Agent2Agent protocol (Google, April 2025, now a Linux Foundation project) lets independently built agents find each other through an Agent Card (JSON describing skills and endpoint), send tasks, stream progress and exchange artefacts. MCP connects an agent to tools; A2A connects agents to agents across teams or vendors. Strands, CrewAI, Google ADK, LangGraph and AgentCore Runtime support it.

      Agent Skills

      A skill is a folder with a SKILL.md (name, description, instructions) plus optional scripts and reference files. The agent sees only the short description; it loads the full instructions when a task matches. This keeps context small while giving the agent deep procedures ("how we write Glue jobs", "our PR review checklist"). Anthropic introduced it in October 2025 and published it as an open standard; Strands, Deep Agents and AgentCore harness load skills.

      Interview: MCP vs function calling vs A2A?

      "Function calling is the model-level mechanism: the model emits a structured call. MCP is the integration protocol: a standard way for an application to discover and call tools, resources and prompts hosted in servers, so tools are reusable across apps.
