      What to notice

      Every version turns a Python function's signature and docstring into the same JSON tool spec you wrote by hand, and every version runs the same Converse tool loop underneath. The differences are where state lives, how you add control (graph edges, hooks, middleware, Flows, Workflows) and what's built in for deployment and tracing.

      Production on AWS

      Bedrock AgentCore map

      AgentCore is a set of managed services for running agents in production, built with any framework (Strands, LangGraph, CrewAI, LlamaIndex, OpenAI Agents SDK) and any model. Use the pieces you need; they work independently. Your AgentCore Runtime Handbook covers the hands-on deployment; this lesson is the interview map.

      ComponentWhat it doesStatus
      RuntimeHosts agent or tool code serverlessly with per-session microVM isolation, long sessions (up to 8 hours), streaming, and HTTP/MCP/A2A protocols. Bring a container or a Python entrypoint.GA Oct 2025
      HarnessManaged agent loop from a declarative definition (model, tools, skills, instructions); filesystem, shell, memory, browser per session; switch models mid-session; export to Strands code. No extra charge beyond the AgentCore pieces used.GA Jun 2026
      GatewayTurns Lambda functions, OpenAPI/Smithy APIs and MCP servers into one MCP endpoint with auth and semantic tool searchGA
      PolicyRules (written in natural language or Cedar) checked by Gateway on every tool call, outside the agent code; can also apply Bedrock Guardrails (Jun 2026)GA Mar 2026
      MemoryShort-term events plus long-term records extracted by strategies (semantic facts, summaries, user preferences, episodic)GA
      IdentityInbound auth (Cognito, Okta, Entra ID) and outbound OAuth/API-key vault so agents act on behalf of usersGA
      Code Interpreter, BrowserSandboxed code execution and managed headless browsersGA
      ObservabilityOpenTelemetry traces, metrics and logs into CloudWatch GenAI observabilityGA
      Evaluations13 built-in evaluators (quality, safety, task completion, tool use) plus custom LLM or Lambda code judges; online (sampled live traces) and on-demand (CI)GA Mar 2026
      Agent RegistryCatalogue and approval workflow for agents and toolsGA Aug 2026
      The AgentCore CLI flow (details in your AgentCore Runtime Handbook)Terminal
      agentcore create --name OpsAgent        # scaffold a project (framework template or harness)
agentcore dev                           # run locally
agentcore deploy                        # package, push, create the Runtime endpoint
agentcore invoke --runtime OpsAgent --payload '{"prompt": "Which Glue jobs failed today?"}'
agentcore logs; agentcore traces        # debug
agentcore eval                          # run evaluations
agentcore export                        # harness → Strands code when you need custom orchestration
      Interview: AgentCore vs Bedrock Agents (classic)?

      "Bedrock Agents is the earlier fully managed, console-configured agent with action groups and knowledge bases: quick, but limited control over the loop. AgentCore is infrastructure for any framework and model: you bring your own agent code (or use the declarative harness) and get isolated runtime sessions, a tool gateway with policies, memory, identity, observability and evaluations. For new agentic systems I'd use AgentCore; Bedrock Agents still fits simple managed use cases."

      Interview: Why not just run the agent on Lambda or ECS?

      "You can, and for simple agents that's fine. But agents have long, stateful, bursty sessions: Lambda's 15-minute limit and shared execution environments are awkward, and on ECS you build session isolation, scaling to zero, auth, token vaults and tracing yourself. Runtime gives per-session microVM isolation, up to 8-hour sessions, and consumption pricing that doesn't bill while waiting on I/O for CPU. Gateway, Identity, Memory and Policy cover the pieces every team otherwise rebuilds."

      Production on AWS · system design

      Reference architecture: a data-platform agent

      A design you can draw in any system-design round: an assistant for data engineers and analysts that answers questions from runbooks and tickets, finds tables, runs safe SQL, checks job health, and opens tickets, with approvals and evals.

      Walk through a request

      1. User asks in Slack: "Why did Marlo IT enriched miss its SLA yesterday?" API Gateway authenticates via the IdP; the agent receives user ID and groups.
      2. A small model classifies intent (incident analysis) and loads relevant memory (user's team, preferred SQL dialect).
      3. The main model plans: check job runs → read logs → compare volumes → search past tickets.
      4. Tool calls go through Gateway; Policy allows run_sql only on the read-only workgroup and only on tables the user's groups can see (Lake Formation enforces it again).
      5. Results are trimmed (row caps, log excerpts) before going back to the model.
      6. The agent proposes "rerun with G.2X workers"; because that's a write, it pauses for approval in Slack.
      7. Every step is traced; tokens and cost are attributed to the user and team; a sample of traces is scored online.

      Non-functional decisions to say out loud

      Latency
      Stream tokens; route simple questions to a small model; cache prompt prefixes; parallel tool calls.
      Cost
      Budget per request (turns, tokens); batch for offline enrichment; measure cost per resolved question.
      Reliability
      Retries with backoff, model fallback, idempotent tools, checkpointed state, timeouts on every tool.
      Security
      On-behalf-of identity, least-privilege tools, Policy, Guardrails, no raw egress, full audit trail.
      Quality
      Golden set of 150 real questions; retrieval and trajectory metrics; release only if scores don't drop.
      IaC and CI/CD
      Terraform for Gateway, Runtime, Memory, KBs; prompts and tool specs versioned in Git; eval job gates deploys.

      Production on AWS

      Observability and LLMOps

      You can't debug or improve an agent you can't see. Trace every model call and tool call with inputs, outputs, tokens, latency, cost and errors, grouped into one trace per request.

      What to capture per span

      SpanAttributes
      Request (root)user/tenant, session/thread id, app version, prompt version, total cost, final status, user feedback
      Model callmodel id, input/output/cached/thinking tokens, latency, time to first token, stop reason, temperature
      Tool calltool name, arguments (redacted), result size, duration, error, retries, approval decision
      Retrievalquery, index, top-k ids and scores, filters applied

      The industry standard is OpenTelemetry with the GenAI semantic conventions (gen_ai.* attributes). Strands emits OTel natively; LangChain/LangGraph trace to LangSmith and can export OTel; AgentCore Observability collects OTel into CloudWatch GenAI observability. Langfuse and Arize Phoenix are popular open-source backends.

      Send Strands or AgentCore traces to CloudWatch (ADOT auto-instrumentation)Terminal
      pip install aws-opentelemetry-distro
export OTEL_PYTHON_DISTRO=aws_distro OTEL_PYTHON_CONFIGURATOR=aws_configurator
export AGENT_OBSERVABILITY_ENABLED=true
opentelemetry-instrument python app.py
      LangChain/LangGraph → LangSmithTerminal
      export LANGSMITH_TRACING=true
export LANGSMITH_API_KEY=...
export LANGSMITH_PROJECT=ops-agent-prod

      The LLMOps loop

      Fine-tuning and efficiency (enough for interviews)

      LoRA / QLoRA
      Train small adapters; QLoRA on a 4-bit base model. Cheap, swappable per task.
      Bedrock customisation
      Fine-tuning, continued pre-training, distillation (big teacher → small student), reinforcement fine-tuning for supported models; custom model import for your own weights.
      When it pays off
      High-volume narrow tasks where a fine-tuned small model matches a big one at a fraction of cost and latency; strict formats; domain language.
      Serving open models
      vLLM or TGI on SageMaker/EKS: continuous batching, paged KV cache, quantisation (AWQ, GPTQ, FP8), speculative decoding.

      Production · hands-on

      Cost and latency engineering

      A demo that costs 40 cents and takes 25 seconds per answer is not a product. Cost and latency come from the same few sources, and there is a standard order in which to attack them. Interviewers ask this as "your bill doubled" or "p95 is 20 seconds, fix it".

      Where the time and money go

      SourceDrivesFirst fix
      Output tokensLatency (generated one by one) and cost (priciest tokens)Ask for shorter answers, set maxTokens, structured output instead of prose
      Input tokens re-sent every turnCost, some latencyPrompt caching, trim history, fewer and smaller chunks, compact tool results
      Number of LLM calls per requestBoth, multipliedFewer agent turns, parallel tool calls, workflows instead of free loops
      Model sizeBothRoute easy work to a small model; reserve the large model for hard steps
      Reasoning / thinking tokensBothLower the effort or budget for simple steps
      Tools and retrievalLatencyRun in parallel, add timeouts, cache results, tune the index
      Cold starts and throttling retriesTail latencyWarm capacity, cross-Region inference, backoff with jitter

      Lever 1: prompt caching

      If many requests start with the same long prefix (system prompt, tool definitions, a policy document), mark the end of that prefix with a cache point. The first call writes the cache; later calls within the cache lifetime read it at a fraction of the input price and skip re-processing it. The prefix must be byte-identical, so keep anything that changes (date, user name, question) after the cache point.

      Prompt caching with Converse: stable prefix first, cache point, then the variable partPython
      import os, boto3
brt = boto3.client("bedrock-runtime", region_name="us-east-1")
RUNBOOK = open("oncall_runbook.md").read()            # long and identical for every request

def ask(question: str) -> str:
    resp = brt.converse(
        modelId=os.environ["BEDROCK_MODEL_ID"],
        system=[{"text": "You are the on-call assistant. Answer from the runbook below.\n\n" + RUNBOOK},
                {"cachePoint": {"type": "default"}}],                 # everything above is cached
        messages=[{"role": "user", "content": [{"text": question}]}], # everything below changes per call
        inferenceConfig={"maxTokens": 400, "temperature": 0},
    )
    u = resp["usage"]
    print(f'in={u["inputTokens"]} cache_write={u.get("cacheWriteInputTokens", 0)} '
          f'cache_read={u.get("cacheReadInputTokens", 0)} out={u["outputTokens"]}')
    return resp["output"]["message"]["content"][0]["text"]

ask("Glue job OOM: first step?")       # first call: cache_write > 0
ask("Who do I page for Marlo?")        # second call: cache_read > 0, lower cost and latency
      Caching rules that trip people up

      Caches expire after a short idle time (minutes), models have a minimum cacheable prefix length, and cache writes cost a little more than normal input. Caching pays off when the same prefix is reused several times within the lifetime. Putting a timestamp or request ID at the top of the system prompt silently disables it. Check cacheReadInputTokens in your logs to prove it's working.

      Lever 2: route by difficulty

      A two-tier router: cheap model first, escalate only when neededPython
      SMALL, LARGE = "small-model", "large-model"       # in real code: two Bedrock model IDs from config

def pick_model(task: dict) -> str:
    """Rule-based routing is the usual starting point: transparent, free and easy to evaluate."""
    if task["kind"] in {"classify", "extract", "grade", "rewrite_query", "summarise_tool_output"}:
        return SMALL
    if task.get("tools_needed", 0) > 2 or task.get("input_tokens", 0) > 30_000:
        return LARGE
    if task["kind"] in {"plan", "final_answer", "sql_generation", "code"}:
        return LARGE
    return SMALL

for t in [{"kind": "grade"}, {"kind": "final_answer"}, {"kind": "qa", "tools_needed": 4}, {"kind": "qa"}]:
    print(t, "->", pick_model(t))
      OutputOutput
      {'kind': 'grade'} -> small-model
{'kind': 'final_answer'} -> large-model
{'kind': 'qa', 'tools_needed': 4} -> large-model
{'kind': 'qa'} -> small-model

      Managed option: Bedrock Intelligent Prompt Routing picks between models in a family per request. Whatever does the routing, evaluate each route on your own eval set: a router that saves 60% and drops accuracy five points may or may not be a good trade.

      Lever 3: cache answers

      A semantic cache: reuse an answer when a new question means the same thingPython
      import zlib
import numpy as np

class SemanticCache:
    def __init__(self, embed, threshold: float = 0.92):
        self.embed, self.threshold = embed, threshold
        self.vecs, self.items = [], []                # production: MemoryDB/Valkey vector search with TTL

    def get(self, question: str, scope: str):
        if not self.vecs:
            return None
        q = self.embed(question)
        sims = np.array(self.vecs) @ q
        i = int(sims.argmax())
        hit = self.items[i]
        if sims[i] >= self.threshold and hit["scope"] == scope:      # never share across tenants/permissions
            return hit["answer"]
        return None

    def put(self, question: str, scope: str, answer: str):
        self.vecs.append(self.embed(question)); self.items.append({"scope": scope, "answer": answer})

def toy_embed(text: str) -> np.ndarray:               # stand-in so this runs offline; use a real embedding model
    v = np.zeros(64)
    for w in text.lower().replace("?", "").split():
        v[zlib.crc32(w.encode()) % 64] += 1
    return v / (np.linalg.norm(v) or 1)

cache = SemanticCache(toy_embed, threshold=0.8)
cache.put("how do I fix a glue job out of memory error", "team-a", "Move to G.2X and avoid collect().")
print(cache.get("how do I fix glue job out of memory error", "team-a"))    # near-duplicate, same scope
print(cache.get("how do I fix glue job out of memory error", "team-b"))    # different scope
print(cache.get("when does the marlo pipeline run", "team-a"))             # different question
      OutputOutput
      Move to G.2X and avoid collect().
None
None
      Cache typeKeyGood forDanger
      Prompt cache (provider)Identical prefixLong system prompts, tool lists, documentsNone for correctness; only savings
      Exact response cacheHash of full requestRepeated identical calls, evals, retriesStale data if sources change
      Semantic cacheEmbedding similarityFAQ-style trafficFalse hits ("in Japan" vs "in Italy"); leaking across users. Scope the key, set a TTL, keep the threshold high
      Tool / retrieval cacheTool name + argsSlow lookups repeated within a runStale results; cache only read-only tools

      Lever 4: make it feel fast

      Streaming with ConverseStream: time to first token is what users noticePython
      import os, time, boto3
brt = boto3.client("bedrock-runtime", region_name="us-east-1")

t0 = time.time(); first = None
resp = brt.converse_stream(
    modelId=os.environ["BEDROCK_MODEL_ID"],
    messages=[{"role": "user", "content": [{"text": "Explain Iceberg snapshot expiry in 5 bullet points."}]}],
    inferenceConfig={"maxTokens": 400})
for event in resp["stream"]:
    if "contentBlockDelta" in event:
        first = first or time.time() - t0
        print(event["contentBlockDelta"]["delta"].get("text", ""), end="", flush=True)
    elif "metadata" in event:
        usage = event["metadata"]["usage"]
print(f"\n\nTTFT {first:.2f}s  total {time.time() - t0:.2f}s  out tokens {usage['outputTokens']}")

      Measure three numbers separately: TTFT (time to first token), tokens per second, and total time. Streaming improves the first without changing the last. For agents, also stream progress events ("searching runbooks…", "running query…") so a 20-second task doesn't look frozen.

      Lever 5: don't do it in real time

      Classification, extraction, summarisation and embedding of large backlogs don't need an instant answer. Bedrock batch inference takes JSONL from S3 and returns results to S3 at a lower price than on-demand. For a data engineer this is the natural shape: an LLM step inside a Glue, Step Functions or Airflow pipeline.

      The order to apply them

      1. Measure: log tokens, calls and latency per request, tagged by feature. Use Bedrock cost breakdowns in Cost Explorer and application inference profiles for per-team attribution.
      2. Cut waste: shorter outputs, fewer chunks, trimmed tool results, capped history.
      3. Cache the stable prefix.
      4. Route simple steps to a small model.
      5. Parallelise independent tool calls and retrieval.
      6. Batch whatever can wait.
      7. Only then consider fine-tuning or distilling a small model, or provisioned capacity for steady high volume.

      After every change, rerun the eval set. Cost optimisation without a quality check is just making the product worse cheaply.

      Interview: p95 latency for your agent is 25 seconds. How do you bring it down?

      "I'd break the trace into spans first: model calls, tool calls, retrieval, and count turns. Usually it's too many sequential LLM turns and long outputs. So: reduce turns by giving the agent better task-level tools and letting it call tools in parallel; cap and shorten outputs; move grading, routing and summarising steps to a small fast model; cache the system prompt and tool definitions; add timeouts and caching to slow tools. For perceived latency I'd stream tokens and progress events so time to first token is under a couple of seconds. For tail latency I'd look at throttling retries and cold starts: cross-Region inference profiles, backoff with jitter, and on AgentCore Runtime V2 the snapshot starts and the OverheadLatency metric to separate platform time from my code. I'd confirm quality held on the eval set."

      Interview: How does prompt caching differ from a semantic cache?

      "Prompt caching is done by the model provider: it stores the processed form of an identical prompt prefix, so the next request with that same prefix is cheaper and faster, but the model still generates a fresh answer. It's safe for correctness. A semantic cache is something I build: it stores final answers keyed by the embedding of the question and returns a stored answer when a new question is similar enough, skipping the model completely. That saves more but can return a wrong answer on a near-miss or leak across users, so it needs a high similarity threshold, a TTL, and a cache key scoped by tenant and permissions."

      Production · hands-on

      Reliability: retries, fallbacks, quotas and idempotency

      LLM APIs fail more often than the databases you're used to: throttling, timeouts, overloaded models, malformed outputs. A production system assumes every call can fail and decides in advance what happens next.

      The errors you will see on Bedrock

      ErrorMeaningDo
      ThrottlingException (429)Over your requests-per-minute or tokens-per-minute quotaRetry with exponential backoff and jitter; smooth traffic; request a quota increase; use cross-Region inference
      ServiceUnavailableException, InternalServerExceptionTemporary service problemRetry; then fall back
      ModelTimeoutException, read timeoutGeneration took too longRaise client read timeout for long outputs; stream; reduce output; fall back
      ModelNotReadyExceptionModel warming upRetry
      ValidationExceptionYour request is wrong (bad message order, too many tokens, bad schema)Do not retry; fix the request. Often means context overflow: trim and retry once
      AccessDeniedExceptionIAM or model access not grantedDo not retry; fix permissions
      Stop reason max_tokensAnswer cut offNot an exception: check stopReason on every response
      Stop reason guardrail_intervened / content filteredBlocked by a guardrailReturn a safe message; log it; don't retry blindly

      A call wrapper with retry and model fallback

      resilient.pyPython
      import os, random, time, boto3
from botocore.config import Config
from botocore.exceptions import ClientError, ReadTimeoutError

brt = boto3.client("bedrock-runtime", region_name="us-east-1",
                   config=Config(read_timeout=120, connect_timeout=5, retries={"max_attempts": 1}))  # we do our own retries

RETRYABLE = {"ThrottlingException", "ServiceUnavailableException", "InternalServerException",
             "ModelTimeoutException", "ModelNotReadyException"}
CHAIN = [os.environ["BEDROCK_MODEL_ID"],                       # primary
         os.environ.get("BEDROCK_FALLBACK_MODEL_ID", "")]      # e.g. another model or a cross-Region profile

def converse_resilient(messages, system=None, max_attempts=4, **kw):
    last = None
    for model in [m for m in CHAIN if m]:
        for attempt in range(max_attempts):
            try:
                resp = brt.converse(modelId=model, messages=messages, system=system or [], **kw)
                if resp["stopReason"] == "max_tokens":
                    resp["truncated"] = True                    # surface it; caller decides
                resp["model_used"] = model
                return resp
            except ReadTimeoutError as e:
                last = e
            except ClientError as e:
                last = e
                if e.response["Error"]["Code"] not in RETRYABLE:
                    raise                                       # validation / access errors: fail fast
            time.sleep(min(20, 2 ** attempt) * random.uniform(0.5, 1.0))   # exponential backoff + jitter
        # retries exhausted on this model: fall through to the next one in the chain
    raise RuntimeError(f"all models failed: {last}")
      Backoff with jitter
      Wait 1, 2, 4, 8… seconds, multiplied by a random factor. Without jitter, all your workers retry at the same instant and get throttled again.
      Fallback chain
      A second model or Region to try when the first is unavailable. The fallback may behave differently, so it must pass the same eval set. AgentCore Gateway can now do this at the platform level with ordered targets and a circuit breaker.
      Circuit breaker
      After N failures, stop calling the failing target for a cool-down period instead of making every request wait for a timeout.
      Cross-Region inference
      An inference profile routes requests to capacity in several Regions. It raises effective throughput and resilience; check data-residency rules before using a geography-wide or global profile.
      Timeouts everywhere
      On the model call, each tool, the whole agent run, and the user request. A missing timeout is how one stuck tool hangs a whole system.
      Graceful degradation
      If the reranker is down, answer from un-reranked results. If the LLM is down, return search results. Decide these in advance.

      Idempotency: the rule for anything that writes

      Agents retry, harnesses replay nodes after a crash, and users double-click. Any tool that changes state must be safe to call twice.

      An idempotent write tool using a DynamoDB conditional putPython
      import hashlib, json, boto3
from botocore.exceptions import ClientError

ddb = boto3.client("dynamodb"); glue = boto3.client("glue")

def rerun_glue_job(job_name: str, reason: str, run_key: str) -> dict:
    """Start a Glue job at most once per run_key. run_key comes from the harness (thread id + step), not the model."""
    key = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([job_name, run_key]).encode()).hexdigest()
    try:
        ddb.put_item(TableName="agent_actions",
                     Item={"pk": {"S": key}, "job": {"S": job_name}, "reason": {"S": reason}, "state": {"S": "STARTING"}},
                     ConditionExpression="attribute_not_exists(pk)")         # first writer wins
    except ClientError as e:
        if e.response["Error"]["Code"] == "ConditionalCheckFailedException":
            prev = ddb.get_item(TableName="agent_actions", Key={"pk": {"S": key}})["Item"]
            return {"status": "already_done", "job_run_id": prev.get("job_run_id", {}).get("S"), "note": "Not started again."}
        raise
    run_id = glue.start_job_run(JobName=job_name, Arguments={"--triggered_by": "agent"})["JobRunId"]
    ddb.update_item(TableName="agent_actions", Key={"pk": {"S": key}},
                    UpdateExpression="SET job_run_id = :r, #s = :s", ExpressionAttributeNames={"#s": "state"},
                    ExpressionAttributeValues={":r": {"S": run_id}, ":s": {"S": "STARTED"}})
    return {"status": "started", "job_run_id": run_id}

      This is the same conditional-write pattern you'd use for exactly-once processing in a pipeline. Say that in an interview: it shows your data-engineering habits carry over.

      Quotas and throughput planning

      • Bedrock on-demand quotas are per model, per Region: requests per minute and tokens per minute. Agents burn tokens per minute fast because each turn re-sends the context.
      • Estimate: peak requests/min × average total tokens per request × average turns. Compare with your quota before launch, not after.
      • Options when you're short: quota increase, cross-Region inference profile, queue and smooth with SQS, smaller model for sub-steps, batch for offline work, provisioned or reserved capacity for steady high load.
      • Protect yourself from your own users: per-user and per-tenant rate limits and token budgets at the API layer.
      Interview: How do you make an LLM-based service reliable?

      "I assume every model and tool call can fail. Calls go through one wrapper with timeouts, retries with exponential backoff and jitter for throttling and transient errors, and no retry for validation or permission errors. There's a fallback chain to a second model or Region with a circuit breaker, and the fallback is tested on the same evals. I check stop reasons so truncated or guardrail-blocked outputs aren't treated as answers, and validate structured output with a repair retry. Write tools are idempotent with keys from the harness, and risky ones need approval. Agent runs have step, token and wall-clock budgets and are checkpointed so they resume after a crash. I plan capacity against tokens-per-minute quotas, queue bursts, and define degraded modes. Then alarms on error rate, latency, cost and eval scores."

      Interview: You get ThrottlingException in production at peak. What are your options?

      "Short term: retry with backoff and jitter, and smooth bursts with a queue so requests wait instead of failing. Check whether it's requests-per-minute or tokens-per-minute; with agents it's usually tokens, so trimming context and caching helps directly. Medium term: request a quota increase, switch to a cross-Region inference profile for more capacity, move sub-steps to a smaller model with its own quota, and push offline work to batch. For predictable high volume, buy provisioned or reserved capacity. I'd also add per-tenant limits so one heavy user can't exhaust the shared quota, and an alarm on throttle rate."

      Production · process

      Shipping and operating: the LLMOps release loop

      Prompts, models, tools and retrieval settings are all "code" that changes behaviour. They need the same discipline as a pipeline release: versions, tests, staged rollout, monitoring and rollback. This is the process to describe when asked "how do you take an agent to production?"

      What to version

      ArtefactWhere it livesNote
      Prompts and tool descriptionsGit (or a prompt registry with version IDs)Reviewed like code; the version ID is logged with every request
      Model ID / inference profileConfig, pinned to an exact versionNever "latest". Model upgrades are releases that must pass evals
      Retrieval configConfig: embedding model, chunking, top-k, thresholdsIndex is versioned with the embedding model
      Guardrail configBedrock Guardrails versionUse numbered versions, not the working draft
      Eval dataset and gradersGit / S3 with a versionSo scores are comparable across runs
      Agent code and containerGit + ECR image tag; AgentCore Runtime version and endpointEndpoints let you point "prod" at a tested version and roll back by re-pointing

      Production readiness checklist

      Quality

      • Eval set of 50+ cases incl. edge and adversarial
      • CI gate with thresholds
      • Pass rate measured over several trials
      • Human-reviewed sample each week

      Safety and security

      • Least-privilege IAM role per agent
      • Guardrails on input and output; PII handling
      • Untrusted content treated as data
      • Approval for destructive or external actions
      • Access control in retrieval and tools, tested
      • Audit log of actions with user identity

      Reliability

      • Timeouts, retries, fallback, circuit breaker
      • Step, token and time budgets per run
      • Idempotent write tools
      • Checkpointed long runs
      • Load tested against quotas

      Operations

      • Traces with prompt/model/version IDs
      • Dashboards: latency, error rate, tokens, cost per request
      • Alarms on the above and on online eval scores
      • Feedback capture (thumbs, edits, escalations)
      • Runbook and rollback tested
      • Cost budget and per-tenant limits

      Rollout techniques

      Shadow mode
      The new version runs on real traffic but its output is only logged and scored, never shown. Zero user risk; best first step for model upgrades.
      Canary
      Send a small share of traffic to the new version, compare metrics, widen gradually.
      A/B test
      Split traffic and compare a business metric (resolution rate, edits, escalations), not just eval scores.
      Feature flag / kill switch
      Turn off a tool or the whole agent without a deploy.
      Rollback
      Re-point the endpoint or flag to the previous prompt, model and index versions together, since they were tested as a set.

      What to watch after launch

      SignalWhy
      Online eval scores on sampled traffic (faithfulness, helpfulness, goal success)Quality drift that latency and error dashboards can't see
      Refusal / "I don't know" rateRising means corpus gaps or over-strict guardrails
      Steps and tokens per run, tool error rateEarly sign of loops or a broken tool
      User feedback, edit rate, escalation to humanThe real outcome metric
      Cost per request and per tenantBudget control; spot abuse
      Index freshness lagStale answers
      Guardrail intervention rateAttacks or false positives
      On AWS

      AgentCore Observability sends OpenTelemetry traces to CloudWatch; AgentCore Evaluations can score sampled production sessions (online) and datasets (on-demand and batch), including the failure-analysis evaluator that groups failed sessions by root cause. Bedrock model invocation logging captures prompts and responses to S3 or CloudWatch Logs; treat those logs as sensitive data.

      Interview: A new model version is out and the team wants to switch. What's your process?

      "I treat it as a release. First I run the full offline eval set on the new model with current prompts and compare to the baseline per metric and per slice, plus cost and latency. Prompts tuned for the old model often need adjusting, so I iterate there. Then shadow traffic: the new model processes real requests but only logs, and online evaluators score both. Then a canary at a few percent with alarms on quality, error rate, latency and cost, widening in steps. Model ID is pinned in config so rollback is one change, and I keep the old version available until the new one has been stable. I'd also check the fallback chain and structured-output behaviour, since those break quietly."

      Evals

      What evals are and how they're run

      An eval is an automated test for an AI system: a dataset of inputs, the system's outputs, and a scorer that says how good each output is. Because LLM outputs vary, evals measure rates and averages across many cases rather than asserting one exact string.

      The four parts of every eval

      Kinds of evals

      KindWhen it runsPurpose
      Offline / regressionEvery PR and before release, on a fixed golden datasetCatch quality drops from prompt, model, tool or code changes
      OnlineContinuously on a sample of production tracesDetect drift and real-world failures; no expected answers, so judges and heuristics
      Unit-levelPer componentRetriever recall, a tool's argument accuracy, a router's classification accuracy
      End-to-endWhole taskDid the user's goal get done correctly, safely, within budget?
      Red-team / safetyBefore launch and periodicallyPrompt injection, data leakage, harmful output, policy bypass
      Human reviewSmall samples, calibrationGround truth for judges; subjective quality

      Scorer types

      Deterministic code
      Exact match, regex, JSON schema valid, SQL executes, row counts equal, tool called with correct args, latency under 5 s. Cheapest and most reliable; use whenever possible.
      Reference metrics
      Compare to expected answer: F1, semantic similarity, retrieval recall@k.
      LLM-as-judge
      A model scores against a rubric ("Is every claim supported by the context? 1–5 with reason"). Scales to subjective criteria; must be calibrated against human labels.
      Pairwise
      Judge picks the better of two outputs (A/B of prompt versions). More reliable than absolute scores.

      Building the golden dataset

      1. Start with 30–50 real questions from users, logs or SMEs, not invented ones.
      2. Cover categories in proportion: easy/hard, each intent, each data source, edge cases, "should refuse" cases, injection attempts.
      3. For each: input, expected answer or facts, expected sources/tools, and a rubric.
      4. Version it in Git (JSONL). Add every production failure you fix. Grow toward 150–500.
      5. Optionally generate synthetic cases with an LLM from your documents, then have a human review them.
      golden.jsonl (one case)JSON
      {"id": "dq-017", "category": "incident", "input": "Why did qm_trusted rows drop on 2026-11-02?",
 "expected_facts": ["only 23 of 48 source files arrived", "upstream SFTP delay"],
 "expected_tools": ["get_table_row_counts", "get_upstream_file_count"],
 "must_not": ["rerun_glue_job"], "rubric": "Names upstream file shortfall as cause; proposes checking SFTP"}

      LLM-as-judge, done properly

      A rubric judge with Converse and forced structured outputPython
      JUDGE_SCHEMA = {"type": "object", "properties": {
    "score": {"type": "integer", "minimum": 1, "maximum": 5},
    "reason": {"type": "string"},
    "unsupported_claims": {"type": "array", "items": {"type": "string"}}},
    "required": ["score", "reason"]}

def judge(question, answer, context, rubric, judge_model=os.environ["JUDGE_MODEL_ID"]):
    prompt = f"""You are grading an AI assistant's answer. Be strict.
<question>{question}</question>
<context>{context}</context>
<answer>{answer}</answer>
<rubric>{rubric}</rubric>
Score 1-5: 5 = fully correct, every claim supported by the context; 3 = partly correct or has unsupported claims;
1 = wrong or unsafe. Think about each claim before scoring."""
    r = brt.converse(modelId=judge_model, messages=[{"role": "user", "content": [{"text": prompt}]}],
                     inferenceConfig={"temperature": 0},
                     toolConfig={"tools": [{"toolSpec": {"name": "grade", "description": "Record the grade",
                                                         "inputSchema": {"json": JUDGE_SCHEMA}}}],
                                 "toolChoice": {"tool": {"name": "grade"}}})
    return next(b["toolUse"]["input"] for b in r["output"]["message"]["content"] if "toolUse" in b)
      Judge pitfalls

      Judges favour longer answers, their own model family, and the first option shown. Mitigate: specific rubrics with examples per score, binary or 3-point scales over 10-point, swap order in pairwise tests, use a different (often stronger) model than the one being tested, and check agreement with 50–100 human labels before trusting it.

      Interview: How do you evaluate an LLM application?

      "I start with a golden set of real inputs labelled with expected answers, sources and tools, split by category. Each change runs the set offline: deterministic checks first (format, SQL runs, right tool), then component metrics like retrieval recall and faithfulness, then an LLM judge with a rubric calibrated on human labels. Results are compared to the last release, and CI blocks merges if key metrics drop beyond a threshold. In production I trace everything and score a sample online, and every confirmed failure becomes a new test case. I also track cost and latency as first-class metrics."

      Evals

      Evaluating RAG

      Measure retrieval and generation separately. Most RAG failures are retrieval failures, and you can't fix what you don't isolate.

      Retrieval metrics (need labelled relevant chunks)

      Retrieval metrics calculatorLive lab

      Click results to mark them relevant (✓). The question has 3 relevant chunks in total.

      Recall@k
      Of all relevant chunks, what fraction are in the top k? The key metric: if it's not retrieved, the model can't use it.
      Precision@k
      Of the top k, what fraction are relevant? Low precision means noisy context.
      MRR
      Mean of 1/rank of the first relevant result. Rewards getting a good chunk to the top.
      nDCG@k
      Rank-weighted relevance, supports graded relevance. Standard for comparing rankers.
      Hit rate
      Did at least one relevant chunk appear in top k?

      Generation metrics (RAGAS-style)

      MetricQuestion it answersNeeds reference answer?
      Faithfulness (groundedness)Are all claims in the answer supported by the retrieved context?No
      Answer relevancyDoes the answer address the question?No
      Context precisionAre the relevant chunks ranked high in the context?Yes (or judge)
      Context recallDoes the context contain everything needed for the reference answer?Yes
      Answer correctnessDoes it match the reference answer's facts?Yes
      Citation accuracyDo cited sources actually support the cited claims?No
      RAGAS with Bedrock models (pip install ragas langchain-aws)Python
      from ragas import EvaluationDataset, evaluate
from ragas.metrics import Faithfulness, ResponseRelevancy, LLMContextRecall, LLMContextPrecisionWithReference
from ragas.llms import LangchainLLMWrapper
from ragas.embeddings import LangchainEmbeddingsWrapper
from langchain_aws import ChatBedrockConverse, BedrockEmbeddings

judge_llm = LangchainLLMWrapper(ChatBedrockConverse(model=os.environ["JUDGE_MODEL_ID"], temperature=0))
judge_emb = LangchainEmbeddingsWrapper(BedrockEmbeddings(model_id="amazon.titan-embed-text-v2:0"))

rows = []
for case in golden:                                   # your labelled questions
    hits = retrieve(case["input"])
    rows.append({"user_input": case["input"], "retrieved_contexts": [h["text"] for h in hits],
                 "response": answer(case["input"]), "reference": case["reference_answer"]})

result = evaluate(EvaluationDataset.from_list(rows),
                  metrics=[Faithfulness(), ResponseRelevancy(), LLMContextRecall(), LLMContextPrecisionWithReference()],
                  llm=judge_llm, embeddings=judge_emb)
print(result)                     # e.g. {'faithfulness': 0.91, 'answer_relevancy': 0.88, 'context_recall': 0.79, ...}
result.to_pandas().to_csv("rag_eval.csv")
      Version note (checked Nov 2026)

      ragas 0.4 still accepts these imports but marks them deprecated in favour of ragas.metrics.collections. In a fresh environment with the newest LangChain packages, ragas 0.4.3 failed to import because of a removed langchain_community module. Keep evals in their own pinned virtual environment, and don't let an eval library dictate your app's LangChain version.

      On AWS

      Bedrock Evaluations has RAG evaluation jobs for Knowledge Bases or your own RAG outputs (retrieve-only or retrieve-and-generate) with LLM-as-judge metrics such as correctness, completeness, faithfulness, citation precision and coverage. AgentCore Evaluations covers agents.

      Interview: Faithfulness is 0.95 but users say answers are wrong. What's happening?

      "Faithfulness only checks that the answer is supported by the retrieved context, not that the context was the right context. If retrieval brings back outdated or off-topic chunks, the model can faithfully repeat wrong information. I'd check context recall and answer correctness against references, look for stale documents in the index, missing filters such as region or version, and whether the right document exists at all."

      Evals

      Evaluating agents

      Agents add two dimensions: the path they take (trajectory) and how consistent they are over repeated runs. A correct final answer reached by deleting a table is still a failure.

      What to measure

      LevelMetricHow
      OutcomeTask success / goal completionCheck end state (row exists, ticket created, query result matches) or judge vs rubric
      TrajectoryRight tools, right order, no forbidden tools, no redundant callsCompare the tool-call sequence to expected (exact, in-order, any-order, subset)
      Tool callsTool selection accuracy, argument correctness, error ratePer-call checks on traces
      SafetyPolicy violations, injection success rate, PII leaksRed-team set; guardrail logs
      EfficiencyTurns, tokens, cost, latency per taskFrom traces
      Reliabilitypass@k, pass^kRun each task k times
      ConversationHelpfulness, asking clarification when needed, multi-turn coherenceSimulated users (LLM plays the user) + judges
      pass@k vs pass^k: why consistency mattersLive lab

      pass@k = chance at least one of k tries succeeds = 1 − (1 − p)k. Good for "can it ever do this?" (code generation with tests). pass^k = chance all k tries succeed = pk. Good for "can customers rely on it?" (τ-bench popularised this for agents). An 80% agent succeeds on all 8 attempts only about 17% of the time.

      A trajectory + outcome eval harness with pytest (CI-ready)Python
      import json, statistics, pytest
from my_agent import run_agent_with_trace     # returns (final_text, [tool_calls], usage)

CASES = [json.loads(l) for l in open("golden.jsonl")]

def tools_in_order(expected: list[str], actual: list[str]) -> bool:
    it = iter(actual)
    return all(name in it for name in expected)          # expected is a subsequence of actual

@pytest.mark.parametrize("case", CASES, ids=lambda c: c["id"])
def test_case(case):
    text, calls, usage = run_agent_with_trace(case["input"])
    names = [c["name"] for c in calls]
    assert not set(case.get("must_not", [])) & set(names), f"forbidden tool used: {names}"
    assert tools_in_order(case.get("expected_tools", []), names), f"trajectory {names}"
    assert usage["turns"] <= 10 and usage["cost_usd"] <= 0.25
    verdict = judge(case["input"], text, context=json.dumps(calls)[:8000], rubric=case["rubric"])
    assert verdict["score"] >= 4, verdict["reason"]

def test_suite_pass_rate(record_property):
    # Run in CI as a separate job: aggregate pass rate must not drop vs main branch baseline
    baseline = json.load(open("eval_baseline.json"))["pass_rate"]
    current = json.load(open("eval_results.json"))["pass_rate"]
    record_property("pass_rate", current)
    assert current >= baseline - 0.02

      Tools for agent evals

      AgentCore Evaluations
      Built-in evaluators (e.g. helpfulness, correctness, goal success, tool selection and parameter accuracy, harmfulness) on AgentCore traces; custom LLM-judge or Lambda code evaluators; ground truth with reference answers, assertions and expected tool sequences; online and on-demand; agentcore eval in CI.
      Strands Evals
      SDK for test cases, LLM judges, trajectory evaluators and simulated users for Strands agents.
      LangSmith
      Datasets, experiments, trajectory evaluators (agentevals), pairwise comparisons, online evaluators on traces.
      Others
      DeepEval (pytest-style), RAGAS (agent and RAG metrics), Langfuse, Arize Phoenix, Promptfoo (red-teaming), OpenAI Evals, Inspect.
      Interview: How do you evaluate a multi-step agent?

      "At three levels. Outcome: did the task end in the right state, checked by code where possible. Trajectory: did it use the expected tools in a sensible order, never call forbidden tools, and avoid loops, compared against expected tool sequences. Step level: tool selection and argument accuracy, error recovery. I add efficiency (turns, tokens, cost, latency) and run each case several times to get pass^k, because users care about consistency. Offline in CI against a golden set, online on sampled traces with AgentCore Evaluations or LangSmith, and failures go back into the set."

      Interview: What's an eval harness vs an agent harness?

      "The agent harness is the runtime around the model that makes it act: loop, tools, context, guardrails. The eval harness is the test rig around the whole agent: it loads the dataset, runs the agent per case in a clean environment with mocked or sandboxed tools, captures traces, applies scorers and compares against a baseline. You need a good eval harness to improve the agent harness safely."

      Evals · project

      Project: build an eval harness with an LLM judge and a CI gate

      Reading about evals is not the same as having them. This project is small enough to finish in an evening and gives you something few candidates can show: a repository where a pull request that makes the agent worse fails the build.

      The pieces

      1. A dataset of cases in JSONL, in Git.
      2. A runner that calls your system for each case and records the output and trace facts.
      3. Graders: code checks where possible, an LLM judge where meaning matters.
      4. An aggregate report and thresholds.
      5. A CI job that fails when a threshold is missed.
      evals/cases.jsonl: one case per line; mix easy, hard, edge and "should refuse"JSON
      {"id": "oom-1", "question": "Glue job fails with java.lang.OutOfMemoryError. What should I change?", "expected_sources": ["glue-oom.md"], "must_include": ["G.2X"], "reference": "Move workers from G.1X to G.2X and avoid collect() on large DataFrames."}
{"id": "inc-1", "question": "What happens if the DynamoDB watermark is missing?", "expected_sources": ["incremental.md"], "must_include": ["full load"], "reference": "The job performs a full load."}
{"id": "neg-1", "question": "What is our parental leave policy?", "expected_sources": [], "must_refuse": true, "reference": "Not covered by the runbooks; the assistant should say it doesn't know."}

      Code graders: fast, free, deterministic

      evals/metrics.py (pure Python; run it)Python
      def recall_at_k(retrieved: list[str], expected: list[str], k: int = 5) -> float | None:
    if not expected:
        return None                                   # not applicable (e.g. refusal cases)
    top = [r.split("#")[0] for r in retrieved[:k]]
    return sum(e in top for e in expected) / len(expected)

def mrr(retrieved: list[str], expected: list[str]) -> float | None:
    if not expected:
        return None
    for rank, r in enumerate(retrieved, 1):
        if r.split("#")[0] in expected:
            return 1 / rank
    return 0.0

def must_include(answer: str, phrases: list[str]) -> bool:
    return all(p.lower() in answer.lower() for p in phrases)

def refused(answer: str) -> bool:
    return any(s in answer.lower() for s in ["i don't know", "not covered", "couldn't find", "no information"])

def mean(xs):
    xs = [x for x in xs if x is not None]
    return round(sum(xs) / len(xs), 3) if xs else None

if __name__ == "__main__":
    got = ["iceberg.md#0", "glue-oom.md#0", "incremental.md#0"]
    print(recall_at_k(got, ["glue-oom.md"], k=1), recall_at_k(got, ["glue-oom.md"], k=3), mrr(got, ["glue-oom.md"]))
    print(must_include("Switch to G.2X workers.", ["g.2x"]), refused("I don't know based on the indexed documents."))
      OutputOutput

      The LLM judge: for meaning, with a rubric and structured output

      evals/judge.pyPython
      import os, boto3

brt = boto3.client("bedrock-runtime", region_name="us-east-1")
JUDGE_MODEL = os.environ.get("JUDGE_MODEL_ID", os.environ["BEDROCK_MODEL_ID"])   # ideally a different, strong model

RUBRIC = """You are grading an assistant's answer for a data-engineering help desk.

<question>{question}</question>
<sources>{sources}</sources>
<reference_answer>{reference}</reference_answer>
<answer>{answer}</answer>

Grade two things independently.
1. faithfulness: is every factual claim in the answer supported by the sources?
   pass = all claims supported (or the answer correctly says it doesn't know); fail = any unsupported claim.
2. correctness: does the answer agree with the reference answer on the key facts?
   pass = same key facts (wording may differ); fail = missing or contradicting a key fact.
Think step by step in `reasoning` first, then give the verdicts."""

TOOL = {"toolSpec": {"name": "grade", "description": "Record the grades.", "inputSchema": {"json": {
    "type": "object",
    "properties": {"reasoning": {"type": "string"},
                   "faithfulness": {"type": "string", "enum": ["pass", "fail"]},
                   "correctness": {"type": "string", "enum": ["pass", "fail"]}},
    "required": ["reasoning", "faithfulness", "correctness"]}}}}

def judge(question: str, sources: str, reference: str, answer: str) -> dict:
    resp = brt.converse(
        modelId=JUDGE_MODEL,
        messages=[{"role": "user", "content": [{"text": RUBRIC.format(
            question=question, sources=sources, reference=reference, answer=answer)}]}],
        toolConfig={"tools": [TOOL], "toolChoice": {"tool": {"name": "grade"}}},
        inferenceConfig={"temperature": 0, "maxTokens": 600})
    return next(b["toolUse"]["input"] for b in resp["output"]["message"]["content"] if "toolUse" in b)
      Judges need judging

      Before trusting the judge, label 30–50 answers by hand and measure how often the judge agrees with you. If agreement is under about 85–90%, fix the rubric: make criteria binary, define pass and fail with examples, grade one thing per criterion. Use pass/fail rather than 1–10 scales; nobody, human or model, can say what separates a 6 from a 7. Re-check agreement whenever you change the judge model.

      Runner and thresholds

      evals/run.py: run every case, grade, print a table, write a JSON reportPython
      import json, sys
from concurrent.futures import ThreadPoolExecutor
from metrics import recall_at_k, mrr, must_include, refused, mean
from judge import judge
from app import ask          # your system under test: returns {"answer": str, "sources": [{"uri":..., "text":...}]}

THRESHOLDS = {"recall@5": 0.85, "faithfulness": 0.95, "correctness": 0.80, "refusal_accuracy": 1.0}

def run_case(case: dict) -> dict:
    out = ask(case["question"])
    retrieved = [s["uri"].split("/")[-1] for s in out["sources"]]
    row = {"id": case["id"], "recall@5": recall_at_k(retrieved, case.get("expected_sources", [])),
           "mrr": mrr(retrieved, case.get("expected_sources", []))}
    if case.get("must_refuse"):
        row["refusal_accuracy"] = float(refused(out["answer"]))
    else:
        row["includes"] = float(must_include(out["answer"], case.get("must_include", [])))
        g = judge(case["question"], "\n".join(s["text"] for s in out["sources"]), case["reference"], out["answer"])
        row["faithfulness"], row["correctness"] = float(g["faithfulness"] == "pass"), float(g["correctness"] == "pass")
        row["judge_reasoning"] = g["reasoning"]
    return row

def main(path="evals/cases.jsonl") -> int:
    cases = [json.loads(line) for line in open(path) if line.strip()]
    with ThreadPoolExecutor(max_workers=4) as pool:
        rows = list(pool.map(run_case, cases))
    summary = {m: mean([r.get(m) for r in rows]) for m in ["recall@5", "mrr", "includes", "faithfulness", "correctness", "refusal_accuracy"]}
    json.dump({"summary": summary, "rows": rows}, open("evals/report.json", "w"), indent=2)
    failed = {m: v for m, v in summary.items() if m in THRESHOLDS and v is not None and v < THRESHOLDS[m]}
    for m, v in summary.items():
        print(f"{m:18} {v}   {'FAIL < ' + str(THRESHOLDS[m]) if m in failed else ''}")
    for r in rows:
        if r.get("faithfulness") == 0 or r.get("correctness") == 0:
            print("  failing case:", r["id"], "-", r.get("judge_reasoning", "")[:160])
    return 1 if failed else 0

if __name__ == "__main__":
    sys.exit(main())

      Growing the dataset

      • Start with 20–30 cases written by hand from real questions. A small honest set beats a large synthetic one.
      • Every production failure becomes a case before it is fixed.
      • Cover slices: easy, multi-hop, exact-identifier, out-of-scope, adversarial (prompt injection in a document), other languages.
      • Synthetic cases (ask a model to write questions from your documents) are fine for volume, but review them and keep a human-written core.
      • Hold out some cases you never look at while tuning prompts, so you don't overfit to the test.
      • For agents, add trajectory facts to each row: tools called, number of steps, tokens, and run each case 3–5 times to get a pass rate.
      What to put in the README

      A table: baseline RAG vs + hybrid vs + rerank vs agentic, with recall@5, faithfulness, correctness, p95 latency and cost per question. That one table shows you measure, compare and make trade-offs, which is what senior interviews are testing.

      Interview: How would you set up evaluation for a RAG or agent system from scratch?

      "I'd start by reading real outputs and collecting 20 to 30 representative cases with expected results, including ones that should be refused. I'd write code graders first: retrieval recall and MRR against expected sources, required phrases, valid schema, tool calls made. For meaning, an LLM judge with a binary rubric per criterion, such as faithfulness and correctness, returning structured output with reasoning first; I'd calibrate it against my own labels until agreement is high. A runner executes all cases, writes a report, and CI fails the pull request if any metric falls below its threshold. For agents I'd also grade the trajectory and run several trials per case for a pass rate. In production I'd sample live traffic for online scoring and turn every failure into a new case."

      Interview: What are the weaknesses of LLM-as-judge, and how do you handle them?

      "Judges are models, so they're inconsistent, can be biased toward longer or more confident answers or toward their own model family, and can be fooled by text in the answer. Vague rubrics and numeric scales make it worse. I handle it by using binary criteria with explicit definitions and examples, one criterion per verdict, asking for reasoning before the verdict, temperature zero, and structured output. I validate the judge against human labels and report agreement, use a different and strong model as judge where possible, and keep code-based checks for anything that can be checked exactly. For close calls or high stakes, humans review a sample."

      Build your portfolio

      Six portfolio projects

      Two or three of these, finished properly, beat fifty cloned tutorials. Each is chosen to show what Agentic AI Data Engineer roles screen for: real data, AWS deployment, evals with numbers, and cost awareness. Use public data (AWS docs, NYC taxi, TPC-DS, GitHub repos) so you can publish them.

      1. Lakehouse text-to-SQL agent with guardrails

      Build
      Agent that answers business questions over a Glue Catalog / Athena dataset (TPC-DS or NYC taxi). Tools: find_tables (searches catalog metadata via embeddings), get_schema, run_readonly_sql (row cap, cost check with EXPLAIN), plot_result.
      Shows
      Schema retrieval (RAG over metadata), SQL validation loop (evaluator-optimiser), least-privilege tools, LangGraph with HITL for expensive queries.
      Evals
      100 questions with gold SQL; execution accuracy (result sets match), invalid-SQL rate, cost per question.
      Stack
      LangGraph or Strands, Bedrock Converse, Athena read-only workgroup, Lake Formation, AgentCore Runtime.

      2. Production RAG with an incremental indexing pipeline

      Build
      RAG over AWS documentation or a public wiki dump. Event-driven ingestion (S3 → EventBridge → SQS → Lambda), hash-based change detection, deletes, metadata ACL filters, hybrid search + reranking.
      Shows
      Your data-engineering strength applied to AI: idempotency, manifests, DQ checks, re-index strategy.
      Evals
      Recall@5, MRR, faithfulness, answer correctness on 150 questions; before/after tables for hybrid and rerank.
      Stack
      OpenSearch Serverless or S3 Vectors, Titan embeddings, LlamaIndex or custom, RAGAS, Terraform.

      3. Pipeline failure triage agent

      Build
      On a Step Functions or Glue failure event, an agent pulls the error, CloudWatch log excerpts, recent config changes and similar past incidents, then posts a root-cause hypothesis and suggested fix to Slack, with a button to rerun (approval required).
      Shows
      Event-driven agents, agentic RAG over incidents, HITL, Gateway tools from Lambdas.
      Evals
      Replay 40 historical failures; root-cause accuracy judged against the real resolution; time to triage.

      4. Legacy ETL migration agent (Alteryx/SQL → PySpark)

      Build
      Parse a workflow definition, produce a mapping document for review, then generate PySpark Glue code in a house style, run it on sample data and compare outputs to the legacy results until they match.
      Shows
      Loop engineering: worker + verifier (data diff) + budgets; human review gates; skills for coding standards.
      Evals
      Percentage of workflows converted with matching outputs on first try and within N iterations; reviewer edits needed.

      5. Multi-agent PR reviewer

      Build
      Supervisor + specialist reviewers (security, performance, standards per stack, tests), SAST tool results as inputs, risk score, approval gate, GitHub comments.
      Shows
      Multi-agent orchestration, structured outputs, harness engineering, A2A or MCP integration.

      6. MCP server for your data platform

      Shows
      Tool design, protocol knowledge (2026 spec), auth, reuse across frameworks.

      What a winning README contains

      1. One-paragraph problem statement and a 60-second demo GIF or video.
      2. Architecture diagram and why each choice was made (and what you rejected).
      3. Eval table: metrics before/after each improvement, with dataset size.
      4. Cost and latency per request, and how you reduced them.

      Build your portfolio

      Practice exercises with solutions

      Short, focused exercises that join the dots between lessons. Try each before opening the solution. They're also common live-coding interview tasks.

      1. Add parallel tool execution to the hand-written loop

      Models can return several toolUse blocks in one turn. Run them concurrently.

      Solution
      Run tool calls in parallel with a thread poolPython
      from concurrent.futures import ThreadPoolExecutor

def run_call(block):
    call = block["toolUse"]
    try:
        out = TOOLS[call["name"]](**call["input"])
        return {"toolResult": {"toolUseId": call["toolUseId"], "content": [{"json": {"result": out}}]}}
    except Exception as e:
        return {"toolResult": {"toolUseId": call["toolUseId"], "status": "error", "content": [{"text": str(e)}]}}

calls = [b for b in msg["content"] if "toolUse" in b]
with ThreadPoolExecutor(max_workers=8) as pool:
    results = list(pool.map(run_call, calls))       # order preserved
messages.append({"role": "user", "content": results})

      2. Detect a stuck agent

      Stop when the agent calls the same tool with the same arguments three times.

      Solution
      Repeat-call detectorPython
      from collections import Counter
seen = Counter()
def is_stuck(name: str, args: dict, limit: int = 3) -> bool:
    key = (name, json.dumps(args, sort_keys=True))
    seen[key] += 1
    return seen[key] >= limit

      3. Truncate large tool outputs safely

      Keep tool results under ~4,000 characters but tell the model it was truncated.

      Solution
      Truncation with a notePython
      def clip(text: str, limit: int = 4000) -> str:
    if len(text) <= limit:
        return text
    head, tail = text[: limit * 3 // 4], text[-limit // 4 :]
    return f"{head}\n... [truncated {len(text) - limit} chars; refine the query to see more] ...\n{tail}"

      4. Query rewriting for follow-up questions

      Turn "what about Italy?" into a standalone search query using the last few messages.

      Solution
      Standalone query rewriterPython
      def rewrite(history: list[str], question: str) -> str:
    prompt = ("Rewrite the final question as a standalone search query. Keep names, dates and IDs. "
              "Return only the query.\n\n" + "\n".join(history[-6:]) + f"\nFinal question: {question}")
    r = brt.converse(modelId=SMALL_MODEL, messages=[{"role": "user", "content": [{"text": prompt}]}],
                     inferenceConfig={"temperature": 0, "maxTokens": 100})
    return r["output"]["message"]["content"][0]["text"].strip()

      5. Validate generated SQL before running it

      Reject anything that isn't a single SELECT, add a LIMIT if missing, and check Athena will scan under 10 GB.

      Solution
      SQL guard with sqlglotPython
      import sqlglot
from sqlglot import exp

def guard_sql(sql: str, max_rows: int = 200) -> str:
    stmts = sqlglot.parse(sql, read="athena")
    if len(stmts) != 1 or not isinstance(stmts[0], (exp.Select, exp.Union)):
        raise ValueError("Only one SELECT statement is allowed")
    tree = stmts[0]
    if isinstance(tree, exp.Select) and not tree.args.get("limit"):
        tree = tree.limit(max_rows)
    return tree.sql(dialect="athena")
# Then run in a workgroup with BytesScannedCutoffPerQuery set (enforced by Athena, not by the model).

      6. Reciprocal Rank Fusion

      Solution
      RRF in five linesPython
      def rrf(rankings: list[list[str]], k: int = 60) -> list[tuple[str, float]]:
    scores = {}
    for ranking in rankings:
        for rank, doc in enumerate(ranking, start=1):
            scores[doc] = scores.get(doc, 0) + 1 / (k + rank)
    return sorted(scores.items(), key=lambda x: -x[1])

      7. Cosine similarity and top-k without libraries

      Solution
      Pure PythonPython
      import math, heapq
def cosine(a, b):
    dot = sum(x * y for x, y in zip(a, b))
    return dot / (math.sqrt(sum(x * x for x in a)) * math.sqrt(sum(y * y for y in b)))
def top_k(query, vectors: dict, k=3):
    return heapq.nlargest(k, ((cosine(query, v), doc) for doc, v in vectors.items()))

      8. Streaming a LangGraph agent to a web client

      Solution
      FastAPI + server-sent eventsPython
      from fastapi import FastAPI
from fastapi.responses import StreamingResponse
api = FastAPI()

@api.get("/chat")
def chat(q: str, thread: str):
    def gen():
        for chunk, meta in app.stream({"messages": [("user", q)]},
                                      {"configurable": {"thread_id": thread}}, stream_mode="messages"):
            if getattr(chunk, "content", None):
                yield f"data: {json.dumps({'node': meta['langgraph_node'], 'text': chunk.text})}\n\n"
    return StreamingResponse(gen(), media_type="text/event-stream")

      Interview prep

      Question bank: 45 questions

      Grouped the way interview loops usually run. Answer out loud first, then open the model answer. Many more are at the end of each lesson.

      LLM fundamentals

      1. What is a token and why does it matter for cost and limits?

      A sub-word unit from the model's vocabulary. Pricing, context windows and rate limits are all in tokens, output tokens cost more and are slower, and non-English text and code use more tokens per character.

      2. Encoder vs decoder models?

      Encoders (BERT) read the whole input bidirectionally and are used for embeddings, classification and reranking. Decoders (GPT, Claude, Llama) use causal attention and generate text token by token. Original Transformer had both for translation.

      3. What does temperature do? What would you set for SQL generation?

      Scales logits before softmax; low values concentrate probability on top tokens. For SQL, extraction and tool use: 0–0.2. Note reasoning models may fix it.

      4. What are reasoning models and when are they worth it?

      Models trained (often with RL on verifiable rewards) to generate internal reasoning before answering. Worth it for multi-step planning, maths, complex code, hard tool orchestration; not for simple lookups or latency-critical paths, since thinking tokens cost time and money.

      5. How do you get reliable JSON?

      Structured outputs: JSON schema or forced tool call, validated by Pydantic; retry with the validation error on failure. Not regex over free text.

      6. What is prompt caching and how do you design for it?

      Reusing computed KV for identical prompt prefixes; cheaper and faster input. Put stable content first (system prompt, tool specs, long documents) and variable content last; mark cache points; verify with cache-read token counts.

      7. Explain hallucination and three mitigations.

      Fluent but unsupported output, because the model optimises plausibility. Mitigate with grounding (RAG + citations + "say I don't know"), tool verification (run the SQL, check the API), and evaluation of faithfulness; plus lower temperature and contextual grounding guardrails.

      RAG

      8. Walk me through a RAG pipeline end to end.

      Offline: ingest, parse, clean, chunk with metadata, embed, index (vector + keyword). Online: rewrite query, hybrid retrieve top 30, filter by ACL/metadata, rerank to top 5, build prompt with tagged sources, generate with citations, validate grounding, log everything, evaluate.

      9. How do you pick chunk size?

      Start with structure-aware 300–800 tokens and 10–15% overlap, then tune against retrieval recall on a labelled set; use parent-child if you need both precision and context.

      10. Why hybrid search?

      Vectors capture meaning but miss exact identifiers; BM25 catches codes, names and rare terms. Fusing (RRF) gives better recall than either.

      11. What is a reranker and what does it cost?

      A cross-encoder that scores query and chunk together; much more accurate than embedding similarity but slower, so you apply it to a small candidate set. Adds 100–500 ms and per-document cost.

      12. How do you handle document permissions in RAG?

      Store ACL metadata on each chunk from the source system, resolve the user's groups at query time, filter in the vector query. Never rely on the prompt. Re-sync ACLs on change; test with users from different groups in evals.

      13. How do you keep the index fresh?

      Event-driven or scheduled delta sync, content hashes to skip unchanged docs, deterministic chunk IDs for upserts, explicit deletes, a manifest table, and freshness monitoring (max lag, counts reconciliation).

      14. When would you use GraphRAG?

      When questions need relationships across many documents or corpus-wide themes ("which suppliers are connected to the delayed shipments?"), and vector RAG measurably fails. It costs more to build and maintain.

      15. RAG vs fine-tuning?

      RAG for knowledge (fresh, citable, permissioned). Fine-tuning for behaviour, format, style or making a small model good at a narrow task. Often both.

      16. How do you evaluate retrieval separately from generation?

      Retrieval: recall@k, MRR, nDCG on labelled relevant chunks. Generation: faithfulness, answer relevancy, correctness vs reference. Diagnose a wrong answer by checking if the right chunk was retrieved.

      Agents

      17. What is an agent? How is it different from a chain?

      An LLM that chooses its own next action in a loop using tools until a goal is met. A chain or workflow follows a path fixed in code.

      18. Explain ReAct.

      Interleaved Thought → Action → Observation; observations ground the next reasoning step. Today implemented with native tool calls in a loop with limits.

      19. How does tool calling work at the API level?

      Send tool specs (name, description, JSON schema). Model returns tool-use blocks and a tool_use stop reason. App executes, returns tool results with matching IDs, calls the model again.

      20. How do you design tools for agents?

      Few, task-level tools with clear descriptions, typed validated inputs, small outputs, informative errors, read-only by default, idempotent, with approvals for writes.

      21. Single agent vs multi-agent?

      Prefer one agent with good tools. Use multi-agent for parallel breadth-first work, separate permission scopes, or very different expertise; accept higher token cost and coordination risk.

      22. What is MCP and why did it win?

      An open protocol for apps to discover and call tools, resources and prompts in servers. It turned N×M integrations into N+M, every major vendor adopted it, and it's governed by a neutral foundation.

      23. What changed in the July 2026 MCP spec?

      The base protocol became stateless (no initialize handshake or session ID) so servers scale behind ordinary load balancers; a Tasks extension handles long-running calls via task handles; MCP Apps return UI; a formal extensions framework; OAuth/OIDC-aligned auth.

      24. MCP vs A2A?

      MCP: agent ↔ tools/data. A2A: agent ↔ agent (discovery via Agent Cards, task delegation, streaming, artefacts).

      25. What is context engineering?

      Curating everything in the context window at each step (instructions, retrieved facts, history, tool results, memory) so the model has what it needs and nothing distracting; includes compaction, clearing, just-in-time retrieval, subagents.

      26. What is harness engineering?

      Engineering the runtime around the model: loop, tools, context, verification, guardrails, budgets, observability. Fix mistakes by changing the harness so they can't recur.

      27. What is loop engineering?

      Designing outer loops that drive an agent toward a verifiable goal without a human prompting each step: goal, independent verifier, feedback, stop conditions (success, max iterations, budget, no progress), checkpoints and human approval for irreversible steps.

      28. Types of agent memory?

      Short-term (conversation/state), long-term semantic (facts), episodic (past sessions), procedural (instructions, skills), preferences. Implemented with checkpointers, stores, AgentCore Memory strategies.

      29. How do you add human-in-the-loop?

      Pause before risky tools: LangGraph interrupt + checkpointer + resume with Command; LangChain HumanInTheLoopMiddleware; Strands hooks or interrupts; AgentCore Policy for rule-based gates. Show the proposed action and arguments, allow approve/edit/reject, log the decision.

      30. How do you secure an agent against prompt injection?

      Assume it will happen and limit blast radius: untrusted content labelled as data, least-privilege identity, no arbitrary egress, policy checks and approvals outside the model, guardrail scanning, audit logs, injection cases in evals.

      Frameworks and AWS

      31. LangChain 1.x vs LangGraph?

      LangChain gives create_agent, integrations and middleware for standard agents; it runs on LangGraph. Use LangGraph directly for custom graphs: explicit branching, cycles with checks, durable state, interrupts, parallel fan-out.

      32. When Strands, when LangGraph?

      Strands: model-driven, quick to build, AWS-native defaults, MCP/A2A, OTel, easy AgentCore deploy. LangGraph: explicit control flow, complex durable workflows with HITL. Both deploy on AgentCore.

      33. What does CrewAI do differently?

      Role-based agents (role, goal, backstory) collaborating on tasks in sequential or hierarchical processes, plus Flows for deterministic event-driven orchestration around crews.

      34. Where does LlamaIndex shine?

      Data ingestion, parsing and retrieval: loaders, node parsers, index types, query engines, routers, sub-question decomposition, LlamaParse; Workflows for event-driven pipelines.

      35. Converse vs InvokeModel vs Responses/Chat Completions on Bedrock?

      Converse: uniform interface for all chat models with tools and Guardrails. Invoke: provider-native body. OpenAI-compatible Chat Completions/Responses: reuse OpenAI SDK code; on bedrock-mantle the Responses API adds background jobs and server-side tools.

      36. What is Bedrock Mantle?

      AWS's distributed inference engine with its own endpoint exposing OpenAI-compatible Chat Completions and Responses plus Anthropic Messages APIs; used for many open-weight models; OpenAI SDK works by changing base URL and key.

      37. Name the AgentCore components.

      Runtime, harness, Gateway, Policy, Memory, Identity, Code Interpreter, Browser, Observability, Evaluations (and Agent Registry in preview).

      38. Bedrock Knowledge Bases vs custom RAG?

      KB: fast, managed connectors, chunking, storage, sync, citations, agentic retrieval in the managed option. Custom: full control over parsing, ACL logic, ranking and lakehouse joins. Start managed, customise hot paths.

      Evals and operations

      39. How do you build an eval dataset?

      Real user questions by category, labelled with expected facts, sources, tools and rubrics, including refusal and injection cases; versioned; grows with every production failure.

      40. How do you trust an LLM judge?

      Specific rubrics, small scales, structured output, a different model from the system under test, order swapping for pairwise, and measured agreement with human labels before use.

      41. pass@k vs pass^k?

      pass@k: at least one of k attempts succeeds. pass^k: all k succeed; measures reliability. Agents for customers need high pass^k.

      42. What do you trace in production?

      Per request: user, version, total cost, status. Per model call: tokens, latency, stop reason. Per tool call: name, redacted args, result size, errors, approvals. Retrieval: query, IDs, scores. In OpenTelemetry with GenAI conventions.

      43. How do you reduce latency of an agent?

      Stream; smaller models for routing and easy steps; parallel tool calls; fewer turns via better tools; cache prompts; shorter context; precompute retrieval; set timeouts.

      44. How do you roll out a prompt or model change safely?

      Version it, run offline evals vs baseline, shadow or canary on a slice of traffic with online evals, monitor cost and latency, keep instant rollback (prompt/version alias).

      45. What's the biggest mistake teams make with GenAI apps?

      No evals. Without a measured dataset, every change is a guess, demos look great and production quietly fails. Close second: giving agents broad permissions and relying on the prompt for safety.

      Interview prep · 30 more questions

      Question bank 2: LLM fundamentals and RAG

      Thirty more questions with answers you can say aloud in about a minute. Cover the answer, say yours, then compare. If your answer misses the main point twice, reread the linked chapter.

      LLM fundamentals

      Interview: What is a large language model, in one minute?

      "It's a neural network, almost always a decoder-only transformer, trained on a huge amount of text to predict the next token. Text is split into tokens, each token becomes a vector, layers of self-attention and feed-forward networks mix information across the sequence, and the last layer outputs a probability for every possible next token. Generation is that prediction repeated, feeding each chosen token back in. Pre-training gives it language and world knowledge; instruction tuning and feedback-based training make it follow instructions. It has no memory between calls and no access to facts after its training cutoff unless I put them in the prompt or give it tools."

      Interview: Why do LLMs hallucinate, and how do you reduce it?

      "The model is trained to produce plausible text, not to know when it doesn't know. When the prompt lacks the facts, it still produces the most likely continuation, which can be fluent and wrong. I reduce it by grounding: retrieve the facts and instruct the model to answer only from them with citations, and allow an explicit 'I don't know'. I use tools for anything computable or live, like numbers from a database, keep temperature low for factual work, use structured output for constrained fields, and add a groundedness check on the answer. Then I measure faithfulness on an eval set, because you can only reduce what you measure. You can't get it to zero, so high-risk actions need verification or human approval."

      Interview: What's the difference between zero-shot, few-shot and chain-of-thought prompting?

      "Zero-shot gives only the instruction. Few-shot adds a handful of input-output examples so the model copies the pattern, which is the most reliable way to fix format and edge-case behaviour. Chain-of-thought asks the model to reason step by step before answering, which helps multi-step problems. With current reasoning models, that thinking is built in and controlled by an effort or budget setting, so I don't need to write 'think step by step'; I give clear goals and let the model plan. I'd still use few-shot examples when I need a precise output style."

      Interview: What does the system prompt do, and what makes a good one?

      "It sets the model's role, rules and context for the whole conversation and is given more weight than user text. A good one states who the model is working for and the goal, the constraints that matter and why, the tools and when to use them, the output format, and what to do when unsure. I write it like a brief for a capable new colleague: specific, with reasons, with examples for tricky cases, and organised in sections. I avoid long lists of shouted rules, because models follow explained instructions better than emphatic ones, and I keep volatile content out of it so it can be cached."

      Interview: What are embeddings?

      "An embedding is a vector, typically a few hundred to a few thousand numbers, that represents the meaning of a piece of text, so that similar meanings are close together. They come from encoder-style models trained with contrastive learning: pull matching pairs together, push non-matching apart. I use them for semantic search, clustering, deduplication, classification and as the retrieval step in RAG. Important practical points: query and documents must use the same model, similarity is usually cosine, dimension trades quality against storage, and embeddings capture topic better than exact identifiers, which is why I pair them with keyword search."

      Interview: Temperature 0 gave me two different answers. Why?

      "Temperature zero means always pick the highest-probability token, but the probabilities themselves can differ slightly between runs.
